3-Hour Exam Time Budget (20 Questions / 100 Marks Strategy)

Part A (10 Short Answers × 3 Marks = 30M): Allocate 5 mins per answer (50 mins total) — target ½ to 1 page highlighting key formula and core definition.
Part B (10 Essay Answers × 7 Marks = 70M): Allocate 12 mins per answer (120 mins total) — target 1.5 to 2 pages with schematic circuit/block diagrams and step-by-step derivations.
Strategy & Review Buffer (10 mins): 5 mins at start for question choice planning + 5 mins at end for question numbering and equation verification.

Course Code: EST100 • 5 Modules • 26 Questions

Engineering Mechanics

Statics and dynamics of engineering structures: Coplanar force systems, Lami's theorem, moments and couples, Varignon's theorem, equilibrium conditions, friction and belt friction, centroids and second moment of area (moment of inertia), kinematics and kinetics of particles, D'Alembert's principle, work-energy, and impulse-momentum relations.

Module 1

Statics of Particles, Coplanar Forces and Rigid Body Equilibrium

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation & Theory ID: MECH-Q1

State the Parallelogram Law of Forces and the Principle of Transmissibility of Forces. Explain the analytical method of resolving a system of coplanar concurrent forces to find the resultant and state the conditions of static equilibrium. State the Parallelogram Law of Forces and the Principle of Transmissibility of Forces. Explain the analytical method of resolving a system of coplanar concurrent forces to find the resultant and state the conditions of static equilibrium. State the Parallelogram Law of Forces and the Principle of Transmissibility of Forces. Explain the analytical method of resolving a system of coplanar concurrent forces to find the resultant and state the conditions of static equilibrium.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$R = \sqrt{(\Sigma F_x)^2 + (\Sigma F_y)^2}, \quad \tan\theta_R = \left|\frac{\Sigma F_y}{\Sigma F_x}\right|, \quad \Sigma F_x = 0, \; \Sigma F_y = 0$$

Parallelogram Law: $R = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2 + 2PQ\cos\theta}$, $\tan\alpha = \frac{Q\sin\theta}{P + Q\cos\theta}$.
Transmissibility Principle: Point of application can slide along line of action on a rigid body without altering external equilibrium.
Cartesian Resolution: $R = \sqrt{(\Sigma F_x)^2 + (\Sigma F_y)^2}$.
Equilibrium: Resultant must vanish ($\Sigma F_x = 0$, $\Sigma F_y = 0$).

$$R = \sqrt{(\Sigma F_x)^2 + (\Sigma F_y)^2}, \quad \tan\theta_R = \left|\frac{\Sigma F_y}{\Sigma F_x}\right|, \quad \Sigma F_x = 0, \; \Sigma F_y = 0$$

Parallelogram Law: $R = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2 + 2PQ\cos\theta}$, $\tan\alpha = \frac{Q\sin\theta}{P + Q\cos\theta}$.
Transmissibility Principle: Point of application can slide along line of action on a rigid body without altering external equilibrium.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Engineering mechanics is grounded in Newtonian principles governing the action of forces upon material bodies at rest or in motion. When multiple forces act simultaneously on a particle or rigid body, the combined physical effect of these forces can be represented by a single equivalent force called the resultant.

Fundamental Principles of Mechanics: 1. Parallelogram Law of Forces: Discovered experimentally by Stevinus in 1586, this axiom states that if two forces acting concurrently on a particle at a point are represented in magnitude and direction by the two adjacent sides of a parallelogram drawn from that point, their resultant is represented in magnitude and direction by the diagonal of the parallelogram passing through that same point. Let two concurrent forces $\mathbf{P}$ and $\mathbf{Q}$ act at an angle $\theta$. By completing the parallelogram $OACB$ where $\vec{OA} = \mathbf{P}$ and $\vec{OB} = \mathbf{Q}$, the diagonal $\vec{OC} = \mathbf{R}$. Dropping a perpendicular $CD$ from vertex $C$ to the extension of line $OA$: In the right-angled triangle $\Delta ODC$: $$OC^2 = OD^2 + CD^2 = (OA + AD)^2 + CD^2$$ From $\Delta ADC$, $AD = Q \cos\theta$ and $CD = Q \sin\theta$. Substituting these: $$R^2 = (P + Q \cos\theta)^2 + (Q \sin\theta)^2 = P^2 + 2PQ \cos\theta + Q^2 \cos^2\theta + Q^2 \sin^2\theta$$ Since $\cos^2\theta + \sin^2\theta = 1$: $$R = \sqrt{P^2 + Q^2 + 2PQ \cos\theta}$$ The direction angle $\alpha$ of resultant $\mathbf{R}$ with respect to force $\mathbf{P}$ is: $$\tan\alpha = \frac{CD}{OD} = \frac{Q \sin\theta}{P + Q \cos\theta} \implies \alpha = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{Q \sin\theta}{P + Q \cos\theta}\right)$$

2. Principle of Transmissibility of Forces: Stated by French mathematician Pierre Varignon, this principle asserts that the internal conditions of motion or external equilibrium of a rigid body remain unchanged if a force acting at a given point on the body is transmitted to act at any other point along its line of action, provided its magnitude, direction, and sense remain unaltered. This principle allows forces acting on rigid bodies to be treated as sliding vectors along their lines of action.

Analytical Resolution of a System of Coplanar Concurrent Forces: When a system comprises $n$ concurrent forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \dots, \mathbf{F}_n$ acting at a common point $O$, inclined at angles $\theta_1, \theta_2, \dots, \theta_n$ relative to the positive horizontal $x$-axis, the analytical method of resolution provides a general, systematic approach for determining the resultant: 1. Resolution into Orthogonal Components: Each individual force $\mathbf{F}_i$ is resolved into its horizontal and vertical Cartesian components: $$F_{ix} = F_i \cos\theta_i$$ $$F_{iy} = F_i \sin\theta_i$$ 2. Algebraic Summation along Coordinate Axes: The algebraic sum of the horizontal components of all forces is: $$\Sigma F_x = F_1 \cos\theta_1 + F_2 \cos\theta_2 + \dots + F_n \cos\theta_n = \sum_{i=1}^n F_i \cos\theta_i$$ The algebraic sum of the vertical components of all forces is: $$\Sigma F_y = F_1 \sin\theta_1 + F_2 \sin\theta_2 + \dots + F_n \sin\theta_n = \sum_{i=1}^n F_i \sin\theta_i$$ 3. Resultant Vector Formulation: The resultant force vector $\mathbf{R}$ is the vector sum of these orthogonal components: $$\mathbf{R} = (\Sigma F_x)\hat{\mathbf{i}} + (\Sigma F_y)\hat{\mathbf{j}}$$ 4. Magnitude and Direction of Resultant: The scalar magnitude of the resultant force is: $$R = \sqrt{(\Sigma F_x)^2 + (\Sigma F_y)^2}$$ The inclination angle $\theta_R$ of the resultant with respect to the horizontal $x$-axis is given by: $$\tan\theta_R = \left| \frac{\Sigma F_y}{\Sigma F_x} \right| \implies \theta_R = \tan^{-1}\left( \frac{|\Sigma F_y|}{|\Sigma F_x|} \right)$$ The true physical quadrant of resultant $\mathbf{R}$ is determined by the individual algebraic signs of $\Sigma F_x$ and $\Sigma F_y$.

Analytical Conditions of Static Equilibrium: A particle subjected to a system of coplanar concurrent forces is in static equilibrium if and only if the resultant force acting on it is identically zero ($\mathbf{R} = 0$). This vector condition translates into two independent scalar algebraic equations: $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \Sigma F_y = 0$$

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation & Application ID: MECH-Q2

State and prove Lami's Theorem using the sine rule on a force triangle. Apply Lami's theorem to find the cord tension and wall reaction for a smooth sphere of weight W suspended against a vertical wall. State and prove Lami's Theorem using the sine rule on a force triangle. Apply Lami's theorem to find the cord tension and wall reaction for a smooth sphere of weight W suspended against a vertical wall. State and prove Lami's Theorem using the sine rule on a force triangle. Apply Lami's theorem to find the cord tension and wall reaction for a smooth sphere of weight W suspended against a vertical wall.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}, \quad T = W \frac{l + r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}, \quad R_w = W \frac{r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$

Lami's Theorem: For 3 concurrent coplanar forces in equilibrium, each force is proportional to the sine of the angle between the other two.
Closed Triangle: Proved using the Sine Rule on the closed force triangle ($\Delta ABC$).
Suspended Sphere: Normal reaction $R_w = W\tan\theta$, Tension $T = W/\cos\theta$ where $\sin\theta = \frac{r}{l+r}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Lami's Theorem is a classical kinematic equation derived by French mathematician Bernard Lamy in 1687. It provides a simple, direct trigonometric method for analyzing the static equilibrium of a particle acted upon by exactly three concurrent, coplanar forces.

Statement of Lami's Theorem: If three coplanar, concurrent forces acting at a point are in static equilibrium, each force is directly proportional to the sine of the angle included between the other two forces. Mathematically, let three forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \mathbf{F}_3$ act concurrently at point $O$ in equilibrium. Let $\alpha$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, $\beta$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, and $\gamma$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$. Lami's theorem states: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$

Mathematical Proof of Lami's Theorem: Consider three concurrent coplanar forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \mathbf{F}_3$ in equilibrium: $$\mathbf{F}_1 + \mathbf{F}_2 + \mathbf{F}_3 = 0$$ According to the polygon law of forces, if three concurrent forces are in equilibrium, they can be represented in magnitude and direction by the three sides of a closed triangle taken in order. Let triangle $\Delta ABC$ represent this force equilibrium, where side $\vec{AB} = \mathbf{F}_1$, side $\vec{BC} = \mathbf{F}_2$, and side $\vec{CA} = \mathbf{F}_3$. Now consider the external angles between the vector directions at point $O$: - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\gamma$. In $\Delta ABC$, the interior angle at vertex $B$ between the sides representing $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\angle B = 180^\circ - \gamma$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$ is $\alpha$. The interior angle at vertex $C$ is $\angle C = 180^\circ - \alpha$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_3$ and $\mathbf{F}_1$ is $\beta$. The interior angle at vertex $A$ is $\angle A = 180^\circ - \beta$. Applying the standard Law of Sines (Sine Rule) to triangle $\Delta ABC$: $$\frac{AB}{\sin(\angle C)} = \frac{BC}{\sin(\angle A)} = \frac{CA}{\sin(\angle B)}$$ Substituting the force magnitudes and interior angles: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin(180^\circ - \alpha)} = \frac{F_2}{\sin(180^\circ - \beta)} = \frac{F_3}{\sin(180^\circ - \gamma)}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$ This completes the mathematical proof.

Classic Engineering Application: Equilibrium of a Suspended Sphere: Problem Statement: A uniform smooth sphere of weight $W$ and radius $r$ is suspended from a peg on a smooth vertical wall by a flexible, inextensible cord of length $l$ attached to a point on its surface. Determine the tension $T$ in the cord and the normal reaction $R_w$ exerted by the vertical wall on the sphere.

Analytical Solution: 1. Identifying Forces and Drawing the Free Body Diagram (FBD): The center of the sphere is designated as $C$. The three forces acting concurrently at center $C$ are: - The downward gravitational weight $W$ acting vertically through $C$. - The normal reaction $R_w$ from the smooth wall, which acts horizontally through the point of contact $A$ and passes directly through center $C$ (since the wall is frictionless, reaction is purely normal). - The tension force $T$ exerted by the cord along line $BC$, where $B$ is the suspension peg on the wall. The line of action of the cord must pass through the center of mass $C$ for rotational equilibrium. 2. Geometry of the Cord: In the right-angled triangle formed by peg $B$, wall contact $A$, and sphere center $C$: - The hypotenuse is $BC = l + r$ (cord length plus radius). - The horizontal side is $AC = r$ (sphere radius). Let $\theta$ be the inclination angle of the cord with the vertical wall: $$\sin\theta = \frac{AC}{BC} = \frac{r}{l + r} \implies \theta = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{r}{l + r}\right)$$ $$\cos\theta = \frac{AB}{BC} = \frac{\sqrt{(l + r)^2 - r^2}}{l + r} = \frac{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}{l + r}$$ 3. Determining Angles Between Forces at Center $C$: - The angle between normal reaction $R_w$ (horizontal) and weight $W$ (downward vertical) is $90^\circ$. - The angle between tension $T$ and weight $W$ is $180^\circ - \theta$. - The angle between tension $T$ and wall reaction $R_w$ is $90^\circ + \theta$. 4. Applying Lami's Theorem: $$\frac{T}{\sin(90^\circ)} = \frac{R_w}{\sin(180^\circ - \theta)} = \frac{W}{\sin(90^\circ + \theta)}$$ Since $\sin(90^\circ) = 1$, $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$, and $\sin(90^\circ + \theta) = \cos\theta$: $$\frac{T}{1} = \frac{R_w}{\sin\theta} = \frac{W}{\cos\theta}$$ Solving for the cord tension $T$: $$T = \frac{W}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{l + r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ Solving for the wall reaction $R_w$: $$R_w = W \tan\theta = W \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ These simple closed-form expressions highlight the power of Lami's theorem in statics.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Lami's Theorem is a classical kinematic equation derived by French mathematician Bernard Lamy in 1687. It provides a simple, direct trigonometric method for analyzing the static equilibrium of a particle acted upon by exactly three concurrent, coplanar forces.

Let $\alpha$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, $\beta$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, and $\gamma$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$. Lami's theorem states: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$

Mathematical Proof of Lami's Theorem: Consider three concurrent coplanar forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \mathbf{F}_3$ in equilibrium: $$\mathbf{F}_1 + \mathbf{F}_2 + \mathbf{F}_3 = 0$$ According to the polygon law of forces, if three concurrent forces are in equilibrium, they can be represented in magnitude and direction by the three sides of a closed triangle taken in order. Let triangle $\Delta ABC$ represent this force equilibrium, where side $\vec{AB} = \mathbf{F}_1$, side $\vec{BC} = \mathbf{F}_2$, and side $\vec{CA} = \mathbf{F}_3$. Now consider the external angles between the vector directions at point $O$: - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\gamma$. In $\Delta ABC$, the interior angle at vertex $B$ between the sides representing $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\angle B = 180^\circ - \gamma$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$ is $\alpha$. The interior angle at vertex $C$ is $\angle C = 180^\circ - \alpha$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_3$ and $\mathbf{F}_1$ is $\beta$. The interior angle at vertex $A$ is $\angle A = 180^\circ - \beta$. Applying the standard Law of Sines (Sine Rule) to triangle $\Delta ABC$: $$\frac{AB}{\sin(\angle C)} = \frac{BC}{\sin(\angle A)} = \frac{CA}{\sin(\angle B)}$$ Substituting the force magnitudes and interior angles: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin(180^\circ - \alpha)} = \frac{F_2}{\sin(180^\circ - \beta)} = \frac{F_3}{\sin(180^\circ - \gamma)}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$ This completes the mathematical proof.

Classic Engineering Application: Equilibrium of a Suspended Sphere: Problem Statement: A uniform smooth sphere of weight $W$ and radius $r$ is suspended from a peg on a smooth vertical wall by a flexible, inextensible cord of length $l$ attached to a point on its surface. Determine the tension $T$ in the cord and the normal reaction $R_w$ exerted by the vertical wall on the sphere.

Analytical Solution: 1. Identifying Forces and Drawing the Free Body Diagram (FBD): The center of the sphere is designated as $C$. The three forces acting concurrently at center $C$ are: - The downward gravitational weight $W$ acting vertically through $C$. - The normal reaction $R_w$ from the smooth wall, which acts horizontally through the point of contact $A$ and passes directly through center $C$ (since the wall is frictionless, reaction is purely normal). - The tension force $T$ exerted by the cord along line $BC$, where $B$ is the suspension peg on the wall. The line of action of the cord must pass through the center of mass $C$ for rotational equilibrium. 2. Geometry of the Cord: In the right-angled triangle formed by peg $B$, wall contact $A$, and sphere center $C$: - The hypotenuse is $BC = l + r$ (cord length plus radius). - The horizontal side is $AC = r$ (sphere radius). Let $\theta$ be the inclination angle of the cord with the vertical wall: $$\sin\theta = \frac{AC}{BC} = \frac{r}{l + r} \implies \theta = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{r}{l + r}\right)$$ $$\cos\theta = \frac{AB}{BC} = \frac{\sqrt{(l + r)^2 - r^2}}{l + r} = \frac{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}{l + r}$$ 3. Determining Angles Between Forces at Center $C$: - The angle between normal reaction $R_w$ (horizontal) and weight $W$ (downward vertical) is $90^\circ$. - The angle between tension $T$ and weight $W$ is $180^\circ - \theta$. - The angle between tension $T$ and wall reaction $R_w$ is $90^\circ + \theta$. 4. Applying Lami's Theorem: $$\frac{T}{\sin(90^\circ)} = \frac{R_w}{\sin(180^\circ - \theta)} = \frac{W}{\sin(90^\circ + \theta)}$$ Since $\sin(90^\circ) = 1$, $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$, and $\sin(90^\circ + \theta) = \cos\theta$: $$\frac{T}{1} = \frac{R_w}{\sin\theta} = \frac{W}{\cos\theta}$$ Solving for the cord tension $T$: $$T = \frac{W}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{l + r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ Solving for the wall reaction $R_w$: $$R_w = W \tan\theta = W \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ These simple closed-form expressions highlight the power of Lami's theorem in statics.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Lami's Theorem is a classical kinematic equation derived by French mathematician Bernard Lamy in 1687. It provides a simple, direct trigonometric method for analyzing the static equilibrium of a particle acted upon by exactly three concurrent, coplanar forces.

Statement of Lami's Theorem: If three coplanar, concurrent forces acting at a point are in static equilibrium, each force is directly proportional to the sine of the angle included between the other two forces. Mathematically, let three forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \mathbf{F}_3$ act concurrently at point $O$ in equilibrium. Let $\alpha$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, $\beta$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$, and $\gamma$ be the angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$. Lami's theorem states: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$

Mathematical Proof of Lami's Theorem: Consider three concurrent coplanar forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \mathbf{F}_3$ in equilibrium: $$\mathbf{F}_1 + \mathbf{F}_2 + \mathbf{F}_3 = 0$$ According to the polygon law of forces, if three concurrent forces are in equilibrium, they can be represented in magnitude and direction by the three sides of a closed triangle taken in order. Let triangle $\Delta ABC$ represent this force equilibrium, where side $\vec{AB} = \mathbf{F}_1$, side $\vec{BC} = \mathbf{F}_2$, and side $\vec{CA} = \mathbf{F}_3$. Now consider the external angles between the vector directions at point $O$: - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\gamma$. In $\Delta ABC$, the interior angle at vertex $B$ between the sides representing $\mathbf{F}_1$ and $\mathbf{F}_2$ is $\angle B = 180^\circ - \gamma$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_2$ and $\mathbf{F}_3$ is $\alpha$. The interior angle at vertex $C$ is $\angle C = 180^\circ - \alpha$. - The external angle between $\mathbf{F}_3$ and $\mathbf{F}_1$ is $\beta$. The interior angle at vertex $A$ is $\angle A = 180^\circ - \beta$. Applying the standard Law of Sines (Sine Rule) to triangle $\Delta ABC$: $$\frac{AB}{\sin(\angle C)} = \frac{BC}{\sin(\angle A)} = \frac{CA}{\sin(\angle B)}$$ Substituting the force magnitudes and interior angles: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin(180^\circ - \alpha)} = \frac{F_2}{\sin(180^\circ - \beta)} = \frac{F_3}{\sin(180^\circ - \gamma)}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$: $$\frac{F_1}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{F_2}{\sin\beta} = \frac{F_3}{\sin\gamma}$$ This completes the mathematical proof.

Classic Engineering Application: Equilibrium of a Suspended Sphere: Problem Statement: A uniform smooth sphere of weight $W$ and radius $r$ is suspended from a peg on a smooth vertical wall by a flexible, inextensible cord of length $l$ attached to a point on its surface. Determine the tension $T$ in the cord and the normal reaction $R_w$ exerted by the vertical wall on the sphere.

Analytical Solution: 1. Identifying Forces and Drawing the Free Body Diagram (FBD): The center of the sphere is designated as $C$. The three forces acting concurrently at center $C$ are: - The downward gravitational weight $W$ acting vertically through $C$. - The normal reaction $R_w$ from the smooth wall, which acts horizontally through the point of contact $A$ and passes directly through center $C$ (since the wall is frictionless, reaction is purely normal). - The tension force $T$ exerted by the cord along line $BC$, where $B$ is the suspension peg on the wall. The line of action of the cord must pass through the center of mass $C$ for rotational equilibrium. 2. Geometry of the Cord: In the right-angled triangle formed by peg $B$, wall contact $A$, and sphere center $C$: - The hypotenuse is $BC = l + r$ (cord length plus radius). - The horizontal side is $AC = r$ (sphere radius). Let $\theta$ be the inclination angle of the cord with the vertical wall: $$\sin\theta = \frac{AC}{BC} = \frac{r}{l + r} \implies \theta = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{r}{l + r}\right)$$ $$\cos\theta = \frac{AB}{BC} = \frac{\sqrt{(l + r)^2 - r^2}}{l + r} = \frac{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}{l + r}$$ 3. Determining Angles Between Forces at Center $C$: - The angle between normal reaction $R_w$ (horizontal) and weight $W$ (downward vertical) is $90^\circ$. - The angle between tension $T$ and weight $W$ is $180^\circ - \theta$. - The angle between tension $T$ and wall reaction $R_w$ is $90^\circ + \theta$. 4. Applying Lami's Theorem: $$\frac{T}{\sin(90^\circ)} = \frac{R_w}{\sin(180^\circ - \theta)} = \frac{W}{\sin(90^\circ + \theta)}$$ Since $\sin(90^\circ) = 1$, $\sin(180^\circ - \theta) = \sin\theta$, and $\sin(90^\circ + \theta) = \cos\theta$: $$\frac{T}{1} = \frac{R_w}{\sin\theta} = \frac{W}{\cos\theta}$$ Solving for the cord tension $T$: $$T = \frac{W}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{l + r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ Solving for the wall reaction $R_w$: $$R_w = W \tan\theta = W \frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta} = W \frac{r}{\sqrt{l^2 + 2lr}}$$ These simple closed-form expressions highlight the power of Lami's theorem in statics.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A Free Body Diagram (FBD) is a graphical representation used in engineering mechanics to isolate a body or system of interconnected bodies from its surroundings. In an FBD, all external physical contacts, supports, walls, and constraints are replaced by their corresponding reactive forces and moments, along with externally applied loads and body forces (weight). Constructing an accurate FBD is the essential prerequisite for setting up the equations of static and dynamic equilibrium.

Steps for Constructing an FBD: 1. Isolate the Body: Draw a clear schematic outline of the isolated body, detached from all surrounding structures. 2. Apply External and Body Forces: Draw the weight vector $W = mg$ acting vertically downward through the center of gravity, along with any given applied forces. 3. Replace Supports with Reactive Forces: - Smooth frictionless surface: Replace with a normal reaction force $N$ directed perpendicular to the contact tangent into the body. - Flexible cable / cord: Replace with a tensile force $T$ directed away from the body along the cord axis. - Rough surface: Include both normal reaction $N$ and a tangential frictional force $F_f$ opposing relative motion. - Contact between curved surfaces: Normal reaction acts along the common normal passing through the centers of curvature.

Equilibrium of Nested Cylinders in a Rectangular Channel: Problem: Two smooth, identical solid cylinders $A$ and $B$, each having weight $W = 1000\text{ N}$ and radius $R = 250\text{ mm}$, rest inside a horizontal rectangular channel of internal width $L = 800\text{ mm}$. Cylinder $A$ rests on the horizontal bottom floor and touches the left vertical wall. Cylinder $B$ rests on cylinder $A$ and touches the right vertical wall. Determine all contact reactions at the walls, floor, and between the cylinders.

Step-by-Step Analytical Equilibrium Solution: 1. Geometric Configuration and Line of Centers: Let $C_A$ and $C_B$ denote the center points of cylinders $A$ and $B$. The horizontal distance between the left wall and center $C_A$ is $R$. The horizontal distance between the right wall and center $C_B$ is $R$. Therefore, the horizontal distance $\Delta x$ between the centers $C_A$ and $C_B$ is: $$\Delta x = L - R - R = L - 2R = 800 - 2(250) = 800 - 500 = 300\text{ mm}$$ When the two smooth cylinders touch, the distance between their centers is: $$d = C_A C_B = R + R = 2R = 2(250) = 500\text{ mm}$$ Let $\theta$ be the angle that the line of centers $C_A C_B$ makes with the horizontal: $$\cos\theta = \frac{\Delta x}{d} = \frac{300}{500} = 0.6 \implies \theta = \cos^{-1}(0.6) \approx 53.13^\circ$$ $$\sin\theta = \sqrt{1 - \cos^2\theta} = \sqrt{1 - 0.6^2} = 0.8$$

2. Equilibrium of the Upper Cylinder $B$: Upper cylinder $B$ is acted upon by three concurrent forces at center $C_B$: - Downward gravitational weight: $W_B = 1000\text{ N}$ ($\downarrow$). - Horizontal normal reaction from the right vertical wall: $R_{B,wall}$ acting to the left ($\leftarrow$). - Mutual contact normal reaction $R_{AB}$ exerted by cylinder $A$ on $B$, directed upward along the line of centers at angle $\theta = 53.13^\circ$ above the horizontal ($\nearrow$). Applying equations of equilibrium for cylinder $B$: Vertical force balance ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$R_{AB} \sin\theta - W_B = 0 \implies R_{AB} (0.8) = 1000$$ $$R_{AB} = \frac{1000}{0.8} = 1250\text{ N}$$ Horizontal force balance ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): $$-R_{B,wall} + R_{AB} \cos\theta = 0 \implies R_{B,wall} = R_{AB} \cos\theta = 1250 \times 0.6 = 750\text{ N}$$ Thus, the right wall exerts a horizontal reaction of $750\text{ N}$ on cylinder $B$.

3. Equilibrium of the Lower Cylinder $A$: Lower cylinder $A$ is acted upon by four forces: - Downward gravitational weight: $W_A = 1000\text{ N}$ ($\downarrow$). - Downward contact normal reaction from cylinder $B$: By Newton's third law, $R_{BA} = R_{AB} = 1250\text{ N}$, inclined at angle $\theta = 53.13^\circ$ below the horizontal ($\swarrow$). - Horizontal normal reaction from the left vertical wall: $R_{A,wall}$ acting to the right ($\rightarrow$). - Vertical normal reaction from the horizontal floor: $R_{A,floor}$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). Applying equations of equilibrium for cylinder $A$: Horizontal force balance ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): $$R_{A,wall} - R_{BA} \cos\theta = 0 \implies R_{A,wall} = 1250 \times 0.6 = 750\text{ N}$$ Vertical force balance ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$R_{A,floor} - W_A - R_{BA} \sin\theta = 0$$ $$R_{A,floor} = 1000 + 1250 \times (0.8) = 1000 + 1000 = 2000\text{ N}$$

Summary of Contact Reactions: - Normal reaction between the two cylinders: $R_{AB} = 1250\text{ N}$ - Left vertical wall reaction: $R_{A,wall} = 750\text{ N}$ (acting rightward) - Right vertical wall reaction: $R_{B,wall} = 750\text{ N}$ (acting leftward) - Horizontal bottom floor reaction: $R_{A,floor} = 2000\text{ N}$ (acting upward, exactly balancing total weight $W_A + W_B$)

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A Free Body Diagram (FBD) is a graphical representation used in engineering mechanics to isolate a body or system of interconnected bodies from its surroundings. In an FBD, all external physical contacts, supports, walls, and constraints are replaced by their corresponding reactive forces and moments, along with externally applied loads and body forces (weight). Constructing an accurate FBD is the essential prerequisite for setting up the equations of static and dynamic equilibrium.

Steps for Constructing an FBD: 1. Isolate the Body: Draw a clear schematic outline of the isolated body, detached from all surrounding structures. 2. Apply External and Body Forces: Draw the weight vector $W = mg$ acting vertically downward through the center of gravity, along with any given applied forces. 3. Replace Supports with Reactive Forces: - Smooth frictionless surface: Replace with a normal reaction force $N$ directed perpendicular to the contact tangent into the body. - Flexible cable / cord: Replace with a tensile force $T$ directed away from the body along the cord axis. - Rough surface: Include both normal reaction $N$ and a tangential frictional force $F_f$ opposing relative motion. - Contact between curved surfaces: Normal reaction acts along the common normal passing through the centers of curvature.

Equilibrium of Nested Cylinders in a Rectangular Channel: Problem: Two smooth, identical solid cylinders $A$ and $B$, each having weight $W = 1000\text{ N}$ and radius $R = 250\text{ mm}$, rest inside a horizontal rectangular channel of internal width $L = 800\text{ mm}$. Cylinder $A$ rests on the horizontal bottom floor and touches the left vertical wall. Cylinder $B$ rests on cylinder $A$ and touches the right vertical wall. Determine all contact reactions at the walls, floor, and between the cylinders.

Step-by-Step Analytical Equilibrium Solution: 1. Geometric Configuration and Line of Centers: Let $C_A$ and $C_B$ denote the center points of cylinders $A$ and $B$. The horizontal distance between the left wall and center $C_A$ is $R$. The horizontal distance between the right wall and center $C_B$ is $R$. Therefore, the horizontal distance $\Delta x$ between the centers $C_A$ and $C_B$ is: $$\Delta x = L - R - R = L - 2R = 800 - 2(250) = 800 - 500 = 300\text{ mm}$$ When the two smooth cylinders touch, the distance between their centers is: $$d = C_A C_B = R + R = 2R = 2(250) = 500\text{ mm}$$ Let $\theta$ be the angle that the line of centers $C_A C_B$ makes with the horizontal: $$\cos\theta = \frac{\Delta x}{d} = \frac{300}{500} = 0.6 \implies \theta = \cos^{-1}(0.6) \approx 53.13^\circ$$ $$\sin\theta = \sqrt{1 - \cos^2\theta} = \sqrt{1 - 0.6^2} = 0.8$$

2. Equilibrium of the Upper Cylinder $B$: Upper cylinder $B$ is acted upon by three concurrent forces at center $C_B$: - Downward gravitational weight: $W_B = 1000\text{ N}$ ($\downarrow$). - Horizontal normal reaction from the right vertical wall: $R_{B,wall}$ acting to the left ($\leftarrow$). - Mutual contact normal reaction $R_{AB}$ exerted by cylinder $A$ on $B$, directed upward along the line of centers at angle $\theta = 53.13^\circ$ above the horizontal ($\nearrow$). Applying equations of equilibrium for cylinder $B$: Vertical force balance ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$R_{AB} \sin\theta - W_B = 0 \implies R_{AB} (0.8) = 1000$$ $$R_{AB} = \frac{1000}{0.8} = 1250\text{ N}$$ Horizontal force balance ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): $$-R_{B,wall} + R_{AB} \cos\theta = 0 \implies R_{B,wall} = R_{AB} \cos\theta = 1250 \times 0.6 = 750\text{ N}$$ Thus, the right wall exerts a horizontal reaction of $750\text{ N}$ on cylinder $B$.

3. Equilibrium of the Lower Cylinder $A$: Lower cylinder $A$ is acted upon by four forces: - Downward gravitational weight: $W_A = 1000\text{ N}$ ($\downarrow$). - Downward contact normal reaction from cylinder $B$: By Newton's third law, $R_{BA} = R_{AB} = 1250\text{ N}$, inclined at angle $\theta = 53.13^\circ$ below the horizontal ($\swarrow$). - Horizontal normal reaction from the left vertical wall: $R_{A,wall}$ acting to the right ($\rightarrow$). - Vertical normal reaction from the horizontal floor: $R_{A,floor}$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). Applying equations of equilibrium for cylinder $A$: Horizontal force balance ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): $$R_{A,wall} - R_{BA} \cos\theta = 0 \implies R_{A,wall} = 1250 \times 0.6 = 750\text{ N}$$ Vertical force balance ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$R_{A,floor} - W_A - R_{BA} \sin\theta = 0$$ $$R_{A,floor} = 1000 + 1250 \times (0.8) = 1000 + 1000 = 2000\text{ N}$$

Summary of Contact Reactions: - Normal reaction between the two cylinders: $R_{AB} = 1250\text{ N}$ - Left vertical wall reaction: $R_{A,wall} = 750\text{ N}$ (acting rightward) - Right vertical wall reaction: $R_{B,wall} = 750\text{ N}$ (acting leftward) - Horizontal bottom floor reaction: $R_{A,floor} = 2000\text{ N}$ (acting upward, exactly balancing total weight $W_A + W_B$)

Define a couple, list its characteristics, and explain how a force can be replaced by an equivalent force-couple system.

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma \mathbf{M}_O = \mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{R} \implies \sum_{i=1}^n F_i d_i = R \cdot d_R, \quad \mathbf{M}_{\text{couple}} = \mathbf{r}_{BA} \times \mathbf{F}$$

Varignon's Theorem of Moments: The algebraic sum of moments of concurrent forces about any point equals the moment of their resultant about that point ($\Sigma M_O = M_{R, O}$).
Couple Characteristics: Net force is zero; moment $M = F \cdot d$ is a free vector, identical about all points.
Force Shift: Shifting force $F$ by perpendicular distance $d$ requires adding a couple $M = F \cdot d$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The rotational tendency of a force about a specified pivot point or axis is quantified by the Moment of a Force. In vector mechanics, the moment of a force vector $\mathbf{F}$ acting at a point defined by position vector $\mathbf{r}$ relative to a reference origin $O$ is defined as the cross product: $$\mathbf{M}_O = \mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{F}$$ The magnitude of the moment is given by $M_O = r F \sin\theta = F \cdot d$, where $d = r \sin\theta$ represents the perpendicular distance (moment arm) from the moment center $O$ to the line of action of force $\mathbf{F}$.

Varignon's Theorem of Moments (Principle of Moments): Stated by French mathematician Pierre Varignon in 1687, this theorem is one of the most widely used principles in engineering statics. Statement: The algebraic sum of the moments of any number of coplanar concurrent forces about any arbitrary point in their plane is equal to the moment of their resultant force about the same point. Mathematically, if forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \dots, \mathbf{F}_n$ act concurrently at point $A$ defined by position vector $\mathbf{r}$ relative to origin $O$, and their resultant is $\mathbf{R} = \sum \mathbf{F}_i$, then: $$\Sigma \mathbf{M}_O = \mathbf{M}_R \implies \sum_{i=1}^n (\mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{F}_i) = \mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{R}$$

Vector Proof of Varignon's Theorem: Let concurrent forces $\mathbf{F}_1, \mathbf{F}_2, \dots, \mathbf{F}_n$ act at point $A$. The position vector of point $A$ with respect to moment pivot $O$ is $\mathbf{r}$. By definition of resultant force: $$\mathbf{R} = \mathbf{F}_1 + \mathbf{F}_2 + \dots + \mathbf{F}_n = \sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{F}_i$$ The total moment of all individual forces about point $O$ is: $$\Sigma \mathbf{M}_O = \mathbf{M}_1 + \mathbf{M}_2 + \dots + \mathbf{M}_n = (\mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{F}_1) + (\mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{F}_2) + \dots + (\mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{F}_n)$$ Using the distributive property of the vector cross product over vector addition: $$\Sigma \mathbf{M}_O = \mathbf{r} \times (\mathbf{F}_1 + \mathbf{F}_2 + \dots + \mathbf{F}_n) = \mathbf{r} \times \mathbf{R} = \mathbf{M}_{R, O}$$ This completes the vector proof. In scalar terms for coplanar forces: $$\sum_{i=1}^n F_i d_i = R \cdot d_R$$ where $d_R$ is the perpendicular distance from $O$ to the resultant's line of action.

Theory of Couples in Mechanics: A couple is defined as a system formed by two parallel forces having equal magnitudes, opposite directions, and distinct non-collinear parallel lines of action separated by a perpendicular distance $d$. 1. Characteristics of a Couple: - Zero Net Force: The resultant translational force of a couple is identically zero ($\mathbf{R} = \mathbf{F} + (-\mathbf{F}) = 0$). A couple produces pure rotation without linear translation. - Invariance of Moment (Free Vector): Unlike the moment of a single force (which depends on the choice of pivot point $O$), the moment of a couple is identical about every point in space. Proof: Let forces $-\mathbf{F}$ and $+\mathbf{F}$ act at points $A$ and $B$, having position vectors $\mathbf{r}_A$ and $\mathbf{r}_B$ relative to an arbitrary origin $O$. The total moment about $O$ is: $$\mathbf{M} = (\mathbf{r}_A \times -\mathbf{F}) + (\mathbf{r}_B \times \mathbf{F}) = (\mathbf{r}_B - \mathbf{r}_A) \times \mathbf{F} = \mathbf{r}_{BA} \times \mathbf{F}$$ where $\mathbf{r}_{BA} = \mathbf{r}_B - \mathbf{r}_A$ is the relative displacement vector pointing from $A$ to $B$. The scalar magnitude is: $$M = F \cdot r_{BA} \sin\theta = F \cdot d$$ Since $\mathbf{M}$ is independent of position vectors $\mathbf{r}_A$ and $\mathbf{r}_B$, a couple is a free vector and can be translated anywhere within its plane without altering its external rotational effect on a rigid body.

Resolution of a Force into a Force and a Couple at Another Point: In structural analysis, it is frequently necessary to shift the line of action of a force $\mathbf{F}$ from point $A$ to another point $B$ separated by perpendicular distance $d$. 1. At point $B$, introduce two equal, opposite, and collinear forces $+\mathbf{F}$ and $-\mathbf{F}$ parallel to the original force. Because their net sum is zero, the mechanical state of the rigid body is unaffected. 2. The original force $+\mathbf{F}$ at $A$ and the opposing force $-\mathbf{F}$ at $B$ form a couple of moment $M = F \cdot d$. 3. The remaining force $+\mathbf{F}$ at $B$ provides the original linear translational action. Therefore, any force $\mathbf{F}$ acting on a rigid body can be replaced at an arbitrary point $B$ by an identical parallel force $\mathbf{F}$ combined with a couple of moment $M = F \cdot d$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma F_x = 0, \quad \Sigma F_y = 0, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0, \quad R = \sqrt{R_x^2 + R_y^2}$$

2D Rigid Body Equilibrium: Requires 3 independent equations ($\Sigma F_x = 0, \Sigma F_y = 0, \Sigma M = 0$).
Support Reactions:
- Roller: 1 normal reaction ($R_N$).
- Pin/Hinged: 2 orthogonal reactions ($R_x, R_y$).
- Fixed: 3 reactions ($R_x, R_y, M_{\text{fix}}$).
UDL Conversion: Replaced by equivalent point load $W = w \cdot L$ at segment centroid.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A rigid body is defined as an idealization of a solid body in which the deformations between constituent particles are negligibly small compared to the overall dimensions. Unlike a particle (which can be treated as a point mass without rotational degrees of freedom), a rigid body can undergo both linear translation and rotation. Consequently, for a rigid body to remain in static equilibrium in a two-dimensional plane, both the resultant force and the resultant moment about any point must vanish simultaneously.

General Equations of Equilibrium in Two Dimensions: For any coplanar force system acting on a rigid body, the vector equilibrium equations $\mathbf{R} = 0$ and $\mathbf{M}_O = 0$ yield three independent scalar equilibrium equations: $$\Sigma F_x = 0$$ $$\Sigma F_y = 0$$ $$\Sigma M_O = 0 \quad (\text{about any arbitrary reference pivot } O)$$

Classification of Structural Supports and Reactive Forces in 2D: 1. Roller Support: Allows free translation along the supporting plane and free rotation about the pin axis. It resists motion strictly perpendicular to the supporting surface, providing exactly one unknown reactive force $R_N$ oriented normal to the plane. 2. Hinged / Pin Support: Restrains both horizontal and vertical translation, but permits free rotation about the pin axis. It exerts an unknown reaction at an unknown angle, which is resolved into two orthogonal unknown reaction components: horizontal force $R_x$ and vertical force $R_y$. 3. Fixed / Built-in Support: Completely restrains translation in all directions as well as rotation. It develops three unknown reactions: horizontal force $R_x$, vertical force $R_y$, and a reactive resisting couple (fixing moment) $M$.

Worked Problem: Determination of Support Reactions for a Beam: Problem Statement: A horizontal beam $AB$ of span $L = 8\text{ m}$ is simply supported with a pin support at left end $A$ and a roller support at right end $B$. The beam carries the following loads: - A uniformly distributed load (UDL) of $w = 4\text{ kN/m}$ over the left half of the beam from $x = 0$ to $x = 4\text{ m}$. - A concentrated point load of $P = 20\text{ kN}$ applied at distance $x = 6\text{ m}$ from $A$, inclined at $60^\circ$ to the horizontal towards the left. - A concentrated clockwise couple $M_0 = 16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ applied at the center of the beam ($x = 4\text{ m}$). Determine the support reactions at ends $A$ and $B$.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Converting Distributed and Inclined Loads to Equivalent Components: - The UDL extends over length $L_{udl} = 4\text{ m}$. Its equivalent total concentrated load is: $$W_{udl} = w \times L_{udl} = 4\text{ kN/m} \times 4\text{ m} = 16\text{ kN}$$ This equivalent load acts at the centroid of the distributed rectangle, which is at distance $\bar{x} = \frac{4}{2} = 2\text{ m}$ from support $A$. - The inclined load $P = 20\text{ kN}$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$ is resolved into Cartesian components: Horizontal component (directed leftward): $$P_x = P \cos(60^\circ) = 20 \times 0.5 = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\leftarrow)$$ Vertical component (directed downward): $$P_y = P \sin(60^\circ) = 20 \times \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 17.32\text{ kN} \quad (\downarrow)$$

2. Setting Up Support Reactions on the Free Body Diagram: - At pin support $A$: Two unknown reactive forces: horizontal reaction $R_{Ax}$ (assumed rightward $\rightarrow$) and vertical reaction $R_{Ay}$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$). - At roller support $B$: One unknown vertical reactive force $R_B$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$).

3. Applying the Equations of Static Equilibrium: First, Horizontal Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): Taking rightward forces as positive: $$R_{Ax} - P_x = 0 \implies R_{Ax} - 10 = 0 \implies R_{Ax} = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\rightarrow)$$

Second, Rotational Moment Equilibrium about Support $A$ ($\Sigma M_A = 0$): Taking counter-clockwise moments as positive ($+ \circlearrowleft$): - Moment of $W_{udl}$ at $x = 2\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(16\text{ kN} \times 2\text{ m}) = -32\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Applied concentrated couple at $x = 4\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of vertical component $P_y$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(17.32\text{ kN} \times 6\text{ m}) = -103.92\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of roller reaction $R_B$ at $x = 8\text{ m}$: Counter-clockwise $\implies +(R_B \times 8\text{ m})$ Summing all moments about pivot $A$: $$\Sigma M_A = -32 - 16 - 103.92 + 8 R_B = 0$$ $$-151.92 + 8 R_B = 0 \implies 8 R_B = 151.92 \implies R_B = \frac{151.92}{8} \approx 18.99\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Third, Vertical Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): Taking upward forces as positive: $$R_{Ay} + R_B - W_{udl} - P_y = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 16 - 17.32 = 0 \implies R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 33.32 = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} = 33.32 - 18.99 = 14.33\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Total Resultant Reaction at Pin Support $A$: $$R_A = \sqrt{R_{Ax}^2 + R_{Ay}^2} = \sqrt{10^2 + 14.33^2} = \sqrt{100 + 205.35} = \sqrt{305.35} \approx 17.47\text{ kN}$$ Inclination angle with the horizontal: $$\theta_A = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{R_{Ay}}{R_{Ax}}\right) = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{14.33}{10}\right) \approx 55.09^\circ$$ Final Support Reactions: - Pin Support $A$: $R_{Ax} = 10.0\text{ kN} \rightarrow$, $R_{Ay} = 14.33\text{ kN} \uparrow$ (Resultant $R_A = 17.47\text{ kN}$ at $55.09^\circ$). - Roller Support $B$: $R_B = 18.99\text{ kN} \uparrow$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The beam carries the following loads: - A uniformly distributed load (UDL) of $w = 4\text{ kN/m}$ over the left half of the beam from $x = 0$ to $x = 4\text{ m}$. - A concentrated point load of $P = 20\text{ kN}$ applied at distance $x = 6\text{ m}$ from $A$, inclined at $60^\circ$ to the horizontal towards the left. - A concentrated clockwise couple $M_0 = 16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ applied at the center of the beam ($x = 4\text{ m}$). Determine the support reactions at ends $A$ and $B$.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Converting Distributed and Inclined Loads to Equivalent Components: - The UDL extends over length $L_{udl} = 4\text{ m}$. Its equivalent total concentrated load is: $$W_{udl} = w \times L_{udl} = 4\text{ kN/m} \times 4\text{ m} = 16\text{ kN}$$ This equivalent load acts at the centroid of the distributed rectangle, which is at distance $\bar{x} = \frac{4}{2} = 2\text{ m}$ from support $A$. - The inclined load $P = 20\text{ kN}$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$ is resolved into Cartesian components: Horizontal component (directed leftward): $$P_x = P \cos(60^\circ) = 20 \times 0.5 = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\leftarrow)$$ Vertical component (directed downward): $$P_y = P \sin(60^\circ) = 20 \times \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 17.32\text{ kN} \quad (\downarrow)$$

2. Setting Up Support Reactions on the Free Body Diagram: - At pin support $A$: Two unknown reactive forces: horizontal reaction $R_{Ax}$ (assumed rightward $\rightarrow$) and vertical reaction $R_{Ay}$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$). - At roller support $B$: One unknown vertical reactive force $R_B$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$).

3. Applying the Equations of Static Equilibrium: First, Horizontal Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): Taking rightward forces as positive: $$R_{Ax} - P_x = 0 \implies R_{Ax} - 10 = 0 \implies R_{Ax} = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\rightarrow)$$

Second, Rotational Moment Equilibrium about Support $A$ ($\Sigma M_A = 0$): Taking counter-clockwise moments as positive ($+ \circlearrowleft$): - Moment of $W_{udl}$ at $x = 2\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(16\text{ kN} \times 2\text{ m}) = -32\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Applied concentrated couple at $x = 4\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of vertical component $P_y$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(17.32\text{ kN} \times 6\text{ m}) = -103.92\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of roller reaction $R_B$ at $x = 8\text{ m}$: Counter-clockwise $\implies +(R_B \times 8\text{ m})$ Summing all moments about pivot $A$: $$\Sigma M_A = -32 - 16 - 103.92 + 8 R_B = 0$$ $$-151.92 + 8 R_B = 0 \implies 8 R_B = 151.92 \implies R_B = \frac{151.92}{8} \approx 18.99\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Third, Vertical Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): Taking upward forces as positive: $$R_{Ay} + R_B - W_{udl} - P_y = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 16 - 17.32 = 0 \implies R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 33.32 = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} = 33.32 - 18.99 = 14.33\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Total Resultant Reaction at Pin Support $A$: $$R_A = \sqrt{R_{Ax}^2 + R_{Ay}^2} = \sqrt{10^2 + 14.33^2} = \sqrt{100 + 205.35} = \sqrt{305.35} \approx 17.47\text{ kN}$$ Inclination angle with the horizontal: $$\theta_A = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{R_{Ay}}{R_{Ax}}\right) = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{14.33}{10}\right) \approx 55.09^\circ$$ Final Support Reactions: - Pin Support $A$: $R_{Ax} = 10.0\text{ kN} \rightarrow$, $R_{Ay} = 14.33\text{ kN} \uparrow$ (Resultant $R_A = 17.47\text{ kN}$ at $55.09^\circ$). - Roller Support $B$: $R_B = 18.99\text{ kN} \uparrow$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

A rigid body is defined as an idealization of a solid body in which the deformations between constituent particles are negligibly small compared to the overall dimensions. Unlike a particle (which can be treated as a point mass without rotational degrees of freedom), a rigid body can undergo both linear translation and rotation. Consequently, for a rigid body to remain in static equilibrium in a two-dimensional plane, both the resultant force and the resultant moment about any point must vanish simultaneously.

General Equations of Equilibrium in Two Dimensions: For any coplanar force system acting on a rigid body, the vector equilibrium equations $\mathbf{R} = 0$ and $\mathbf{M}_O = 0$ yield three independent scalar equilibrium equations: $$\Sigma F_x = 0$$ $$\Sigma F_y = 0$$ $$\Sigma M_O = 0 \quad (\text{about any arbitrary reference pivot } O)$$

Classification of Structural Supports and Reactive Forces in 2D: 1. Roller Support: Allows free translation along the supporting plane and free rotation about the pin axis. It resists motion strictly perpendicular to the supporting surface, providing exactly one unknown reactive force $R_N$ oriented normal to the plane. 2. Hinged / Pin Support: Restrains both horizontal and vertical translation, but permits free rotation about the pin axis. It exerts an unknown reaction at an unknown angle, which is resolved into two orthogonal unknown reaction components: horizontal force $R_x$ and vertical force $R_y$. 3. Fixed / Built-in Support: Completely restrains translation in all directions as well as rotation. It develops three unknown reactions: horizontal force $R_x$, vertical force $R_y$, and a reactive resisting couple (fixing moment) $M$.

Worked Problem: Determination of Support Reactions for a Beam: Problem Statement: A horizontal beam $AB$ of span $L = 8\text{ m}$ is simply supported with a pin support at left end $A$ and a roller support at right end $B$. The beam carries the following loads: - A uniformly distributed load (UDL) of $w = 4\text{ kN/m}$ over the left half of the beam from $x = 0$ to $x = 4\text{ m}$. - A concentrated point load of $P = 20\text{ kN}$ applied at distance $x = 6\text{ m}$ from $A$, inclined at $60^\circ$ to the horizontal towards the left. - A concentrated clockwise couple $M_0 = 16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ applied at the center of the beam ($x = 4\text{ m}$). Determine the support reactions at ends $A$ and $B$.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Converting Distributed and Inclined Loads to Equivalent Components: - The UDL extends over length $L_{udl} = 4\text{ m}$. Its equivalent total concentrated load is: $$W_{udl} = w \times L_{udl} = 4\text{ kN/m} \times 4\text{ m} = 16\text{ kN}$$ This equivalent load acts at the centroid of the distributed rectangle, which is at distance $\bar{x} = \frac{4}{2} = 2\text{ m}$ from support $A$. - The inclined load $P = 20\text{ kN}$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$ is resolved into Cartesian components: Horizontal component (directed leftward): $$P_x = P \cos(60^\circ) = 20 \times 0.5 = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\leftarrow)$$ Vertical component (directed downward): $$P_y = P \sin(60^\circ) = 20 \times \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 17.32\text{ kN} \quad (\downarrow)$$

2. Setting Up Support Reactions on the Free Body Diagram: - At pin support $A$: Two unknown reactive forces: horizontal reaction $R_{Ax}$ (assumed rightward $\rightarrow$) and vertical reaction $R_{Ay}$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$). - At roller support $B$: One unknown vertical reactive force $R_B$ (assumed upward $\uparrow$).

3. Applying the Equations of Static Equilibrium: First, Horizontal Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): Taking rightward forces as positive: $$R_{Ax} - P_x = 0 \implies R_{Ax} - 10 = 0 \implies R_{Ax} = 10\text{ kN} \quad (\rightarrow)$$

Second, Rotational Moment Equilibrium about Support $A$ ($\Sigma M_A = 0$): Taking counter-clockwise moments as positive ($+ \circlearrowleft$): - Moment of $W_{udl}$ at $x = 2\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(16\text{ kN} \times 2\text{ m}) = -32\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Applied concentrated couple at $x = 4\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -16\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of vertical component $P_y$ at $x = 6\text{ m}$: Clockwise $\implies -(17.32\text{ kN} \times 6\text{ m}) = -103.92\text{ kN}\cdot\text{m}$ - Moment of roller reaction $R_B$ at $x = 8\text{ m}$: Counter-clockwise $\implies +(R_B \times 8\text{ m})$ Summing all moments about pivot $A$: $$\Sigma M_A = -32 - 16 - 103.92 + 8 R_B = 0$$ $$-151.92 + 8 R_B = 0 \implies 8 R_B = 151.92 \implies R_B = \frac{151.92}{8} \approx 18.99\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Third, Vertical Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): Taking upward forces as positive: $$R_{Ay} + R_B - W_{udl} - P_y = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 16 - 17.32 = 0 \implies R_{Ay} + 18.99 - 33.32 = 0$$ $$R_{Ay} = 33.32 - 18.99 = 14.33\text{ kN} \quad (\uparrow)$$

Total Resultant Reaction at Pin Support $A$: $$R_A = \sqrt{R_{Ax}^2 + R_{Ay}^2} = \sqrt{10^2 + 14.33^2} = \sqrt{100 + 205.35} = \sqrt{305.35} \approx 17.47\text{ kN}$$ Inclination angle with the horizontal: $$\theta_A = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{R_{Ay}}{R_{Ax}}\right) = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{14.33}{10}\right) \approx 55.09^\circ$$ Final Support Reactions: - Pin Support $A$: $R_{Ax} = 10.0\text{ kN} \rightarrow$, $R_{Ay} = 14.33\text{ kN} \uparrow$ (Resultant $R_A = 17.47\text{ kN}$ at $55.09^\circ$). - Roller Support $B$: $R_B = 18.99\text{ kN} \uparrow$.

Theory & Derivation ID: MECH-Q6

State Coulomb's laws of dry friction. Define the terms: static friction, limiting friction, kinetic friction, angle of friction, angle of repose, and cone of friction. Prove that the angle of repose is equal to the angle of friction. State Coulomb's laws of dry friction. Define the terms: static friction, limiting friction, kinetic friction, angle of friction, angle of repose, and cone of friction. Prove that the angle of repose is equal to the angle of friction. State Coulomb's laws of dry friction. Define the terms: static friction, limiting friction, kinetic friction, angle of friction, angle of repose, and cone of friction. Prove that the angle of repose is equal to the angle of friction.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$F_L = \mu_s N, \quad \tan\phi = \mu_s \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}\mu_s, \quad \theta_{\text{repose}} = \phi = \tan^{-1}\mu_s$$

Coulomb's Friction Laws: Limiting friction $F_L = \mu_s N$ is independent of contact area; kinetic friction $\mu_k < \mu_s$.
Angle of Friction ($\phi$): Angle between total reaction $R$ and normal $N$: $\tan\phi = \mu_s$.
Angle of Repose ($\theta$): Maximum incline angle before sliding down under self-weight: $\tan\theta = \mu_s \implies \theta = \phi$.
Cone of Friction: Inverted cone of semi-vertex angle $\phi$; forces inside cone cannot cause slip.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Friction is the tangential contact resisting force that develops between two solid contacting surfaces when one surface slides or tends to slide across the other. Formulated empirically by Guillaume Amontons and Charles-Augustin de Coulomb, dry friction (Coulomb friction) arises from microscopic surface asperities interlocking and adhesion bonds forming across real contact areas.

Classification of Dry Friction: 1. Static Friction ($F_s$): The resisting tangential force that exists between two contacting bodies when there is an impending tendency to slide, but actual relative motion has not yet initiated. Static friction is a self-adjusting force: as the tangential disturbing force $P$ increases, the static friction force $F_s$ increases proportionally in magnitude and opposes $P$ to maintain static equilibrium ($F_s = P$). 2. Limiting Friction ($F_{max}$ or $F_L$): The maximum possible value that static friction can attain just before relative sliding motion begins. It represents the boundary threshold between rest and motion. 3. Kinetic (Dynamic) Friction ($F_k$): The resisting frictional force operating between surfaces in active relative sliding motion. Once motion initiates, the interlocking asperities are sheared off or ride over one another, causing kinetic friction to drop to a value lower than the limiting static friction ($F_k < F_{max}$).

Coulomb's Laws of Dry Friction: 1. First Law: The limiting friction force $F_L$ is directly proportional to the normal reaction force $N$ pressing the contacting surfaces together: $$F_L = \mu_s N$$ where $\mu_s$ is the dimensionless Coefficient of Static Friction. 2. Second Law: For surfaces in relative motion, the kinetic friction force $F_k$ is directly proportional to the normal reaction force: $$F_k = \mu_k N$$ where $\mu_k$ is the Coefficient of Kinetic Friction, with $\mu_k < \mu_s$. 3. Third Law: The frictional force is independent of the apparent geometric area of contact, depending only on the actual microscopic contact area (which is governed by normal load $N$). 4. Fourth Law: Kinetic friction is largely independent of relative sliding velocity at low to moderate speeds.

Angle of Friction ($\phi$): At limiting equilibrium, the contacting surface exerts two simultaneous forces on a body: the normal reaction $N$ perpendicular to the contact interface, and the limiting friction force $F_L = \mu_s N$ tangential to the interface. The resultant of these two forces is the Total Reaction $\mathbf{R}$: $$R = \sqrt{N^2 + F_L^2} = \sqrt{N^2 + (\mu_s N)^2} = N \sqrt{1 + \mu_s^2}$$ The Angle of Friction $\phi$ is defined as the angle that the total resultant reaction $\mathbf{R}$ makes with the normal reaction $N$ at the instant of limiting equilibrium: $$\tan\phi = \frac{F_L}{N} = \frac{\mu_s N}{N} = \mu_s \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}(\mu_s)$$

Angle of Repose ($\theta$): The Angle of Repose $\theta$ is defined as the maximum angle of inclination of a rough plane with the horizontal at which a body placed upon it will remain at rest in limiting equilibrium under the action of its own weight alone, without sliding downward. Proof that Angle of Repose equals Angle of Friction ($\theta = \phi$): Consider a body of weight $W$ resting on a rough plane inclined at angle $\theta$ to the horizontal. Resolving the weight $W$ along and perpendicular to the inclined plane: - Component perpendicular to the incline: $W \cos\theta$ - Component parallel to the incline acting downward: $W \sin\theta$ Setting up equations of limiting equilibrium: Perpendicular to incline ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$N = W \cos\theta$$ Along the incline ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): $$F_L - W \sin\theta = 0 \implies F_L = W \sin\theta$$ Since the body is at limiting equilibrium, $F_L = \mu_s N$: $$W \sin\theta = \mu_s (W \cos\theta)$$ Dividing both sides by $W \cos\theta$: $$\tan\theta = \mu_s$$ Since we established earlier that $\mu_s = \tan\phi$ (where $\phi$ is the angle of friction): $$\tan\theta = \tan\phi \implies \theta = \phi$$ This proves that the angle of repose is identically equal to the angle of friction.

Cone of Friction: When an external disturbing force tends to move a body in various directions across a horizontal plane, the limiting friction force $F_L$ always acts in the opposite direction. Consequently, the total reaction vector $\mathbf{R}$ tilts by angle $\phi$ away from the normal reaction $N$ in whichever direction motion is impending. If the direction of impending motion rotates through a full circle of $360^\circ$ around the contact point, the total reaction vector $\mathbf{R}$ sweeps out a right circular inverted cone called the Cone of Friction. - The vertex of the cone is located at the point of contact. - The axis of the cone aligns with the normal reaction $N$. - The semi-vertex angle of the cone is the angle of friction $\phi = \tan^{-1}\mu_s$. Significance: If the resultant applied force falls inside the cone of friction, motion is physically impossible regardless of the magnitude of the force (self-locking condition).

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation & Application ID: MECH-Q7

A uniform ladder of length L and weight W rests against a rough vertical wall and a rough horizontal floor. Formulate the equilibrium equations, derive the expression for the minimum inclination angle to prevent slipping, and determine the maximum distance a person can climb. A uniform ladder of length L and weight W rests against a rough vertical wall and a rough horizontal floor. Formulate the equilibrium equations, derive the expression for the minimum inclination angle to prevent slipping, and determine the maximum distance a person can climb. A uniform ladder of length L and weight W rests against a rough vertical wall and a rough horizontal floor. Formulate the equilibrium equations, derive the expression for the minimum inclination angle to prevent slipping, and determine the maximum distance a person can climb.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f}, \quad \text{Smooth Wall: } \tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1}{2\mu_f}, \quad x_{max} = \frac{N_w L(\tan\theta + \mu_w) - \frac{W L}{2}}{W_1}$$

Ladder Equilibrium: Floor friction $F_f = \mu_f N_f \rightarrow$, wall reaction $N_w \leftarrow$, wall friction $F_w = \mu_w N_w \uparrow$.
Force Balance: $N_w = \mu_f N_f$, $N_f + \mu_w N_w = W \implies N_f = \frac{W}{1+\mu_f\mu_w}$.
Minimum Angle: Taking $\Sigma M_A = 0$ yields $\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f\mu_w}{2\mu_f}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f}, \quad \text{Smooth Wall: } \tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1}{2\mu_f}, \quad x_{max} = \frac{N_w L(\tan\theta + \mu_w) - \frac{W L}{2}}{W_1}$$

Ladder Equilibrium: Floor friction $F_f = \mu_f N_f \rightarrow$, wall reaction $N_w \leftarrow$, wall friction $F_w = \mu_w N_w \uparrow$.
Force Balance: $N_w = \mu_f N_f$, $N_f + \mu_w N_w = W \implies N_f = \frac{W}{1+\mu_f\mu_w}$.
Minimum Angle: Taking $\Sigma M_A = 0$ yields $\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f\mu_w}{2\mu_f}$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f}, \quad \text{Smooth Wall: } \tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1}{2\mu_f}, \quad x_{max} = \frac{N_w L(\tan\theta + \mu_w) - \frac{W L}{2}}{W_1}$$

Ladder Equilibrium: Floor friction $F_f = \mu_f N_f \rightarrow$, wall reaction $N_w \leftarrow$, wall friction $F_w = \mu_w N_w \uparrow$.
Force Balance: $N_w = \mu_f N_f$, $N_f + \mu_w N_w = W \implies N_f = \frac{W}{1+\mu_f\mu_w}$.
Minimum Angle: Taking $\Sigma M_A = 0$ yields $\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f\mu_w}{2\mu_f}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A ladder resting against a vertical wall and a horizontal floor is a classic problem in engineering mechanics involving two frictional contacts simultaneously. When the ladder tends to slip, the bottom foot slides horizontally outward away from the wall, while the top end slides vertically downward along the wall.

Formulation of Free Body Diagram and Governing Equations: Consider a uniform ladder $AB$ of length $L$ and weight $W$ resting with its lower end $A$ on a rough horizontal floor (coefficient of friction $\mu_f$) and its upper end $B$ against a rough vertical wall (coefficient of friction $\mu_w$). The ladder is inclined at an angle $\theta$ to the horizontal floor. Let the uniform weight $W$ act downward through the ladder's midpoint $G$ at distance $L/2$ from $A$.

At bottom end $A$ (impending slip is to the left): - Normal floor reaction $N_f$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). - Frictional floor force $F_f = \mu_f N_f$ acting horizontally toward the wall ($\rightarrow$). At top end $B$ (impending slip is downward): - Normal wall reaction $N_w$ acting horizontally outward from the wall ($\leftarrow$). - Frictional wall force $F_w = \mu_w N_w$ acting vertically upward along the wall ($\uparrow$).

Applying Equations of Static Equilibrium: 1. Horizontal Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): Taking rightward forces as positive: $$F_f - N_w = 0 \implies N_w = F_f = \mu_f N_f \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ 2. Vertical Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): Taking upward forces as positive: $$N_f + F_w - W = 0 \implies N_f + \mu_w N_w = W \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Substituting Equation 1 ($N_w = \mu_f N_f$) into Equation 2: $$N_f + \mu_w (\mu_f N_f) = W \implies N_f (1 + \mu_f \mu_w) = W$$ Solving for the normal floor reaction: $$N_f = \frac{W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ Substituting $N_f$ back into Equation 1 yields the normal wall reaction: $$N_w = \frac{\mu_f W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ The wall friction is: $$F_w = \mu_w N_w = \frac{\mu_f \mu_w W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$

Derivation of Minimum Inclination Angle ($\theta_{min}$): To establish the threshold angle of inclination $\theta$ below which the ladder will slip, we take moments of all forces about the base pivot $A$ ($\Sigma M_A = 0$): Taking counter-clockwise moments as positive ($+ \circlearrowleft$): - Moment of ladder weight $W$: Clockwise about $A$ with moment arm $\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta$: $$M_W = -W \left(\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta\right)$$ - Moment of normal wall reaction $N_w$: Counter-clockwise about $A$ with vertical moment arm $L\sin\theta$: $$M_{Nw} = +N_w (L\sin\theta)$$ - Moment of wall friction $F_w$: Counter-clockwise about $A$ with horizontal moment arm $L\cos\theta$: $$M_{Fw} = +F_w (L\cos\theta)$$ Summing moments about base $A$: $$\Sigma M_A = N_w L\sin\theta + F_w L\cos\theta - W \frac{L}{2}\cos\theta = 0$$ Dividing throughout by $L$: $$N_w \sin\theta + F_w \cos\theta = \frac{W}{2}\cos\theta$$ Dividing throughout by $\cos\theta$ (since $\cos\theta \neq 0$): $$N_w \tan\theta + F_w = \frac{W}{2} \implies N_w \tan\theta = \frac{W}{2} - F_w$$ Substituting the derived expressions for $N_w$ and $F_w$: $$\left(\frac{\mu_f W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}\right)\tan\theta = \frac{W}{2} - \frac{\mu_f \mu_w W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w} = W \left[ \frac{1 + \mu_f \mu_w - 2\mu_f \mu_w}{2(1 + \mu_f \mu_w)} \right] = W \left[ \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2(1 + \mu_f \mu_w)} \right]$$ Canceling the common term $\frac{W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$ from both sides: $$\mu_f \tan\theta = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2}$$ Solving for $\tan\theta$: $$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f} \implies \theta_{min} = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f}\right)$$ Special Case (Smooth Vertical Wall, $\mu_w = 0$): $$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - (0)}{2\mu_f} = \frac{1}{2\mu_f}$$

Maximum Distance a Person Can Climb Before Slipping: Consider a person of weight $W_1$ climbing up the ladder at inclination $\theta$. Let $x$ be the distance climbed along the ladder from base $A$. The new vertical equilibrium equation is: $$N_f + \mu_w N_w = W + W_1 \implies N_f = \frac{W + W_1}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ $$N_w = \mu_f N_f = \frac{\mu_f (W + W_1)}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ Taking moments about base $A$ with the person at distance $x$: $$N_w L\sin\theta + \mu_w N_w L\cos\theta - W \left(\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta\right) - W_1 (x\cos\theta) = 0$$ Dividing by $\cos\theta$: $$N_w L\tan\theta + \mu_w N_w L - W \frac{L}{2} - W_1 x = 0$$ Solving for the maximum safe climbing distance $x_{max}$: $$x_{max} = \frac{N_w L (\tan\theta + \mu_w) - \frac{W L}{2}}{W_1}$$ If $x_{max} \ge L$, the person can climb to the top of the ladder safely without slipping.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A ladder resting against a vertical wall and a horizontal floor is a classic problem in engineering mechanics involving two frictional contacts simultaneously. When the ladder tends to slip, the bottom foot slides horizontally outward away from the wall, while the top end slides vertically downward along the wall.

Formulation of Free Body Diagram and Governing Equations: Consider a uniform ladder $AB$ of length $L$ and weight $W$ resting with its lower end $A$ on a rough horizontal floor (coefficient of friction $\mu_f$) and its upper end $B$ against a rough vertical wall (coefficient of friction $\mu_w$). The ladder is inclined at an angle $\theta$ to the horizontal floor. Let the uniform weight $W$ act downward through the ladder's midpoint $G$ at distance $L/2$ from $A$.

At bottom end $A$ (impending slip is to the left): - Normal floor reaction $N_f$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). - Frictional floor force $F_f = \mu_f N_f$ acting horizontally toward the wall ($\rightarrow$). At top end $B$ (impending slip is downward): - Normal wall reaction $N_w$ acting horizontally outward from the wall ($\leftarrow$). - Frictional wall force $F_w = \mu_w N_w$ acting vertically upward along the wall ($\uparrow$).

Applying Equations of Static Equilibrium: 1. Horizontal Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_x = 0$): Taking rightward forces as positive: $$F_f - N_w = 0 \implies N_w = F_f = \mu_f N_f \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ 2. Vertical Force Equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): Taking upward forces as positive: $$N_f + F_w - W = 0 \implies N_f + \mu_w N_w = W \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Substituting Equation 1 ($N_w = \mu_f N_f$) into Equation 2: $$N_f + \mu_w (\mu_f N_f) = W \implies N_f (1 + \mu_f \mu_w) = W$$ Solving for the normal floor reaction: $$N_f = \frac{W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ Substituting $N_f$ back into Equation 1 yields the normal wall reaction: $$N_w = \frac{\mu_f W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ The wall friction is: $$F_w = \mu_w N_w = \frac{\mu_f \mu_w W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$

Derivation of Minimum Inclination Angle ($\theta_{min}$): To establish the threshold angle of inclination $\theta$ below which the ladder will slip, we take moments of all forces about the base pivot $A$ ($\Sigma M_A = 0$): Taking counter-clockwise moments as positive ($+ \circlearrowleft$): - Moment of ladder weight $W$: Clockwise about $A$ with moment arm $\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta$: $$M_W = -W \left(\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta\right)$$ - Moment of normal wall reaction $N_w$: Counter-clockwise about $A$ with vertical moment arm $L\sin\theta$: $$M_{Nw} = +N_w (L\sin\theta)$$ - Moment of wall friction $F_w$: Counter-clockwise about $A$ with horizontal moment arm $L\cos\theta$: $$M_{Fw} = +F_w (L\cos\theta)$$ Summing moments about base $A$: $$\Sigma M_A = N_w L\sin\theta + F_w L\cos\theta - W \frac{L}{2}\cos\theta = 0$$ Dividing throughout by $L$: $$N_w \sin\theta + F_w \cos\theta = \frac{W}{2}\cos\theta$$ Dividing throughout by $\cos\theta$ (since $\cos\theta \neq 0$): $$N_w \tan\theta + F_w = \frac{W}{2} \implies N_w \tan\theta = \frac{W}{2} - F_w$$ Substituting the derived expressions for $N_w$ and $F_w$: $$\left(\frac{\mu_f W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}\right)\tan\theta = \frac{W}{2} - \frac{\mu_f \mu_w W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w} = W \left[ \frac{1 + \mu_f \mu_w - 2\mu_f \mu_w}{2(1 + \mu_f \mu_w)} \right] = W \left[ \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2(1 + \mu_f \mu_w)} \right]$$ Canceling the common term $\frac{W}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$ from both sides: $$\mu_f \tan\theta = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2}$$ Solving for $\tan\theta$: $$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f} \implies \theta_{min} = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{1 - \mu_f \mu_w}{2\mu_f}\right)$$ Special Case (Smooth Vertical Wall, $\mu_w = 0$): $$\tan\theta_{min} = \frac{1 - (0)}{2\mu_f} = \frac{1}{2\mu_f}$$

Maximum Distance a Person Can Climb Before Slipping: Consider a person of weight $W_1$ climbing up the ladder at inclination $\theta$. Let $x$ be the distance climbed along the ladder from base $A$. The new vertical equilibrium equation is: $$N_f + \mu_w N_w = W + W_1 \implies N_f = \frac{W + W_1}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ $$N_w = \mu_f N_f = \frac{\mu_f (W + W_1)}{1 + \mu_f \mu_w}$$ Taking moments about base $A$ with the person at distance $x$: $$N_w L\sin\theta + \mu_w N_w L\cos\theta - W \left(\frac{L}{2}\cos\theta\right) - W_1 (x\cos\theta) = 0$$ Dividing by $\cos\theta$: $$N_w L\tan\theta + \mu_w N_w L - W \frac{L}{2} - W_1 x = 0$$ Solving for the maximum safe climbing distance $x_{max}$: $$x_{max} = \frac{N_w L (\tan\theta + \mu_w) - \frac{W L}{2}}{W_1}$$ If $x_{max} \ge L$, the person can climb to the top of the ladder safely without slipping.

Explain the working principle of a wedge for raising heavy loads. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$P = W \left[ \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)} \right] \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3} \right] \xrightarrow{\text{equal }\phi} P = W \tan(\alpha + 2\phi)$$

Wedge Mechanism: Translates small horizontal effort $P$ into large vertical lifting force $W$.
Block & Wedge Equilibrium: Reaction between block and wedge tilts by $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical.
Minimum Driving Force: $P = W\tan(\alpha + 2\phi)$ for equal friction angle $\phi$.
Self-Locking: Wedge will not slip back when $P=0$ if $\alpha < 2\phi$.
