Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A wedge is an inclined plane machine used in engineering practice to lift heavy machinery loads, make small vertical adjustments, or split materials through the application of a comparatively small transverse effort. Because of high frictional resistance along sliding faces, wedges possess the desirable property of being self-locking, meaning the lifted load does not slide back down when the driving effort is removed.

Problem Description and System Geometry: Consider a heavy block of weight $W$ constrained to move strictly in the vertical direction by smooth or rough vertical guides. The block is lifted by driving a slender wedge of self-weight $w$ (often negligible, $w \approx 0$) and wedge angle $\alpha$ horizontally underneath it by applying an external horizontal driving force $P$. Let the friction coefficients be: - $\mu_1 = \tan\phi_1$ along the vertical guide wall. - $\mu_2 = \tan\phi_2$ along the inclined contact interface between block and wedge. - $\mu_3 = \tan\phi_3$ along the horizontal ground surface supporting the wedge. To analyze this system, we separate the assembly into two distinct free body diagrams: one for the vertical block and one for the horizontal wedge.

1. Equilibrium of the Vertical Block (Lifting Mode): The block moves vertically upward ($\uparrow$). Forces acting on the block: - Downward weight $W$ ($\downarrow$). - Normal reaction $N_1$ from the vertical wall acting horizontally to the right ($\rightarrow$). - Frictional force $F_1 = \mu_1 N_1$ along the vertical wall acting vertically downward ($\downarrow$) opposing upward motion. - Contact normal reaction $N_2$ perpendicular to the inclined face of the wedge, acting at angle $\alpha$ to the vertical. - Frictional force $F_2 = \mu_2 N_2$ along the inclined interface, directed downward and to the right along the slope ($\searrow$) opposing the relative motion of the block sliding up the wedge.

The normal reaction $N_2$ and friction $F_2$ combine into a single Total Reaction $R_2$ inclined at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical, where $\phi_2 = \tan^{-1}\mu_2$. Similarly, $N_1$ and $F_1$ combine into total reaction $R_1$ inclined at angle $\phi_1$ below the horizontal. Applying equations of equilibrium for the block: $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies N_1 - N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \cos\alpha = 0$$ $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies N_2 \cos\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_1 N_1 - W = 0$$ Expressing these in terms of total reactions: Resolving horizontally and vertically: $$R_1 \cos\phi_1 = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) \implies R_1 = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1}$$ Vertical balance: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_1 \sin\phi_1 - W = 0$$ Substituting $R_1$: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} = W$$ $$R_2 \left[ \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_1 - \sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} \right] = W$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\cos(A + B) = \cos A \cos B - \sin A \sin B$: $$R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1} = W \implies R_2 = W \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}$$

2. Equilibrium of the Horizontal Wedge: The wedge moves horizontally to the left ($\leftarrow$) under applied horizontal force $P$. Forces acting on the wedge: - Applied horizontal driving force $P$ acting to the left ($\leftarrow$). - Mutual contact reaction from the block: by Newton's third law, this is $R_2$, directed downward and to the left at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical. - Ground normal reaction $N_3$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). - Ground friction force $F_3 = \mu_3 N_3$ acting to the right ($\rightarrow$) opposing motion. $N_3$ and $F_3$ combine into the total ground reaction $R_3$ inclined at friction angle $\phi_3$ to the vertical.

Setting up force balance for the wedge: Resolving all forces along the horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies R_3 \cos\phi_3 - R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) = 0 \implies R_3 = R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies P - R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_3 \sin\phi_3 = 0$$ $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_3 \sin\phi_3$$ Substituting $R_3$: $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} = R_2 \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_3 + \cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$ Using $\sin(A + B) = \sin A \cos B + \cos A \sin B$: $$P = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ Now substituting the derived expression for $R_2$ from block equilibrium: $$P = W \left[ \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)} \right] \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$

Special Case (Uniform Friction Coefficient $\phi_1 = \phi_2 = \phi_3 = \phi$): $$P = W \frac{\cos\phi \sin(\alpha + 2\phi)}{\cos\phi \cos(\alpha + 2\phi)} = W \tan(\alpha + 2\phi)$$ This simple formula gives the minimum horizontal force required to drive the wedge and lift the load $W$.

Condition for Self-Locking of the Wedge: When the driving force $P$ is completely removed ($P = 0$), the wedge will remain locked in position without being pushed backward by load $W$ provided the friction forces are sufficient. The condition for self-locking is: $$\alpha < \phi_2 + \phi_3 \quad \text{or for equal friction: } \alpha < 2\phi$$ If the wedge angle $\alpha$ is smaller than the sum of friction angles, the wedge is self-locking.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A wedge is an inclined plane machine used in engineering practice to lift heavy machinery loads, make small vertical adjustments, or split materials through the application of a comparatively small transverse effort. Because of high frictional resistance along sliding faces, wedges possess the desirable property of being self-locking, meaning the lifted load does not slide back down when the driving effort is removed.

Problem Description and System Geometry: Consider a heavy block of weight $W$ constrained to move strictly in the vertical direction by smooth or rough vertical guides. The block is lifted by driving a slender wedge of self-weight $w$ (often negligible, $w \approx 0$) and wedge angle $\alpha$ horizontally underneath it by applying an external horizontal driving force $P$. Let the friction coefficients be: - $\mu_1 = \tan\phi_1$ along the vertical guide wall. - $\mu_2 = \tan\phi_2$ along the inclined contact interface between block and wedge. - $\mu_3 = \tan\phi_3$ along the horizontal ground surface supporting the wedge. To analyze this system, we separate the assembly into two distinct free body diagrams: one for the vertical block and one for the horizontal wedge.

1. Equilibrium of the Vertical Block (Lifting Mode): The block moves vertically upward ($\uparrow$). Forces acting on the block: - Downward weight $W$ ($\downarrow$). - Normal reaction $N_1$ from the vertical wall acting horizontally to the right ($\rightarrow$). - Frictional force $F_1 = \mu_1 N_1$ along the vertical wall acting vertically downward ($\downarrow$) opposing upward motion. - Contact normal reaction $N_2$ perpendicular to the inclined face of the wedge, acting at angle $\alpha$ to the vertical. - Frictional force $F_2 = \mu_2 N_2$ along the inclined interface, directed downward and to the right along the slope ($\searrow$) opposing the relative motion of the block sliding up the wedge.

The normal reaction $N_2$ and friction $F_2$ combine into a single Total Reaction $R_2$ inclined at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical, where $\phi_2 = \tan^{-1}\mu_2$. Similarly, $N_1$ and $F_1$ combine into total reaction $R_1$ inclined at angle $\phi_1$ below the horizontal. Applying equations of equilibrium for the block: $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies N_1 - N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \cos\alpha = 0$$ $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies N_2 \cos\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_1 N_1 - W = 0$$ Expressing these in terms of total reactions: Resolving horizontally and vertically: $$R_1 \cos\phi_1 = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) \implies R_1 = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1}$$ Vertical balance: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_1 \sin\phi_1 - W = 0$$ Substituting $R_1$: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} = W$$ $$R_2 \left[ \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_1 - \sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} \right] = W$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\cos(A + B) = \cos A \cos B - \sin A \sin B$: $$R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1} = W \implies R_2 = W \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}$$

2. Equilibrium of the Horizontal Wedge: The wedge moves horizontally to the left ($\leftarrow$) under applied horizontal force $P$. Forces acting on the wedge: - Applied horizontal driving force $P$ acting to the left ($\leftarrow$). - Mutual contact reaction from the block: by Newton's third law, this is $R_2$, directed downward and to the left at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical. - Ground normal reaction $N_3$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). - Ground friction force $F_3 = \mu_3 N_3$ acting to the right ($\rightarrow$) opposing motion. $N_3$ and $F_3$ combine into the total ground reaction $R_3$ inclined at friction angle $\phi_3$ to the vertical.

Setting up force balance for the wedge: Resolving all forces along the horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies R_3 \cos\phi_3 - R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) = 0 \implies R_3 = R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies P - R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_3 \sin\phi_3 = 0$$ $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_3 \sin\phi_3$$ Substituting $R_3$: $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} = R_2 \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_3 + \cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$ Using $\sin(A + B) = \sin A \cos B + \cos A \sin B$: $$P = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ Now substituting the derived expression for $R_2$ from block equilibrium: $$P = W \left[ \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)} \right] \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$

Special Case (Uniform Friction Coefficient $\phi_1 = \phi_2 = \phi_3 = \phi$): $$P = W \frac{\cos\phi \sin(\alpha + 2\phi)}{\cos\phi \cos(\alpha + 2\phi)} = W \tan(\alpha + 2\phi)$$ This simple formula gives the minimum horizontal force required to drive the wedge and lift the load $W$.

Condition for Self-Locking of the Wedge: When the driving force $P$ is completely removed ($P = 0$), the wedge will remain locked in position without being pushed backward by load $W$ provided the friction forces are sufficient. The condition for self-locking is: $$\alpha < \phi_2 + \phi_3 \quad \text{or for equal friction: } \alpha < 2\phi$$ If the wedge angle $\alpha$ is smaller than the sum of friction angles, the wedge is self-locking.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

A wedge is an inclined plane machine used in engineering practice to lift heavy machinery loads, make small vertical adjustments, or split materials through the application of a comparatively small transverse effort. Because of high frictional resistance along sliding faces, wedges possess the desirable property of being self-locking, meaning the lifted load does not slide back down when the driving effort is removed.

Problem Description and System Geometry: Consider a heavy block of weight $W$ constrained to move strictly in the vertical direction by smooth or rough vertical guides. The block is lifted by driving a slender wedge of self-weight $w$ (often negligible, $w \approx 0$) and wedge angle $\alpha$ horizontally underneath it by applying an external horizontal driving force $P$. Let the friction coefficients be: - $\mu_1 = \tan\phi_1$ along the vertical guide wall. - $\mu_2 = \tan\phi_2$ along the inclined contact interface between block and wedge. - $\mu_3 = \tan\phi_3$ along the horizontal ground surface supporting the wedge. To analyze this system, we separate the assembly into two distinct free body diagrams: one for the vertical block and one for the horizontal wedge.

1. Equilibrium of the Vertical Block (Lifting Mode): The block moves vertically upward ($\uparrow$). Forces acting on the block: - Downward weight $W$ ($\downarrow$). - Normal reaction $N_1$ from the vertical wall acting horizontally to the right ($\rightarrow$). - Frictional force $F_1 = \mu_1 N_1$ along the vertical wall acting vertically downward ($\downarrow$) opposing upward motion. - Contact normal reaction $N_2$ perpendicular to the inclined face of the wedge, acting at angle $\alpha$ to the vertical. - Frictional force $F_2 = \mu_2 N_2$ along the inclined interface, directed downward and to the right along the slope ($\searrow$) opposing the relative motion of the block sliding up the wedge.

The normal reaction $N_2$ and friction $F_2$ combine into a single Total Reaction $R_2$ inclined at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical, where $\phi_2 = \tan^{-1}\mu_2$. Similarly, $N_1$ and $F_1$ combine into total reaction $R_1$ inclined at angle $\phi_1$ below the horizontal. Applying equations of equilibrium for the block: $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies N_1 - N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \cos\alpha = 0$$ $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies N_2 \cos\alpha - \mu_2 N_2 \sin\alpha - \mu_1 N_1 - W = 0$$ Expressing these in terms of total reactions: Resolving horizontally and vertically: $$R_1 \cos\phi_1 = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) \implies R_1 = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1}$$ Vertical balance: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_1 \sin\phi_1 - W = 0$$ Substituting $R_1$: $$R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} = W$$ $$R_2 \left[ \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_1 - \sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_1}{\cos\phi_1} \right] = W$$ Using the trigonometric identity $\cos(A + B) = \cos A \cos B - \sin A \sin B$: $$R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_1} = W \implies R_2 = W \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)}$$

2. Equilibrium of the Horizontal Wedge: The wedge moves horizontally to the left ($\leftarrow$) under applied horizontal force $P$. Forces acting on the wedge: - Applied horizontal driving force $P$ acting to the left ($\leftarrow$). - Mutual contact reaction from the block: by Newton's third law, this is $R_2$, directed downward and to the left at angle $(\alpha + \phi_2)$ to the vertical. - Ground normal reaction $N_3$ acting vertically upward ($\uparrow$). - Ground friction force $F_3 = \mu_3 N_3$ acting to the right ($\rightarrow$) opposing motion. $N_3$ and $F_3$ combine into the total ground reaction $R_3$ inclined at friction angle $\phi_3$ to the vertical.

Setting up force balance for the wedge: Resolving all forces along the horizontal and vertical axes: $$\Sigma F_y = 0 \implies R_3 \cos\phi_3 - R_2 \cos(\alpha + \phi_2) = 0 \implies R_3 = R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \implies P - R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) - R_3 \sin\phi_3 = 0$$ $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_3 \sin\phi_3$$ Substituting $R_3$: $$P = R_2 \sin(\alpha + \phi_2) + R_2 \frac{\cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} = R_2 \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2)\cos\phi_3 + \cos(\alpha + \phi_2)\sin\phi_3}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$ Using $\sin(A + B) = \sin A \cos B + \cos A \sin B$: $$P = R_2 \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3}$$ Now substituting the derived expression for $R_2$ from block equilibrium: $$P = W \left[ \frac{\cos\phi_1}{\cos(\alpha + \phi_1 + \phi_2)} \right] \left[ \frac{\sin(\alpha + \phi_2 + \phi_3)}{\cos\phi_3} \right]$$

Special Case (Uniform Friction Coefficient $\phi_1 = \phi_2 = \phi_3 = \phi$): $$P = W \frac{\cos\phi \sin(\alpha + 2\phi)}{\cos\phi \cos(\alpha + 2\phi)} = W \tan(\alpha + 2\phi)$$ This simple formula gives the minimum horizontal force required to drive the wedge and lift the load $W$.

Condition for Self-Locking of the Wedge: When the driving force $P$ is completely removed ($P = 0$), the wedge will remain locked in position without being pushed backward by load $W$ provided the friction forces are sufficient. The condition for self-locking is: $$\alpha < \phi_2 + \phi_3 \quad \text{or for equal friction: } \alpha < 2\phi$$ If the wedge angle $\alpha$ is smaller than the sum of friction angles, the wedge is self-locking.

Derive the formula for the ratio of belt tensions T1/T2 = e^(mu*theta) for a flat belt passing over a fixed cylindrical pulley. Derive the formula for the ratio of belt tensions T1/T2 = e^(mu*theta) for a flat belt passing over a fixed cylindrical pulley. Derive the formula for the ratio of belt tensions T1/T2 = e^(mu*theta) for a flat belt passing over a fixed cylindrical pulley. Obtain the expression for the power transmitted by the belt and discuss the effect of centrifugal tension.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\mu \theta}, \quad P = (T_1 - T_2)v = T_1(1 - e^{-\mu\theta})v, \quad T_c = m v^2$$

Flat Belt Tension Ratio: $\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\mu\theta}$ where $\theta$ is wrap angle in radians.
Power Transmitted: $P = (T_1 - T_2)v$ where pitch speed $v = \frac{\pi D N}{60}\text{ m/s}$.
Centrifugal Tension: $T_c = m v^2$; reduces grip $\frac{T_1 - T_c}{T_2 - T_c} = e^{\mu\theta}$; maximum power occurs when $T_c = T_{max}/3$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Flexible power transmission elements—such as flat belts, V-belts, ropes, and band brakes—transmit torque between rotating shafts through frictional grip developed over the curved circumferential contact surface of a cylindrical drum or pulley. As torque is transmitted, the belt stretches slightly, establishing an unequal tension distribution: a higher tension $T_1$ on the driving (tight) side and a lower tension $T_2$ on the trailing (slack) side.

Derivation of the Ratio of Belt Tensions ($\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\mu \theta}$): Consider a flat flexible belt running over a fixed or rotating circular pulley of radius $R$. Let: - $\theta$ = total angle of lap / wrap (contact angle) in radians. - $\mu$ = coefficient of friction between belt and pulley surface. - $T_1$ = tension on the tight side. - $T_2$ = tension on the slack side ($T_1 > T_2$).

To evaluate the tension profile, we isolate an infinitesimal differential segment of the belt subtending an angle $d\theta$ at the center of the pulley: - At one end of the segment, the belt tension is $T$. - At the other end, the tension increases to $T + dT$. - The pulley surface exerts an outward radial normal reaction $dN$ on the element. - The frictional resistance force $dF = \mu dN$ acts tangentially to the pulley, opposing the relative motion/slip of the belt.

Force Equilibrium of the Belt Element: The normal reaction $dN$ acts along the radial centerline bisecting angle $d\theta$. The tensions $T$ and $T + dT$ act along tangents at angles of $\frac{d\theta}{2}$ to the perpendicular axis. 1. Radial Force Balance: Summing forces along the radial direction toward the pulley center: $$\Sigma F_r = dN - T \sin\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) - (T + dT)\sin\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) = 0$$ $$dN = [2T + dT]\sin\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right)$$ Because $d\theta$ is an infinitesimal angle, we apply the small-angle approximation $\sin\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) \approx \frac{d\theta}{2}$. Neglecting the higher-order product $dT \cdot \frac{d\theta}{2}$: $$dN = 2T \left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) = T d\theta \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

2. Tangential Force Balance: Summing forces along the tangential direction: $$\Sigma F_t = (T + dT)\cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) - T \cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) - dF = 0$$ Using the approximation $\cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) \approx 1$: $$(T + dT)(1) - T(1) - dF = 0 \implies dT = dF$$ At limiting friction equilibrium, $dF = \mu dN$. Therefore: $$dT = \mu dN \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

3. Integration Across the Wrap Angle: Substituting Equation 1 ($dN = T d\theta$) into Equation 2: $$dT = \mu (T d\theta) \implies \frac{dT}{T} = \mu d\theta$$ Integrating this separable differential equation across the entire contact wrap angle from $\theta = 0$ (where $T = T_2$, slack side) to $\theta = \theta$ (where $T = T_1$, tight side): $$\int_{T_2}^{T_1} \frac{dT}{T} = \mu \int_0^\theta d\theta$$ $$\left[ \ln T \right]_{T_2}^{T_1} = \mu [\theta]_0^\theta \implies \ln T_1 - \ln T_2 = \mu \theta$$ $$\ln\left(\frac{T_1}{T_2}\right) = \mu \theta$$ Taking the exponential on both sides yields the classical Belt Friction Equation: $$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\mu \theta}$$ Important Notes on the Tension Ratio: 1. The angle of wrap $\theta$ must be expressed strictly in radians ($\theta_{\text{rad}} = \theta_{\text{deg}} \times \frac{\pi}{180}$). 2. For V-belts or ropes running in grooved pulleys with groove angle $2\beta$, the normal reaction is increased by wedging action: $$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\frac{\mu \theta}{\sin\beta}}$$

Power Transmitted by a Belt Drive: Let a belt drive transmit torque to a pulley of diameter $D$ rotating at speed $N$ revolutions per minute ($\text{rpm}$). The linear pitch line velocity of the belt is: $$v = \frac{\pi D N}{60}\text{ m/s}$$ The effective tangential driving force transmitted by the belt to the pulley circumference is the difference between tight-side and slack-side tensions: $$F_{eff} = T_1 - T_2$$ The mechanical power $P$ transmitted is the product of effective driving force and belt speed: $$P = (T_1 - T_2) v = (T_1 - T_2) \frac{\pi D N}{60} \quad \text{(in Watts)}$$ Expressing power in terms of maximum tension $T_1$: $$P = T_1 \left(1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1}\right) v = T_1 \left(1 - e^{-\mu\theta}\right) v$$

Centrifugal Tension Correction ($T_c$): When the belt runs at high speeds, continuous centripetal acceleration gives rise to centrifugal tension: $$T_c = m v^2$$ where $m$ is the mass per unit length of the belt ($\text{kg/m}$) and $v$ is belt velocity. Incorporating $T_c$, the effective tensions governing friction become: $$\frac{T_1 - T_c}{T_2 - T_c} = e^{\mu \theta}$$ The maximum power transmission occurs at an optimum velocity $v = \sqrt{\frac{T_{max}}{3m}}$, where centrifugal tension equals one-third of the maximum allowable belt tension ($T_c = \frac{T_{max}}{3}$).

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Flexible power transmission elements—such as flat belts, V-belts, ropes, and band brakes—transmit torque between rotating shafts through frictional grip developed over the curved circumferential contact surface of a cylindrical drum or pulley. As torque is transmitted, the belt stretches slightly, establishing an unequal tension distribution: a higher tension $T_1$ on the driving (tight) side and a lower tension $T_2$ on the trailing (slack) side.

The angle of wrap $\theta$ must be expressed strictly in radians ($\theta_{\text{rad}} = \theta_{\text{deg}} \times \frac{\pi}{180}$). 2. For V-belts or ropes running in grooved pulleys with groove angle $2\beta$, the normal reaction is increased by wedging action: $$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\frac{\mu \theta}{\sin\beta}}$$

Power Transmitted by a Belt Drive: Let a belt drive transmit torque to a pulley of diameter $D$ rotating at speed $N$ revolutions per minute ($\text{rpm}$). The linear pitch line velocity of the belt is: $$v = \frac{\pi D N}{60}\text{ m/s}$$ The effective tangential driving force transmitted by the belt to the pulley circumference is the difference between tight-side and slack-side tensions: $$F_{eff} = T_1 - T_2$$ The mechanical power $P$ transmitted is the product of effective driving force and belt speed: $$P = (T_1 - T_2) v = (T_1 - T_2) \frac{\pi D N}{60} \quad \text{(in Watts)}$$ Expressing power in terms of maximum tension $T_1$: $$P = T_1 \left(1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1}\right) v = T_1 \left(1 - e^{-\mu\theta}\right) v$$

Centrifugal Tension Correction ($T_c$): When the belt runs at high speeds, continuous centripetal acceleration gives rise to centrifugal tension: $$T_c = m v^2$$ where $m$ is the mass per unit length of the belt ($\text{kg/m}$) and $v$ is belt velocity. Incorporating $T_c$, the effective tensions governing friction become: $$\frac{T_1 - T_c}{T_2 - T_c} = e^{\mu \theta}$$ The maximum power transmission occurs at an optimum velocity $v = \sqrt{\frac{T_{max}}{3m}}$, where centrifugal tension equals one-third of the maximum allowable belt tension ($T_c = \frac{T_{max}}{3}$).

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

2. Tangential Force Balance: Summing forces along the tangential direction: $$\Sigma F_t = (T + dT)\cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) - T \cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) - dF = 0$$ Using the approximation $\cos\left(\frac{d\theta}{2}\right) \approx 1$: $$(T + dT)(1) - T(1) - dF = 0 \implies dT = dF$$ At limiting friction equilibrium, $dF = \mu dN$. Therefore: $$dT = \mu dN \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

3. Integration Across the Wrap Angle: Substituting Equation 1 ($dN = T d\theta$) into Equation 2: $$dT = \mu (T d\theta) \implies \frac{dT}{T} = \mu d\theta$$ Integrating this separable differential equation across the entire contact wrap angle from $\theta = 0$ (where $T = T_2$, slack side) to $\theta = \theta$ (where $T = T_1$, tight side): $$\int_{T_2}^{T_1} \frac{dT}{T} = \mu \int_0^\theta d\theta$$ $$\left[ \ln T \right]_{T_2}^{T_1} = \mu [\theta]_0^\theta \implies \ln T_1 - \ln T_2 = \mu \theta$$ $$\ln\left(\frac{T_1}{T_2}\right) = \mu \theta$$ Taking the exponential on both sides yields the classical Belt Friction Equation: $$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\mu \theta}$$ Important Notes on the Tension Ratio: 1. The angle of wrap $\theta$ must be expressed strictly in radians ($\theta_{\text{rad}} = \theta_{\text{deg}} \times \frac{\pi}{180}$). 2. For V-belts or ropes running in grooved pulleys with groove angle $2\beta$, the normal reaction is increased by wedging action: $$\frac{T_1}{T_2} = e^{\frac{\mu \theta}{\sin\beta}}$$

Power Transmitted by a Belt Drive: Let a belt drive transmit torque to a pulley of diameter $D$ rotating at speed $N$ revolutions per minute ($\text{rpm}$). The linear pitch line velocity of the belt is: $$v = \frac{\pi D N}{60}\text{ m/s}$$ The effective tangential driving force transmitted by the belt to the pulley circumference is the difference between tight-side and slack-side tensions: $$F_{eff} = T_1 - T_2$$ The mechanical power $P$ transmitted is the product of effective driving force and belt speed: $$P = (T_1 - T_2) v = (T_1 - T_2) \frac{\pi D N}{60} \quad \text{(in Watts)}$$ Expressing power in terms of maximum tension $T_1$: $$P = T_1 \left(1 - \frac{T_2}{T_1}\right) v = T_1 \left(1 - e^{-\mu\theta}\right) v$$

Centrifugal Tension Correction ($T_c$): When the belt runs at high speeds, continuous centripetal acceleration gives rise to centrifugal tension: $$T_c = m v^2$$ where $m$ is the mass per unit length of the belt ($\text{kg/m}$) and $v$ is belt velocity. Incorporating $T_c$, the effective tensions governing friction become: $$\frac{T_1 - T_c}{T_2 - T_c} = e^{\mu \theta}$$ The maximum power transmission occurs at an optimum velocity $v = \sqrt{\frac{T_{max}}{3m}}$, where centrifugal tension equals one-third of the maximum allowable belt tension ($T_c = \frac{T_{max}}{3}$).

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 2.4: Infinitesimal Element of Belt on Pulley Showing Differential Tensions and Normal Reaction — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Theory & Application ID: MECH-Q10

State the assumptions in the analysis of pin-jointed trusses. State the conditions for a truss to be perfect, deficient, or redundant. State the rules for identifying zero-force members by inspection and explain the Method of Joints and Method of Sections. State the assumptions in the analysis of pin-jointed trusses. State the conditions for a truss to be perfect, deficient, or redundant. State the rules for identifying zero-force members by inspection and explain the Method of Joints and Method of Sections. State the assumptions in the analysis of pin-jointed trusses. State the conditions for a truss to be perfect, deficient, or redundant. State the rules for identifying zero-force members by inspection and explain the Method of Joints and Method of Sections.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$m = 2j - 3 \text{ (Perfect Truss)}, \quad \Sigma F_x = 0, \; \Sigma F_y = 0 \text{ (Joints)}, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0 \text{ (Sections)}$$

Truss Assumptions: Frictionless pin joints, concurrent axes, axial loads only ($T$ tension or $C$ compression).
Zero-Force Members: Two non-collinear members meeting at unloaded joint carry zero force; two collinear plus one third non-collinear $\implies$ third is zero.
Method of Joints: Analyzes joint by joint ($\Sigma F_x=0, \Sigma F_y=0$).
Method of Sections: Cuts across up to 3 members; solves with $\Sigma M_O = 0$.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$m = 2j - 3 \text{ (Perfect Truss)}, \quad \Sigma F_x = 0, \; \Sigma F_y = 0 \text{ (Joints)}, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0 \text{ (Sections)}$$

Truss Assumptions: Frictionless pin joints, concurrent axes, axial loads only ($T$ tension or $C$ compression).
Zero-Force Members: Two non-collinear members meeting at unloaded joint carry zero force; two collinear plus one third non-collinear $\implies$ third is zero.
Method of Joints: Analyzes joint by joint ($\Sigma F_x=0, \Sigma F_y=0$).
Method of Sections: Cuts across up to 3 members; solves with $\Sigma M_O = 0$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A truss is an engineering structure composed of slender structural members connected together at their endpoints to form a rigid structural framework. Widely used in bridge spans, roof trusses, crane booms, and transmission towers, trusses carry loads over long spans while minimizing material usage.

Assumptions in Ideal Planar Truss Analysis: 1. All truss members are connected together at their ends by frictionless, smooth cylindrical pins. 2. All external loads and support reactions are applied strictly at the pin joints (no transverse loads are applied directly along member lengths). 3. The centroidal longitudinal axes of all members meeting at any joint are concurrent. 4. The self-weight of the members is negligible compared to the applied working loads. Under these standard assumptions, every member acts as a pure two-force member, carrying only axial forces (either axial tension $T$ pulling away from joints or axial compression $C$ pushing into joints), with zero internal bending moments or shear forces.

Structural Determinacy and Classification: Let $m$ be the number of members and $j$ be the number of pin joints in a planar truss: 1. Perfect (Statically Determinate) Truss: $m = 2j - 3$. The truss has just enough members to maintain geometric stability, and internal forces can be determined using equations of statics alone. 2. Deficient (Imperfect / Mechanism) Truss: $m < 2j - 3$. The truss lacks sufficient members and will collapse under certain loads. 3. Redundant (Statically Indeterminate) Truss: $m > 2j - 3$. The truss possesses extra members, requiring deformation analysis to solve.

Rules for Identifying Zero-Force Members by Inspection: Inspecting a truss for zero-force members before performing detailed calculations simplifies the analysis: 1. Rule 1 (Two-Member Joint): If only two non-collinear members meet at an unloaded joint (no external load or support reaction), both members carry zero force. 2. Rule 2 (Three-Member Joint with Two Collinear): If three members meet at an unloaded joint where two of the members are collinear, the third non-collinear member carries zero force. The two collinear members carry equal and opposite forces. 3. Rule 3 (Two Collinear with Applied Load): If two collinear members meet at a joint with an external load applied parallel to them, a third perpendicular member carries zero force.

Method of Joints: The Method of Joints isolates individual pin joints as free body diagrams. Because all member forces meeting at a joint are concurrent, the moment equilibrium equation is trivially satisfied ($\Sigma M = 0$). Only two independent scalar equilibrium equations apply at each joint: $$\Sigma F_x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \Sigma F_y = 0$$ Systematic Analysis Procedure: 1. Determine external support reactions by treating the entire truss as a single rigid body and applying $\Sigma F_x = 0, \Sigma F_y = 0, \Sigma M = 0$. 2. Identify and eliminate all zero-force members using the inspection rules. 3. Locate a joint that connects no more than two unknown member forces. 4. Draw the isolated joint FBD, assuming unknown member forces are tensile (pulling away from the joint). If the computed force is positive, the member is in tension ($T$); if negative, it is in compression ($C$). 5. Apply $\Sigma F_x = 0$ and $\Sigma F_y = 0$ to solve for the two unknown forces. 6. Advance sequentially to adjacent joints, carrying forward solved forces with opposite senses (Newton's third law), until all member forces are determined.

Method of Sections (Ritter's Method): When member forces are required in only a few specific internal members (e.g., near the center of a large bridge span), the Method of Joints is tedious. The Method of Sections determines internal forces directly by cutting the entire truss into two separate parts. Procedure: 1. Determine external support reactions using whole-truss equilibrium. 2. Pass an imaginary cut section line $a-a$ through the truss, cutting through the members whose forces are required. - Constraint: The cut line must not cut more than three non-concurrent, non-parallel members. 3. Isolate either the left or right part of the truss as a free body diagram. 4. Replace the cut members with unknown axial forces acting along their lines of action. 5. Because the isolated part is a rigid body in 2D, apply the three full equations of equilibrium: $$\Sigma F_x = 0, \quad \Sigma F_y = 0, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0$$ Choosing the moment center $O$ at the intersection of two unknown forces eliminates both from the moment equation, enabling direct solution of the third unknown member force in a single algebraic step.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Module 3

Centroid, Center of Gravity, Pappus Theorems and Moment of Inertia

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Theory & Application ID: MECH-Q11

Define the first moment of area and centroid of a composite plane area. Derive the centroid formulas and determine the centroid coordinates of an unsymmetrical T-section. Define the first moment of area and centroid of a composite plane area. Derive the centroid formulas and determine the centroid coordinates of an unsymmetrical T-section. Define the first moment of area and centroid of a composite plane area. Derive the centroid formulas and determine the centroid coordinates of an unsymmetrical T-section.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\bar{x} = \frac{\sum A_i x_i}{\sum A_i}, \quad \bar{y} = \frac{\sum A_i y_i}{\sum A_i}, \quad Q_x = \int y \, dA = A \bar{y}$$

Centroid of Area: Geometric center based on first moment of area: $A \bar{y} = \Sigma A_i y_i$.
Axis of Symmetry: If a section possesses an axis of symmetry, the centroid must lie on that axis.
Composite T-Section: Web ($20\times 180$) and Flange ($150\times 20$) give total area $6600\text{ mm}^2$ and $\bar{y} = \frac{894,000}{6600} = 135.45\text{ mm}$ from base.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Centroid of a plane area represents the geometric center of the figure, defined as the point where the entire area may be considered concentrated. While the term Center of Gravity ($CG$) applies to physical bodies possessing mass and weight, Centroid refers strictly to geometric entities such as curves, plane areas, and volumes where density is uniform or irrelevant.

First Moment of Area and Mathematical Definition: The first moment of a plane area $A$ with respect to a reference coordinate axis is defined as the integral of the product of each differential area element $dA$ and its perpendicular coordinate distance to that axis: $$Q_y = \int_A x \, dA \quad \text{(First Moment of Area with respect to the } y\text{-axis)}$$ $$Q_x = \int_A y \, dA \quad \text{(First Moment of Area with respect to the } x\text{-axis)}$$ If the entire area $A = \int dA$ were concentrated at a single centroidal point $C(\bar{x}, \bar{y})$, its moments about the $y$ and $x$ axes would be $A \bar{x}$ and $A \bar{y}$ respectively. Equating these gives: $$\bar{x} = \frac{\int x \, dA}{\int dA} = \frac{Q_y}{A}$$ $$\bar{y} = \frac{\int y \, dA}{\int dA} = \frac{Q_x}{A}$$

For a composite figure composed of $n$ simple geometric sub-shapes (rectangles, triangles, circles) of known individual areas $A_i$ and centroidal coordinates $(x_i, y_i)$, the integral converts into discrete summations: $$\bar{x} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i} = \frac{A_1 x_1 + A_2 x_2 + \dots + A_n x_n}{A_1 + A_2 + \dots + A_n}$$ $$\bar{y} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i y_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i} = \frac{A_1 y_1 + A_2 y_2 + \dots + A_n y_n}{A_1 + A_2 + \dots + A_n}$$ If the composite shape contains cutouts or hollow holes of area $A_{hole}$, their area is treated as negative (subtracted from the sum).

Worked Problem: Centroid Calculation of an Unsymmetrical T-Section: Problem Statement: Determine the coordinates of the centroid of a structural T-section composed of a top horizontal flange of dimensions $150\text{ mm} \times 20\text{ mm}$ and a vertical web of dimensions $20\text{ mm} \times 180\text{ mm}$.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Setting Up the Reference Coordinate Axes: - Place the horizontal $x$-axis along the bottom edge of the vertical web ($y = 0$). - Place the vertical $y$-axis along the vertical line of symmetry passing through the center of the web. Because the T-section is symmetric about the vertical centerline: $$\bar{x} = 0$$ The centroid lies on the vertical axis of symmetry. We only need to compute $\bar{y}$.

2. Subdividing into Standard Simple Rectangles: - Shape 1 (Vertical Web): - Width $b_1 = 20\text{ mm}$, Height $h_1 = 180\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_1 = b_1 \times h_1 = 20 \times 180 = 3600\text{ mm}^2$ - Height of centroid $y_1$ from the base ($y = 0$): $$y_1 = \frac{h_1}{2} = \frac{180}{2} = 90\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_1 y_1 = 3600 \times 90 = 324,000\text{ mm}^3$

- Shape 2 (Top Horizontal Flange): - Width $b_2 = 150\text{ mm}$, Thickness $h_2 = 20\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_2 = b_2 \times h_2 = 150 \times 20 = 3000\text{ mm}^2$ - The flange rests on top of the web (web height $= 180\text{ mm}$). - Height of centroid $y_2$ from base ($y = 0$): $$y_2 = h_1 + \frac{h_2}{2} = 180 + \frac{20}{2} = 180 + 10 = 190\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_2 y_2 = 3000 \times 190 = 570,000\text{ mm}^3$

3. Tabular Calculation of Composite Centroid: - Total Area: $$A_{total} = A_1 + A_2 = 3600 + 3000 = 6600\text{ mm}^2$$ - Total First Moment of Area about Base: $$\Sigma A_i y_i = A_1 y_1 + A_2 y_2 = 324,000 + 570,000 = 894,000\text{ mm}^3$$ 4. Calculating $\bar{y}$: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\Sigma A_i y_i}{A_{total}} = \frac{894,000\text{ mm}^3}{6600\text{ mm}^2} \approx 135.45\text{ mm}$$ The centroid of the T-section is located on the vertical line of symmetry at a distance of $135.45\text{ mm}$ above the bottom edge of the web.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

2. Subdividing into Standard Simple Rectangles: - Shape 1 (Vertical Web): - Width $b_1 = 20\text{ mm}$, Height $h_1 = 180\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_1 = b_1 \times h_1 = 20 \times 180 = 3600\text{ mm}^2$ - Height of centroid $y_1$ from the base ($y = 0$): $$y_1 = \frac{h_1}{2} = \frac{180}{2} = 90\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_1 y_1 = 3600 \times 90 = 324,000\text{ mm}^3$

- Shape 2 (Top Horizontal Flange): - Width $b_2 = 150\text{ mm}$, Thickness $h_2 = 20\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_2 = b_2 \times h_2 = 150 \times 20 = 3000\text{ mm}^2$ - The flange rests on top of the web (web height $= 180\text{ mm}$). - Height of centroid $y_2$ from base ($y = 0$): $$y_2 = h_1 + \frac{h_2}{2} = 180 + \frac{20}{2} = 180 + 10 = 190\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_2 y_2 = 3000 \times 190 = 570,000\text{ mm}^3$

3. Tabular Calculation of Composite Centroid: - Total Area: $$A_{total} = A_1 + A_2 = 3600 + 3000 = 6600\text{ mm}^2$$ - Total First Moment of Area about Base: $$\Sigma A_i y_i = A_1 y_1 + A_2 y_2 = 324,000 + 570,000 = 894,000\text{ mm}^3$$ 4. Calculating $\bar{y}$: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\Sigma A_i y_i}{A_{total}} = \frac{894,000\text{ mm}^3}{6600\text{ mm}^2} \approx 135.45\text{ mm}$$ The centroid of the T-section is located on the vertical line of symmetry at a distance of $135.45\text{ mm}$ above the bottom edge of the web.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

The Centroid of a plane area represents the geometric center of the figure, defined as the point where the entire area may be considered concentrated. While the term Center of Gravity ($CG$) applies to physical bodies possessing mass and weight, Centroid refers strictly to geometric entities such as curves, plane areas, and volumes where density is uniform or irrelevant.

First Moment of Area and Mathematical Definition: The first moment of a plane area $A$ with respect to a reference coordinate axis is defined as the integral of the product of each differential area element $dA$ and its perpendicular coordinate distance to that axis: $$Q_y = \int_A x \, dA \quad \text{(First Moment of Area with respect to the } y\text{-axis)}$$ $$Q_x = \int_A y \, dA \quad \text{(First Moment of Area with respect to the } x\text{-axis)}$$ If the entire area $A = \int dA$ were concentrated at a single centroidal point $C(\bar{x}, \bar{y})$, its moments about the $y$ and $x$ axes would be $A \bar{x}$ and $A \bar{y}$ respectively. Equating these gives: $$\bar{x} = \frac{\int x \, dA}{\int dA} = \frac{Q_y}{A}$$ $$\bar{y} = \frac{\int y \, dA}{\int dA} = \frac{Q_x}{A}$$

For a composite figure composed of $n$ simple geometric sub-shapes (rectangles, triangles, circles) of known individual areas $A_i$ and centroidal coordinates $(x_i, y_i)$, the integral converts into discrete summations: $$\bar{x} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i x_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i} = \frac{A_1 x_1 + A_2 x_2 + \dots + A_n x_n}{A_1 + A_2 + \dots + A_n}$$ $$\bar{y} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i y_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n A_i} = \frac{A_1 y_1 + A_2 y_2 + \dots + A_n y_n}{A_1 + A_2 + \dots + A_n}$$ If the composite shape contains cutouts or hollow holes of area $A_{hole}$, their area is treated as negative (subtracted from the sum).

Worked Problem: Centroid Calculation of an Unsymmetrical T-Section: Problem Statement: Determine the coordinates of the centroid of a structural T-section composed of a top horizontal flange of dimensions $150\text{ mm} \times 20\text{ mm}$ and a vertical web of dimensions $20\text{ mm} \times 180\text{ mm}$.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Setting Up the Reference Coordinate Axes: - Place the horizontal $x$-axis along the bottom edge of the vertical web ($y = 0$). - Place the vertical $y$-axis along the vertical line of symmetry passing through the center of the web. Because the T-section is symmetric about the vertical centerline: $$\bar{x} = 0$$ The centroid lies on the vertical axis of symmetry. We only need to compute $\bar{y}$.

2. Subdividing into Standard Simple Rectangles: - Shape 1 (Vertical Web): - Width $b_1 = 20\text{ mm}$, Height $h_1 = 180\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_1 = b_1 \times h_1 = 20 \times 180 = 3600\text{ mm}^2$ - Height of centroid $y_1$ from the base ($y = 0$): $$y_1 = \frac{h_1}{2} = \frac{180}{2} = 90\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_1 y_1 = 3600 \times 90 = 324,000\text{ mm}^3$

- Shape 2 (Top Horizontal Flange): - Width $b_2 = 150\text{ mm}$, Thickness $h_2 = 20\text{ mm}$ - Area $A_2 = b_2 \times h_2 = 150 \times 20 = 3000\text{ mm}^2$ - The flange rests on top of the web (web height $= 180\text{ mm}$). - Height of centroid $y_2$ from base ($y = 0$): $$y_2 = h_1 + \frac{h_2}{2} = 180 + \frac{20}{2} = 180 + 10 = 190\text{ mm}$$ - First moment $A_2 y_2 = 3000 \times 190 = 570,000\text{ mm}^3$

3. Tabular Calculation of Composite Centroid: - Total Area: $$A_{total} = A_1 + A_2 = 3600 + 3000 = 6600\text{ mm}^2$$ - Total First Moment of Area about Base: $$\Sigma A_i y_i = A_1 y_1 + A_2 y_2 = 324,000 + 570,000 = 894,000\text{ mm}^3$$ 4. Calculating $\bar{y}$: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\Sigma A_i y_i}{A_{total}} = \frac{894,000\text{ mm}^3}{6600\text{ mm}^2} \approx 135.45\text{ mm}$$ The centroid of the T-section is located on the vertical line of symmetry at a distance of $135.45\text{ mm}$ above the bottom edge of the web.

State and prove Pappus-Guldinus Theorem 1 (Surface Area of Revolution) and Theorem 2 (Volume of Revolution). $$A = 2\pi \bar{y} L \quad (\text{Theorem 1}), \quad V = 2\pi \bar{y} A \quad (\text{Theorem 2})$$

Pappus Theorem 1: Surface area of revolution equals curve length $L$ times distance traveled by its centroid ($2\pi \bar{y} L$).
Pappus Theorem 2: Volume of revolution equals plane area $A$ times distance traveled by its centroid ($2\pi \bar{y} A$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The theorems of Pappus of Alexandria (circa 300 AD), rediscovered by Swiss mathematician Paul Guldin in 1640, provide elegant integral formulations relating the surface area and volume of bodies of revolution to the centroidal paths of their generating plane curves and plane areas.

Theorem 1: Surface Area of Revolution: Statement: The surface area generated by rotating a plane curve of finite length $L$ about a non-intersecting coplanar axis is equal to the product of the length of the generating curve and the distance traveled by its centroid during the revolution. If the curve is rotated through a full angle of $2\pi$ radians ($360^\circ$): $$A = 2\pi \bar{y} L$$ where $L$ is the arc length of the generating curve and $\bar{y}$ is the perpendicular distance of the centroid of the curve from the axis of rotation.

Mathematical Proof of Theorem 1: Let a plane curve of total length $L$ lie in the $xy$-plane, and let the $x$-axis be the axis of revolution. Let $dL$ be an infinitesimal element of the curve located at vertical distance $y$ from the $x$-axis. When the curve rotates through a full revolution ($2\pi$ radians) about the $x$-axis, the differential element $dL$ sweeps out a circular ribbon of radius $y$ and width $dL$. The differential surface area generated is: $$dA = 2\pi y \, dL$$ Integrating over the entire length of the curve: $$A = \int_L 2\pi y \, dL = 2\pi \int_L y \, dL$$ By definition of the centroid of a line/curve: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\int_L y \, dL}{L} \implies \int_L y \, dL = \bar{y} L$$ Substituting this into the surface area integral: $$A = 2\pi \bar{y} L$$ If rotated through an arbitrary angle $\theta$ (in radians), $A = \theta \bar{y} L$. This completes the proof.

Theorem 2: Volume of Revolution: Statement: The volume of a solid generated by rotating a plane area $A$ about a non-intersecting coplanar axis is equal to the product of the generating area and the distance traveled by the centroid of that area during the revolution. For a complete revolution of $2\pi$ radians: $$V = 2\pi \bar{y} A$$ where $A$ is the generating plane area and $\bar{y}$ is the distance of the centroid of the area from the revolution axis.

Mathematical Proof of Theorem 2: Let a plane lamina of area $A$ lie in the $xy$-plane, and let the $x$-axis be the axis of revolution. Consider a differential area element $dA$ situated at vertical coordinate $y$. When the area rotates through $2\pi$ radians about the $x$-axis, the element $dA$ sweeps out a circular ring of radius $y$, cross-sectional area $dA$, and circumference $2\pi y$. The differential volume generated is: $$dV = 2\pi y \, dA$$ Integrating over the entire area $A$: $$V = \int_A 2\pi y \, dA = 2\pi \int_A y \, dA$$ By definition of the centroid of a plane area: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\int_A y \, dA}{A} \implies \int_A y \, dA = \bar{y} A$$ Substituting this yields: $$V = 2\pi \bar{y} A$$ This completes the proof.

Applications to Classic Geometric Solids: 1. Surface Area and Volume of a Sphere: - Generating Curve: A semi-circular arc of radius $R$ rotating about its diameter diameter axis. - Length of semi-circular arc: $L = \pi R$ - Centroid distance from diameter axis: $\bar{y} = \frac{2R}{\pi}$ - Surface Area by Theorem 1: $$A = 2\pi \bar{y} L = 2\pi \left(\frac{2R}{\pi}\right)(\pi R) = 4\pi R^2$$ - Generating Area: A semi-circular area of radius $R$ rotating about its diameter axis. - Area of semi-circle: $A_{semi} = \frac{\pi R^2}{2}$ - Centroid distance from diameter axis: $\bar{y} = \frac{4R}{3\pi}$ - Volume by Theorem 2: $$V = 2\pi \bar{y} A_{semi} = 2\pi \left(\frac{4R}{3\pi}\right)\left(\frac{\pi R^2}{2}\right) = \frac{4}{3}\pi R^3$$

2. Volume of a Right Circular Cone: - Generating Area: A right-angled triangle with base radius $R$ and height $h$ rotating about the vertical axis $h$. - Area of triangle: $A = \frac{1}{2} R h$ - Centroid distance from the axis of rotation: $\bar{x} = \frac{R}{3}$ - Volume by Theorem 2: $$V = 2\pi \bar{x} A = 2\pi \left(\frac{R}{3}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2} R h\right) = \frac{1}{3}\pi R^2 h$$

The theorems of Pappus of Alexandria (circa 300 AD), rediscovered by Swiss mathematician Paul Guldin in 1640, provide elegant integral formulations relating the surface area and volume of bodies of revolution to the centroidal paths of their generating plane curves and plane areas.

For a complete revolution of $2\pi$ radians: $$V = 2\pi \bar{y} A$$ where $A$ is the generating plane area and $\bar{y}$ is the distance of the centroid of the area from the revolution axis.

Mathematical Proof of Theorem 2: Let a plane lamina of area $A$ lie in the $xy$-plane, and let the $x$-axis be the axis of revolution. Consider a differential area element $dA$ situated at vertical coordinate $y$. Define area moment of inertia, polar moment of inertia, and radius of gyration. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2 \quad (\text{Parallel Axis}), \quad I_{zz} = I_{xx} + I_{yy} \quad (\text{Perpendicular Axis})$$

Parallel Axis Theorem: $I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2$; first moment $\int y' dA = 0$ about centroid.
Perpendicular Axis Theorem: $I_{zz} = I_{xx} + I_{yy}$; valid strictly for planar 2D laminas.
Radius of Gyration: $k = \sqrt{I/A} \implies I = A k^2$.

⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2 \quad (\text{Parallel Axis}), \quad I_{zz} = I_{xx} + I_{yy} \quad (\text{Perpendicular Axis})$$

Parallel Axis Theorem: $I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2$; first moment $\int y' dA = 0$ about centroid.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Second Moment of Area, commonly termed the Area Moment of Inertia, is a fundamental geometric property of structural cross-sections that measures their resistance to bending deflection and torsional shearing stresses. For a planar cross-section of area $A$ in the $xy$-plane, the moments of inertia about the Cartesian axes are defined as: $$I_{xx} = \int_A y^2 \, dA \quad \text{and} \quad I_{yy} = \int_A x^2 \, dA$$ The Polar Moment of Inertia $J_z$ about the perpendicular $z$-axis passing through origin $O$ is: $$J_z = I_{zz} = \int_A r^2 \, dA = \int_A (x^2 + y^2)\, dA$$ The Radius of Gyration $k$ represents the distance from the axis at which the entire area could be concentrated without altering its moment of inertia: $$k_x = \sqrt{\frac{I_{xx}}{A}} \quad \text{and} \quad k_y = \sqrt{\frac{I_{yy}}{A}}$$

1. Parallel Axis Theorem (Steiner's Theorem): Statement: The moment of inertia of any plane area about an arbitrary axis $AB$ parallel to its centroidal axis is equal to the moment of inertia about the parallel centroidal axis plus the product of the area and the square of the perpendicular distance between the two axes. Mathematically: $$I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2$$ where $I_G$ is the moment of inertia about the centroidal axis passing through centroid $G$, $A$ is the total area, and $d$ is the perpendicular distance between the two parallel axes.

Mathematical Proof of Parallel Axis Theorem: Consider a plane area $A$. Let $G$ be the centroid of the area. Set up a centroidal coordinate system with its origin at $G$, having axes $x'$ and $y'$. Let an arbitrary parallel axis $AB$ lie at a perpendicular distance $d$ from the centroidal $x'$-axis. Consider an infinitesimal differential area element $dA$ located at coordinate $y'$ from the centroidal axis. Its distance from the parallel axis $AB$ is: $$y = y' + d$$ By definition, the moment of inertia of area $A$ about axis $AB$ is: $$I_{AB} = \int_A y^2 \, dA = \int_A (y' + d)^2 \, dA$$ Expanding the binomial term inside the integral: $$I_{AB} = \int_A \left[(y')^2 + 2d y' + d^2\right] dA = \int_A (y')^2 \, dA + 2d \int_A y' \, dA + d^2 \int_A dA$$ Analyzing the three individual integral terms: 1. First Term: $\int_A (y')^2 \, dA = I_G$ (the moment of inertia about the centroidal axis). 2. Second Term: The integral $\int_A y' \, dA$ represents the first moment of area about the centroidal axis $x'$. By definition of the centroid, the first moment of area about an axis passing through the centroid is identically zero: $$\int_A y' \, dA = A \bar{y}' = A(0) = 0$$ Therefore, the middle term $2d \int_A y' \, dA = 0$. 3. Third Term: $d^2 \int_A dA = A d^2$ (since $\int_A dA = A$). Substituting these evaluations: $$I_{AB} = I_G + 0 + A d^2 = I_G + A d^2$$ This proves the Parallel Axis Theorem. (Note: The theorem can only be applied directly between an axis and a centroidal axis; to transfer between two non-centroidal axes, one must first transfer back to the centroid).

2. Perpendicular Axis Theorem: Statement: For any plane lamina lying in the $xy$-plane, the moment of inertia about the perpendicular $z$-axis (polar axis normal to the plane) is equal to the sum of the moments of inertia about the two mutually perpendicular coplanar axes $x$ and $y$ intersecting at the same point. Mathematically: $$I_{zz} = I_{xx} + I_{yy} \quad \text{or} \quad J_O = I_x + I_y$$

Mathematical Proof of Perpendicular Axis Theorem: Let a thin plane lamina of area $A$ lie in the $xy$-plane. Let $O$ be the origin, with mutually perpendicular coplanar coordinate axes $x$ and $y$. Let the $z$-axis pass through $O$ perpendicular to the plane of the lamina. Consider a differential area element $dA$ with coordinates $(x, y)$. Its distance from the $x$-axis is $y$, so its moment of inertia about the $x$-axis is $dI_x = y^2 dA$. Its distance from the $y$-axis is $x$, so its moment of inertia about the $y$-axis is $dI_y = x^2 dA$. Its radial distance from the origin $O$ (and from the $z$-axis) is $r$. By the Pythagorean theorem: $$r^2 = x^2 + y^2$$ The moment of inertia of the element about the $z$-axis is: $$dI_{zz} = r^2 \, dA$$ Integrating across the entire area $A$: $$I_{zz} = \int_A r^2 \, dA = \int_A (x^2 + y^2) \, dA = \int_A x^2 \, dA + \int_A y^2 \, dA$$ Substituting the definitions $I_{yy} = \int x^2 dA$ and $I_{xx} = \int y^2 dA$: $$I_{zz} = I_{yy} + I_{xx} = I_{xx} + I_{yy}$$ This proves the Perpendicular Axis Theorem. (Crucial limitation: The theorem applies strictly to planar 2D figures; it cannot be applied to three-dimensional solids).

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

The Second Moment of Area, commonly termed the Area Moment of Inertia, is a fundamental geometric property of structural cross-sections that measures their resistance to bending deflection and torsional shearing stresses. For a planar cross-section of area $A$ in the $xy$-plane, the moments of inertia about the Cartesian axes are defined as: $$I_{xx} = \int_A y^2 \, dA \quad \text{and} \quad I_{yy} = \int_A x^2 \, dA$$ The Polar Moment of Inertia $J_z$ about the perpendicular $z$-axis passing through origin $O$ is: $$J_z = I_{zz} = \int_A r^2 \, dA = \int_A (x^2 + y^2)\, dA$$ The Radius of Gyration $k$ represents the distance from the axis at which the entire area could be concentrated without altering its moment of inertia: $$k_x = \sqrt{\frac{I_{xx}}{A}} \quad \text{and} \quad k_y = \sqrt{\frac{I_{yy}}{A}}$$

1. Parallel Axis Theorem (Steiner's Theorem): Statement: The moment of inertia of any plane area about an arbitrary axis $AB$ parallel to its centroidal axis is equal to the moment of inertia about the parallel centroidal axis plus the product of the area and the square of the perpendicular distance between the two axes. Mathematically: $$I_{AB} = I_G + A d^2$$ where $I_G$ is the moment of inertia about the centroidal axis passing through centroid $G$, $A$ is the total area, and $d$ is the perpendicular distance between the two parallel axes.

Mathematical Proof of Parallel Axis Theorem: Consider a plane area $A$. Let $G$ be the centroid of the area. Set up a centroidal coordinate system with its origin at $G$, having axes $x'$ and $y'$. Let an arbitrary parallel axis $AB$ lie at a perpendicular distance $d$ from the centroidal $x'$-axis. Consider an infinitesimal differential area element $dA$ located at coordinate $y'$ from the centroidal axis. Its distance from the parallel axis $AB$ is: $$y = y' + d$$ By definition, the moment of inertia of area $A$ about axis $AB$ is: $$I_{AB} = \int_A y^2 \, dA = \int_A (y' + d)^2 \, dA$$ Expanding the binomial term inside the integral: $$I_{AB} = \int_A \left[(y')^2 + 2d y' + d^2\right] dA = \int_A (y')^2 \, dA + 2d \int_A y' \, dA + d^2 \int_A dA$$ Analyzing the three individual integral terms: 1. First Term: $\int_A (y')^2 \, dA = I_G$ (the moment of inertia about the centroidal axis). 2. Second Term: The integral $\int_A y' \, dA$ represents the first moment of area about the centroidal axis $x'$. By definition of the centroid, the first moment of area about an axis passing through the centroid is identically zero: $$\int_A y' \, dA = A \bar{y}' = A(0) = 0$$ Therefore, the middle term $2d \int_A y' \, dA = 0$. 3. Third Term: $d^2 \int_A dA = A d^2$ (since $\int_A dA = A$). Substituting these evaluations: $$I_{AB} = I_G + 0 + A d^2 = I_G + A d^2$$ This proves the Parallel Axis Theorem. (Note: The theorem can only be applied directly between an axis and a centroidal axis; to transfer between two non-centroidal axes, one must first transfer back to the centroid).

2. Perpendicular Axis Theorem: Statement: For any plane lamina lying in the $xy$-plane, the moment of inertia about the perpendicular $z$-axis (polar axis normal to the plane) is equal to the sum of the moments of inertia about the two mutually perpendicular coplanar axes $x$ and $y$ intersecting at the same point. Mathematically: $$I_{zz} = I_{xx} + I_{yy} \quad \text{or} \quad J_O = I_x + I_y$$

Mathematical Proof of Perpendicular Axis Theorem: Let a thin plane lamina of area $A$ lie in the $xy$-plane. Let $O$ be the origin, with mutually perpendicular coplanar coordinate axes $x$ and $y$. Let the $z$-axis pass through $O$ perpendicular to the plane of the lamina. Consider a differential area element $dA$ with coordinates $(x, y)$. Its distance from the $x$-axis is $y$, so its moment of inertia about the $x$-axis is $dI_x = y^2 dA$. Its distance from the $y$-axis is $x$, so its moment of inertia about the $y$-axis is $dI_y = x^2 dA$. Its radial distance from the origin $O$ (and from the $z$-axis) is $r$. By the Pythagorean theorem: $$r^2 = x^2 + y^2$$ The moment of inertia of the element about the $z$-axis is: $$dI_{zz} = r^2 \, dA$$ Integrating across the entire area $A$: $$I_{zz} = \int_A r^2 \, dA = \int_A (x^2 + y^2) \, dA = \int_A x^2 \, dA + \int_A y^2 \, dA$$ Substituting the definitions $I_{yy} = \int x^2 dA$ and $I_{xx} = \int y^2 dA$: $$I_{zz} = I_{yy} + I_{xx} = I_{xx} + I_{yy}$$ This proves the Perpendicular Axis Theorem. (Crucial limitation: The theorem applies strictly to planar 2D figures; it cannot be applied to three-dimensional solids).

Theory & Application ID: MECH-Q14

Determine the centroidal moments of inertia Ixx and Iyy and the radii of gyration kxx and kyy for an unsymmetrical I-section with given flange and web dimensions. Determine the centroidal moments of inertia Ixx and Iyy and the radii of gyration kxx and kyy for an unsymmetrical I-section with given flange and web dimensions. Determine the centroidal moments of inertia Ixx and Iyy and the radii of gyration kxx and kyy for an unsymmetrical I-section with given flange and web dimensions.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{xx} = \sum \left( \frac{b_i h_i^3}{12} + A_i d_i^2 \right) \approx 54.82 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4, \quad I_{yy} = \sum \frac{h_i b_i^3}{12} \approx 16.26 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4$$

I-Section Centroidal MOI: Calculate $\bar{y}$ using first moments, then apply parallel axis theorem to each rectangle: $I_{xx} = I_G + A d^2$.
Symmetry about Y-Axis: All $d_x = 0 \implies I_{yy} = \sum \frac{h b^3}{12}$.
Radii of Gyration: $k_{xx} = \sqrt{I_{xx}/A} \approx 78.93\text{ mm}$, $k_{yy} = \sqrt{I_{yy}/A} \approx 42.98\text{ mm}$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Determining the second moment of area (moment of inertia) of structural beams is essential in structural engineering for evaluating bending stresses ($\sigma = \frac{M y}{I}$) and deflection profiles.

Problem Statement: Determine the centroidal moments of inertia $I_{xx}$ and $I_{yy}$ and the radii of gyration $k_{xx}$ and $k_{yy}$ for an unsymmetrical I-section with the following dimensions: - Top Flange: Width $b_1 = 120\text{ mm}$, Thickness $t_1 = 20\text{ mm}$ - Web: Thickness $t_2 = 15\text{ mm}$, Height $h_2 = 160\text{ mm}$ - Bottom Flange: Width $b_3 = 200\text{ mm}$, Thickness $t_3 = 20\text{ mm}$

Step 1: Locating the Centroid of the Composite Section: Set the origin at the bottom-left corner of the bottom flange. Because the cross-section is symmetric about the vertical centerline: $$\bar{x} = \frac{b_3}{2} = \frac{200}{2} = 100\text{ mm}$$ The vertical centroidal axis $y-y$ coincides with this line of symmetry.

Now calculate the vertical centroid coordinate $\bar{y}$ from the bottom edge: - Rectangle 1 (Bottom Flange): - Area: $A_1 = 200 \times 20 = 4000\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_1 = \frac{20}{2} = 10\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_1 y_1 = 4000 \times 10 = 40,000\text{ mm}^3$ - Rectangle 2 (Web): - Area: $A_2 = 15 \times 160 = 2400\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_2 = 20 + \frac{160}{2} = 20 + 80 = 100\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_2 y_2 = 2400 \times 100 = 240,000\text{ mm}^3$ - Rectangle 3 (Top Flange): - Area: $A_3 = 120 \times 20 = 2400\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_3 = 20 + 160 + \frac{20}{2} = 180 + 10 = 190\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_3 y_3 = 2400 \times 190 = 456,000\text{ mm}^3$

Total Area: $$A_{total} = A_1 + A_2 + A_3 = 4000 + 2400 + 2400 = 8800\text{ mm}^2$$ Total First Moment: $$\Sigma A_i y_i = 40,000 + 240,000 + 456,000 = 736,000\text{ mm}^3$$ Centroid height from base: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\Sigma A_i y_i}{A_{total}} = \frac{736,000}{8800} \approx 83.64\text{ mm}$$

Step 2: Calculating Centroidal Moment of Inertia $I_{xx}$: Applying the Parallel Axis Theorem $I_{xx, i} = I_{Gi} + A_i d_i^2$, where $I_{Gi} = \frac{b_i h_i^3}{12}$ and $d_i = |y_i - \bar{y}|$: - For Bottom Flange (Shape 1): $$I_{G1} = \frac{200 \times 20^3}{12} = \frac{200 \times 8000}{12} \approx 133,333.33\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_1 = |10 - 83.64| = 73.64\text{ mm}$$ $$A_1 d_1^2 = 4000 \times (73.64)^2 = 4000 \times 5422.85 \approx 21,691,400\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 1} = 133,333 + 21,691,400 = 21,824,733\text{ mm}^4$$

- For Web (Shape 2): $$I_{G2} = \frac{15 \times 160^3}{12} = \frac{15 \times 4,096,000}{12} = 5,120,000\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_2 = |100 - 83.64| = 16.36\text{ mm}$$ $$A_2 d_2^2 = 2400 \times (16.36)^2 = 2400 \times 267.65 \approx 642,360\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 2} = 5,120,000 + 642,360 = 5,762,360\text{ mm}^4$$

- For Top Flange (Shape 3): $$I_{G3} = \frac{120 \times 20^3}{12} = \frac{120 \times 8000}{12} = 80,000\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_3 = |190 - 83.64| = 106.36\text{ mm}$$ $$A_3 d_3^2 = 2400 \times (106.36)^2 = 2400 \times 11312.45 \approx 27,149,880\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 3} = 80,000 + 27,149,880 = 27,229,880\text{ mm}^4$$

Total Moment of Inertia about horizontal centroidal axis $x-x$: $$I_{xx} = I_{xx, 1} + I_{xx, 2} + I_{xx, 3} = 21,824,733 + 5,762,360 + 27,229,880 \approx 54.82 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4$$

Step 3: Calculating Centroidal Moment of Inertia $I_{yy}$: Because the vertical centerline is the common axis of symmetry for all three rectangles, all centroidal shifts in the $x$-direction are zero ($d_{xi} = 0$). Hence, no parallel axis term is needed: $$I_{yy} = \sum \frac{h_i b_i^3}{12}$$ - Bottom Flange: $I_{yy, 1} = \frac{20 \times 200^3}{12} = \frac{20 \times 8,000,000}{12} \approx 13,333,333\text{ mm}^4$ - Web: $I_{yy, 2} = \frac{160 \times 15^3}{12} = \frac{160 \times 3375}{12} = 45,000\text{ mm}^4$ - Top Flange: $I_{yy, 3} = \frac{20 \times 120^3}{12} = \frac{20 \times 1,728,000}{12} = 2,880,000\text{ mm}^4$ $$I_{yy} = 13,333,333 + 45,000 + 2,880,000 \approx 16.26 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4$$

Step 4: Calculating Radii of Gyration: $$k_{xx} = \sqrt{\frac{I_{xx}}{A}} = \sqrt{\frac{54.82 \times 10^6}{8800}} = \sqrt{6229.5} \approx 78.93\text{ mm}$$ $$k_{yy} = \sqrt{\frac{I_{yy}}{A}} = \sqrt{\frac{16.26 \times 10^6}{8800}} = \sqrt{1847.7} \approx 42.98\text{ mm}$$

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Determining the second moment of area (moment of inertia) of structural beams is essential in structural engineering for evaluating bending stresses ($\sigma = \frac{M y}{I}$) and deflection profiles.

Problem Statement: Determine the centroidal moments of inertia $I_{xx}$ and $I_{yy}$ and the radii of gyration $k_{xx}$ and $k_{yy}$ for an unsymmetrical I-section with the following dimensions: - Top Flange: Width $b_1 = 120\text{ mm}$, Thickness $t_1 = 20\text{ mm}$ - Web: Thickness $t_2 = 15\text{ mm}$, Height $h_2 = 160\text{ mm}$ - Bottom Flange: Width $b_3 = 200\text{ mm}$, Thickness $t_3 = 20\text{ mm}$

Step 1: Locating the Centroid of the Composite Section: Set the origin at the bottom-left corner of the bottom flange. Because the cross-section is symmetric about the vertical centerline: $$\bar{x} = \frac{b_3}{2} = \frac{200}{2} = 100\text{ mm}$$ The vertical centroidal axis $y-y$ coincides with this line of symmetry.

Now calculate the vertical centroid coordinate $\bar{y}$ from the bottom edge: - Rectangle 1 (Bottom Flange): - Area: $A_1 = 200 \times 20 = 4000\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_1 = \frac{20}{2} = 10\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_1 y_1 = 4000 \times 10 = 40,000\text{ mm}^3$ - Rectangle 2 (Web): - Area: $A_2 = 15 \times 160 = 2400\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_2 = 20 + \frac{160}{2} = 20 + 80 = 100\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_2 y_2 = 2400 \times 100 = 240,000\text{ mm}^3$ - Rectangle 3 (Top Flange): - Area: $A_3 = 120 \times 20 = 2400\text{ mm}^2$ - Centroid height: $y_3 = 20 + 160 + \frac{20}{2} = 180 + 10 = 190\text{ mm}$ - First moment: $A_3 y_3 = 2400 \times 190 = 456,000\text{ mm}^3$

Total Area: $$A_{total} = A_1 + A_2 + A_3 = 4000 + 2400 + 2400 = 8800\text{ mm}^2$$ Total First Moment: $$\Sigma A_i y_i = 40,000 + 240,000 + 456,000 = 736,000\text{ mm}^3$$ Centroid height from base: $$\bar{y} = \frac{\Sigma A_i y_i}{A_{total}} = \frac{736,000}{8800} \approx 83.64\text{ mm}$$

Step 2: Calculating Centroidal Moment of Inertia $I_{xx}$: Applying the Parallel Axis Theorem $I_{xx, i} = I_{Gi} + A_i d_i^2$, where $I_{Gi} = \frac{b_i h_i^3}{12}$ and $d_i = |y_i - \bar{y}|$: - For Bottom Flange (Shape 1): $$I_{G1} = \frac{200 \times 20^3}{12} = \frac{200 \times 8000}{12} \approx 133,333.33\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_1 = |10 - 83.64| = 73.64\text{ mm}$$ $$A_1 d_1^2 = 4000 \times (73.64)^2 = 4000 \times 5422.85 \approx 21,691,400\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 1} = 133,333 + 21,691,400 = 21,824,733\text{ mm}^4$$

- For Web (Shape 2): $$I_{G2} = \frac{15 \times 160^3}{12} = \frac{15 \times 4,096,000}{12} = 5,120,000\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_2 = |100 - 83.64| = 16.36\text{ mm}$$ $$A_2 d_2^2 = 2400 \times (16.36)^2 = 2400 \times 267.65 \approx 642,360\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 2} = 5,120,000 + 642,360 = 5,762,360\text{ mm}^4$$

- For Top Flange (Shape 3): $$I_{G3} = \frac{120 \times 20^3}{12} = \frac{120 \times 8000}{12} = 80,000\text{ mm}^4$$ $$d_3 = |190 - 83.64| = 106.36\text{ mm}$$ $$A_3 d_3^2 = 2400 \times (106.36)^2 = 2400 \times 11312.45 \approx 27,149,880\text{ mm}^4$$ $$I_{xx, 3} = 80,000 + 27,149,880 = 27,229,880\text{ mm}^4$$

Total Moment of Inertia about horizontal centroidal axis $x-x$: $$I_{xx} = I_{xx, 1} + I_{xx, 2} + I_{xx, 3} = 21,824,733 + 5,762,360 + 27,229,880 \approx 54.82 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4$$

Step 3: Calculating Centroidal Moment of Inertia $I_{yy}$: Because the vertical centerline is the common axis of symmetry for all three rectangles, all centroidal shifts in the $x$-direction are zero ($d_{xi} = 0$). Hence, no parallel axis term is needed: $$I_{yy} = \sum \frac{h_i b_i^3}{12}$$ - Bottom Flange: $I_{yy, 1} = \frac{20 \times 200^3}{12} = \frac{20 \times 8,000,000}{12} \approx 13,333,333\text{ mm}^4$ - Web: $I_{yy, 2} = \frac{160 \times 15^3}{12} = \frac{160 \times 3375}{12} = 45,000\text{ mm}^4$ - Top Flange: $I_{yy, 3} = \frac{20 \times 120^3}{12} = \frac{20 \times 1,728,000}{12} = 2,880,000\text{ mm}^4$ $$I_{yy} = 13,333,333 + 45,000 + 2,880,000 \approx 16.26 \times 10^6\text{ mm}^4$$

Step 4: Calculating Radii of Gyration: $$k_{xx} = \sqrt{\frac{I_{xx}}{A}} = \sqrt{\frac{54.82 \times 10^6}{8800}} = \sqrt{6229.5} \approx 78.93\text{ mm}$$ $$k_{yy} = \sqrt{\frac{I_{yy}}{A}} = \sqrt{\frac{16.26 \times 10^6}{8800}} = \sqrt{1847.7} \approx 42.98\text{ mm}$$

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 3.4: Cross-Sectional Geometry and Centroidal Axes of an Unsymmetric I-Beam — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: MECH-Q15

Define mass moment of inertia and radius of gyration. Derive the mass moment of inertia of a uniform slender rod of mass m and length L about its centroidal transverse axis and one end, and of a thin circular disc about its polar and diameter axes. Define mass moment of inertia and radius of gyration. Derive the mass moment of inertia of a uniform slender rod of mass m and length L about its centroidal transverse axis and one end, and of a thin circular disc about its polar and diameter axes. Define mass moment of inertia and radius of gyration. Derive the mass moment of inertia of a uniform slender rod of mass m and length L about its centroidal transverse axis and one end, and of a thin circular disc about its polar and diameter axes.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$I_{\text{rod}, G} = \frac{1}{12} m L^2, \quad I_{\text{rod}, \text{end}} = \frac{1}{3} m L^2, \quad I_{\text{disc}, z} = \frac{1}{2} m R^2, \quad I_{\text{disc}, d} = \frac{1}{4} m R^2$$

Mass Moment of Inertia: $I = \int r^2 dm$, measures resistance to angular acceleration ($T = I \alpha$).
Slender Rod: Centroidal transverse axis $I_G = \frac{1}{12} m L^2$; about one end $I = I_G + m(L/2)^2 = \frac{1}{3} m L^2$.
Circular Disc: Polar axis $I_z = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$; diameter axis $I_d = \frac{1}{4} m R^2$ (perpendicular axis theorem).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Mass Moment of Inertia of a rigid body is the rotational analogue of inertial mass in linear motion. While mass $m$ quantifies a body's resistance to linear acceleration ($F = ma$), mass moment of inertia $I$ measures its resistance to rotational angular acceleration about a specified rotational axis ($T = I \alpha$). Mathematically, for a rigid body of continuous mass distribution, the mass moment of inertia about an axis is defined as: $$I = \int r^2 \, dm$$ where $dm$ is a differential mass element and $r$ is the perpendicular distance of the element from the axis of rotation. The Mass Radius of Gyration $k$ is given by: $$k = \sqrt{\frac{I}{m}} \implies I = m k^2$$ The Parallel Axis Theorem for mass states that the mass moment of inertia about any axis parallel to a centroidal axis is: $$I = I_G + m d^2$$ where $I_G$ is the moment of inertia about the parallel centroidal axis, $m$ is total mass, and $d$ is the perpendicular distance between axes.

Derivation 1: Mass Moment of Inertia of a Uniform Slender Rod: Consider a straight uniform slender rod of total mass $m$, total length $L$, and uniform linear mass density $\lambda = \frac{m}{L}$. We assume the cross-sectional dimensions of the rod are negligibly small compared to its length.

1. Moment of Inertia About Centroidal Transverse Axis ($y$-axis passing through center $G$): Place the origin $O$ at the center of mass $G$ of the rod, so the rod extends from $x = -L/2$ to $x = +L/2$. Consider a differential element of length $dx$ situated at distance $x$ from the center. The mass of this differential element is: $$dm = \lambda \, dx = \left(\frac{m}{L}\right) dx$$ Its perpendicular distance from the transverse centroidal $y$-axis is $x$. The differential mass moment of inertia is: $$dI_G = x^2 \, dm = x^2 \left(\frac{m}{L}\right) dx$$ Integrating over the entire length of the rod from $-L/2$ to $+L/2$: $$I_G = \frac{m}{L} \int_{-L/2}^{L/2} x^2 \, dx = \frac{m}{L} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_{-L/2}^{L/2} = \frac{m}{3L} \left[ \left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^3 - \left(-\frac{L}{2}\right)^3 \right] = \frac{m}{3L} \left[ \frac{L^3}{8} - \left(-\frac{L^3}{8}\right) \right]$$ $$I_G = \frac{m}{3L} \left(\frac{2L^3}{8}\right) = \frac{m}{3L} \left(\frac{L^3}{4}\right) = \frac{m L^2}{12}$$ Therefore, the centroidal mass moment of inertia of a uniform slender rod is: $$I_G = \frac{1}{12} m L^2$$

2. Moment of Inertia About an Axis Passing Through One End: To find the moment of inertia $I_{end}$ about an axis passing through one end of the rod perpendicular to its length, we apply the Parallel Axis Theorem. The distance between the centroid $G$ and the end of the rod is $d = L/2$: $$I_{end} = I_G + m d^2 = \frac{1}{12} m L^2 + m \left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{12} m L^2 + \frac{1}{4} m L^2 = \left(\frac{1}{12} + \frac{3}{12}\right) m L^2 = \frac{4}{12} m L^2$$ $$I_{end} = \frac{1}{3} m L^2$$ The moment of inertia about one end is four times larger than about the center.

Derivation 2: Mass Moment of Inertia of a Thin Circular Disc: Consider a thin, uniform circular disc of mass $m$, radius $R$, and uniform surface mass density $\sigma = \frac{m}{\pi R^2}$.

1. Moment of Inertia About Central Perpendicular Axis ($z$-axis): Divide the disc into concentric circular rings. Consider an elemental ring of radius $r$ and radial width $dr$. The surface area of the ring is $dA = 2\pi r \, dr$. The mass of the elemental ring is: $$dm = \sigma \, dA = \left(\frac{m}{\pi R^2}\right) (2\pi r \, dr) = \frac{2m}{R^2} r \, dr$$ Because all points on this ring are at the identical radial distance $r$ from the central perpendicular $z$-axis, its moment of inertia is: $$dI_z = r^2 \, dm = r^2 \left( \frac{2m}{R^2} r \, dr \right) = \frac{2m}{R^2} r^3 \, dr$$ Integrating over the entire disc from $r = 0$ to $r = R$: $$I_z = \frac{2m}{R^2} \int_0^R r^3 \, dr = \frac{2m}{R^2} \left[ \frac{r^4}{4} \right]_0^R = \frac{2m}{R^2} \left(\frac{R^4}{4}\right) = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$$ Thus, for a thin uniform circular disc about its polar axis: $$I_z = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$$

2. Moment of Inertia About a Diametral Axis ($x$ or $y$ axis): Because the disc is planar and thin, we apply the Perpendicular Axis Theorem: $$I_z = I_x + I_y$$ By rotational symmetry, the moments of inertia about all diameter axes are identical: $I_x = I_y = I_d$. $$I_z = I_d + I_d = 2 I_d \implies I_d = \frac{I_z}{2}$$ Substituting $I_z = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$: $$I_d = \frac{1}{4} m R^2$$ This gives the mass moment of inertia of a circular disc about any diameter in its plane.

The Mass Moment of Inertia of a rigid body is the rotational analogue of inertial mass in linear motion. While mass $m$ quantifies a body's resistance to linear acceleration ($F = ma$), mass moment of inertia $I$ measures its resistance to rotational angular acceleration about a specified rotational axis ($T = I \alpha$). Mathematically, for a rigid body of continuous mass distribution, the mass moment of inertia about an axis is defined as: $$I = \int r^2 \, dm$$ where $dm$ is a differential mass element and $r$ is the perpendicular distance of the element from the axis of rotation. The Mass Radius of Gyration $k$ is given by: $$k = \sqrt{\frac{I}{m}} \implies I = m k^2$$ The Parallel Axis Theorem for mass states that the mass moment of inertia about any axis parallel to a centroidal axis is: $$I = I_G + m d^2$$ where $I_G$ is the moment of inertia about the parallel centroidal axis, $m$ is total mass, and $d$ is the perpendicular distance between axes.

Consider a differential element of length $dx$ situated at distance $x$ from the center. The mass of this differential element is: $$dm = \lambda \, dx = \left(\frac{m}{L}\right) dx$$ Its perpendicular distance from the transverse centroidal $y$-axis is $x$. The differential mass moment of inertia is: $$dI_G = x^2 \, dm = x^2 \left(\frac{m}{L}\right) dx$$ Integrating over the entire length of the rod from $-L/2$ to $+L/2$: $$I_G = \frac{m}{L} \int_{-L/2}^{L/2} x^2 \, dx = \frac{m}{L} \left[ \frac{x^3}{3} \right]_{-L/2}^{L/2} = \frac{m}{3L} \left[ \left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^3 - \left(-\frac{L}{2}\right)^3 \right] = \frac{m}{3L} \left[ \frac{L^3}{8} - \left(-\frac{L^3}{8}\right) \right]$$ $$I_G = \frac{m}{3L} \left(\frac{2L^3}{8}\right) = \frac{m}{3L} \left(\frac{L^3}{4}\right) = \frac{m L^2}{12}$$ Therefore, the centroidal mass moment of inertia of a uniform slender rod is: $$I_G = \frac{1}{12} m L^2$$

2. Moment of Inertia About an Axis Passing Through One End: To find the moment of inertia $I_{end}$ about an axis passing through one end of the rod perpendicular to its length, we apply the Parallel Axis Theorem. The distance between the centroid $G$ and the end of the rod is $d = L/2$: $$I_{end} = I_G + m d^2 = \frac{1}{12} m L^2 + m \left(\frac{L}{2}\right)^2 = \frac{1}{12} m L^2 + \frac{1}{4} m L^2 = \left(\frac{1}{12} + \frac{3}{12}\right) m L^2 = \frac{4}{12} m L^2$$ $$I_{end} = \frac{1}{3} m L^2$$ The moment of inertia about one end is four times larger than about the center.

Derivation 2: Mass Moment of Inertia of a Thin Circular Disc: Consider a thin, uniform circular disc of mass $m$, radius $R$, and uniform surface mass density $\sigma = \frac{m}{\pi R^2}$.

1. Moment of Inertia About Central Perpendicular Axis ($z$-axis): Divide the disc into concentric circular rings. Consider an elemental ring of radius $r$ and radial width $dr$. The surface area of the ring is $dA = 2\pi r \, dr$. The mass of the elemental ring is: $$dm = \sigma \, dA = \left(\frac{m}{\pi R^2}\right) (2\pi r \, dr) = \frac{2m}{R^2} r \, dr$$ Because all points on this ring are at the identical radial distance $r$ from the central perpendicular $z$-axis, its moment of inertia is: $$dI_z = r^2 \, dm = r^2 \left( \frac{2m}{R^2} r \, dr \right) = \frac{2m}{R^2} r^3 \, dr$$ Integrating over the entire disc from $r = 0$ to $r = R$: $$I_z = \frac{2m}{R^2} \int_0^R r^3 \, dr = \frac{2m}{R^2} \left[ \frac{r^4}{4} \right]_0^R = \frac{2m}{R^2} \left(\frac{R^4}{4}\right) = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$$ Thus, for a thin uniform circular disc about its polar axis: $$I_z = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$$

2. Moment of Inertia About a Diametral Axis ($x$ or $y$ axis): Because the disc is planar and thin, we apply the Perpendicular Axis Theorem: $$I_z = I_x + I_y$$ By rotational symmetry, the moments of inertia about all diameter axes are identical: $I_x = I_y = I_d$. $$I_z = I_d + I_d = 2 I_d \implies I_d = \frac{I_z}{2}$$ Substituting $I_z = \frac{1}{2} m R^2$: $$I_d = \frac{1}{4} m R^2$$ This gives the mass moment of inertia of a circular disc about any diameter in its plane.
