Module 4

Kinematics and Kinetics of Particles, D'Alembert's Principle

5 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation & Analytical ID: MECH-Q16

Formulate the kinematic equations for rectilinear motion with variable acceleration. Solve the equation of motion for a particle subjected to a resisting drag force proportional to velocity (a = -kv) and determine the velocity, displacement, and total distance traveled. Formulate the kinematic equations for rectilinear motion with variable acceleration. Solve the equation of motion for a particle subjected to a resisting drag force proportional to velocity (a = -kv) and determine the velocity, displacement, and total distance traveled. Formulate the kinematic equations for rectilinear motion with variable acceleration. Solve the equation of motion for a particle subjected to a resisting drag force proportional to velocity (a = -kv) and determine the velocity, displacement, and total distance traveled.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$a = \frac{dv}{dt} = v \frac{dv}{ds}, \quad a = -k v \implies v(t) = v_0 e^{-k t}, \quad s(t) = \frac{v_0}{k}(1 - e^{-k t}), \quad s_{max} = \frac{v_0}{k}$$

Variable Acceleration: Use $v = ds/dt$, $a = dv/dt$, or $v \, dv = a \, ds$ based on independent variables.
Linear Drag ($a = -kv$): Velocity decays exponentially $v = v_0 e^{-kt}$.
Stopping Distance: Particle comes to rest asymptotically with total distance $s_{max} = v_0/k$.

$$a = \frac{dv}{dt} = v \frac{dv}{ds}, \quad a = -k v \implies v(t) = v_0 e^{-k t}, \quad s(t) = \frac{v_0}{k}(1 - e^{-k t}), \quad s_{max} = \frac{v_0}{k}$$

Variable Acceleration: Use $v = ds/dt$, $a = dv/dt$, or $v \, dv = a \, ds$ based on independent variables.
Linear Drag ($a = -kv$): Velocity decays exponentially $v = v_0 e^{-kt}$.
Stopping Distance: Particle comes to rest asymptotically with total distance $s_{max} = v_0/k$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Rectilinear motion describes the one-dimensional translation of a particle along a straight line path. When acceleration is constant, motion is governed by standard algebraic kinematic equations ($v = u + at, s = ut + \frac{1}{2}at^2, v^2 = u^2 + 2as$). However, in most engineering situations—such as aerodynamic drag, vehicle braking, fluid viscosity resistance, or spring-mass dynamics—acceleration varies continuously as a function of time $t$, velocity $v$, or displacement $s$.

Fundamental Differential Kinematic Equations: The position coordinate of a particle along a straight line is $s(t)$. The instantaneous velocity $v$ is the first time derivative of displacement: $$v = \frac{ds}{dt} \implies ds = v \, dt$$ The instantaneous acceleration $a$ is the time derivative of velocity: $$a = \frac{dv}{dt} = \frac{d^2 s}{dt^2} \implies dv = a \, dt$$ By applying the chain rule of differentiation, time $t$ can be eliminated to link acceleration directly to displacement: $$a = \frac{dv}{dt} = \frac{dv}{ds} \cdot \frac{ds}{dt} = v \frac{dv}{ds} \implies v \, dv = a \, ds$$

Analytical Solutions for Specific Functional Forms of Acceleration: 1. Case 1: Acceleration as a Function of Time, $a = f(t)$: Separating variables in $dv = a \, dt$: $$\int_{v_0}^v dv = \int_0^t f(t) \, dt \implies v(t) = v_0 + \int_0^t f(t) \, dt$$ Once $v(t)$ is obtained, displacement is integrated: $$\int_{s_0}^s ds = \int_0^t v(t) \, dt \implies s(t) = s_0 + \int_0^t v(t) \, dt$$

2. Case 2: Acceleration as a Function of Displacement, $a = f(s)$: Separating variables in $v \, dv = a \, ds$: $$\int_{v_0}^v v \, dv = \int_{s_0}^s f(s) \, ds \implies \frac{v^2 - v_0^2}{2} = \int_{s_0}^s f(s) \, ds \implies v(s) = \sqrt{v_0^2 + 2\int_{s_0}^s f(s) \, ds}$$ Time is then obtained by integrating $dt = \frac{ds}{v(s)}$: $$t = \int_{s_0}^s \frac{ds}{v(s)}$$

3. Case 3: Acceleration as a Function of Velocity (Resisted Fluid Motion), $a = -k v$: Consider a particle launched into a viscous fluid with initial velocity $v_0$ at $t = 0, s = 0$. The fluid exerts a resisting drag force proportional to velocity, resulting in deceleration: $$a = \frac{dv}{dt} = -k v$$ where $k$ is a positive constant of drag per unit mass ($s^{-1}$). - Velocity as a Function of Time: Separating variables: $$\frac{dv}{v} = -k \, dt$$ Integrating from initial velocity $v_0$ at $t = 0$ to velocity $v$ at time $t$: $$\int_{v_0}^v \frac{dv}{v} = -k \int_0^t dt \implies \left[ \ln v \right]_{v_0}^v = -k t \implies \ln\left(\frac{v}{v_0}\right) = -k t$$ Exponentiating both sides: $$v(t) = v_0 e^{-k t}$$ The velocity decays exponentially with time, approaching zero asymptotically as $t \to \infty$.

- Displacement as a Function of Time: Substituting $v = \frac{ds}{dt}$: $$\frac{ds}{dt} = v_0 e^{-k t} \implies ds = v_0 e^{-k t} \, dt$$ Integrating with initial condition $s(0) = 0$: $$\int_0^s ds = v_0 \int_0^t e^{-k t} \, dt \implies s(t) = v_0 \left[ \frac{e^{-k t}}{-k} \right]_0^t = \frac{v_0}{k} \left(1 - e^{-k t}\right)$$ - Maximum Distance Traveled Before Coming to Rest: As time approaches infinity ($t \to \infty$), $e^{-k t} \to 0$. The total stopping distance $s_{max}$ is finite: $$s_{max} = \lim_{t \to \infty} \frac{v_0}{k}\left(1 - e^{-k t}\right) = \frac{v_0}{k}$$ Alternatively, using $a = v \frac{dv}{ds} = -kv \implies dv = -k \, ds$. Integrating from $v_0$ to $0$ gives $0 - v_0 = -k s_{max} \implies s_{max} = \frac{v_0}{k}$. This demonstrates the consistency of the differential approach.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: MECH-Q17

Derive the parabolic trajectory equation for an unresisted projectile launched with initial velocity u at angle theta. Derive expressions for the time of flight, maximum vertical height, and horizontal range, and obtain the condition for maximum range. Derive the parabolic trajectory equation for an unresisted projectile launched with initial velocity u at angle theta. Derive expressions for the time of flight, maximum vertical height, and horizontal range, and obtain the condition for maximum range. Derive the parabolic trajectory equation for an unresisted projectile launched with initial velocity u at angle theta. Derive expressions for the time of flight, maximum vertical height, and horizontal range, and obtain the condition for maximum range.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$y = x \tan\theta - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta}, \quad T = \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g}, \quad H = \frac{u^2 \sin^2\theta}{2g}, \quad R = \frac{u^2 \sin(2\theta)}{g}$$

Parabolic Trajectory: Eliminating time $t$ gives $y = x\tan\theta - \frac{gx^2}{2u^2\cos^2\theta}$.
Time of Flight: $T = \frac{2u\sin\theta}{g}$ ($y=0$).
Max Height: $H = \frac{u^2\sin^2\theta}{2g}$ ($v_y=0$).
Horizontal Range: $R = \frac{u^2\sin 2\theta}{g}$; maximum range occurs at $\theta = 45^\circ$ ($R_{max} = u^2/g$).

$$y = x \tan\theta - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta}, \quad T = \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g}, \quad H = \frac{u^2 \sin^2\theta}{2g}, \quad R = \frac{u^2 \sin(2\theta)}{g}$$

Parabolic Trajectory: Eliminating time $t$ gives $y = x\tan\theta - \frac{gx^2}{2u^2\cos^2\theta}$.
Time of Flight: $T = \frac{2u\sin\theta}{g}$ ($y=0$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A projectile is any object projected into the atmosphere with an initial velocity that continues in motion under the sole influence of gravity (assuming air resistance is negligible). Because gravity acts purely in the vertical downward direction, the horizontal and vertical motions are independent.

Kinematic Formulation and Coordinate Setup: Let a particle be projected from origin $O(0,0)$ with an initial velocity $u$ at an angle of projection $\theta$ with the horizontal $x$-axis. Resolving initial velocity $u$ into Cartesian components: - Initial horizontal velocity: $u_x = u \cos\theta$ - Initial vertical velocity: $u_y = u \sin\theta$

Governing Equations of Motion: 1. Horizontal Motion: Zero horizontal acceleration ($a_x = 0$): The horizontal velocity remains constant throughout the flight: $$v_x(t) = u_x = u \cos\theta$$ The horizontal displacement at any time $t$ is: $$x(t) = (u \cos\theta) t \implies t = \frac{x}{u \cos\theta} \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ 2. Vertical Motion: Downward gravitational acceleration ($a_y = -g$): The vertical velocity at any time $t$ is: $$v_y(t) = u \sin\theta - g t$$ The vertical displacement at time $t$ is: $$y(t) = (u \sin\theta) t - \frac{1}{2} g t^2 \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

1. Derivation of the Equation of the Path (Trajectory): To determine the geometric path, we eliminate time parameter $t$ by substituting Equation 1 into Equation 2: $$y = (u \sin\theta) \left( \frac{x}{u \cos\theta} \right) - \frac{1}{2} g \left( \frac{x}{u \cos\theta} \right)^2$$ $$y = x \left(\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\right) - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta} \implies y = x \tan\theta - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta}$$ This equation is of the standard quadratic form $y = A x - B x^2$ (where $A = \tan\theta$ and $B = \frac{g}{2u^2 \cos^2\theta}$ are constants). This proves that the trajectory of an unresisted projectile is a parabola with its vertex pointing upward.

2. Derivation of Time of Flight ($T$): The total time of flight $T$ is the total elapsed time from projection until the projectile strikes the horizontal ground plane again ($y = 0$). Setting $y = 0$ in the vertical displacement equation: $$0 = (u \sin\theta) T - \frac{1}{2} g T^2 \implies T \left( u \sin\theta - \frac{1}{2} g T \right) = 0$$ Since $T \neq 0$ (which corresponds to launch): $$u \sin\theta - \frac{1}{2} g T = 0 \implies \frac{1}{2} g T = u \sin\theta$$ $$T = \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g}$$ The time to reach the maximum height $t_h$ (where vertical velocity vanishes, $v_y = 0 \implies u \sin\theta - g t_h = 0$) is exactly half the total time of flight: $$t_h = \frac{u \sin\theta}{g} = \frac{T}{2}$$

3. Derivation of Maximum Vertical Height ($H$): At the peak apex of the trajectory ($t = t_h = \frac{u \sin\theta}{g}$), the vertical displacement reaches its maximum value $H$. Using the kinematic relation $v_y^2 = u_y^2 - 2g H$ with $v_y = 0$: $$0 = (u \sin\theta)^2 - 2g H \implies 2g H = u^2 \sin^2\theta$$ $$H = \frac{u^2 \sin^2\theta}{2g}$$

4. Derivation of Horizontal Range ($R$) and Condition for Maximum Range: The horizontal range $R$ is the horizontal distance covered during the entire time of flight $T$: $$R = x(T) = (u \cos\theta) \cdot T = (u \cos\theta) \left( \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g} \right) = \frac{u^2 (2 \sin\theta \cos\theta)}{g}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $2 \sin\theta \cos\theta = \sin(2\theta)$: $$R = \frac{u^2 \sin(2\theta)}{g}$$

Condition for Maximum Horizontal Range ($R_{max}$): For a given launch velocity $u$, the range $R$ depends solely on the launch angle $\theta$. To find the angle that maximizes $R$: $$\frac{dR}{d\theta} = \frac{u^2}{g} \frac{d}{d\theta}[\sin(2\theta)] = \frac{u^2}{g} [2 \cos(2\theta)] = 0$$ $$\cos(2\theta) = 0 \implies 2\theta = 90^\circ \implies \theta = 45^\circ$$ The maximum horizontal range is achieved at a projection angle of $45^\circ$: $$R_{max} = \frac{u^2 \sin(90^\circ)}{g} = \frac{u^2}{g}$$ For any range $R < R_{max}$, two complementary launch angles ($\theta$ and $90^\circ - \theta$) produce the exact same horizontal range, since $\sin[2(90^\circ - \theta)] = \sin(180^\circ - 2\theta) = \sin(2\theta)$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

4. Derivation of Horizontal Range ($R$) and Condition for Maximum Range: The horizontal range $R$ is the horizontal distance covered during the entire time of flight $T$: $$R = x(T) = (u \cos\theta) \cdot T = (u \cos\theta) \left( \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g} \right) = \frac{u^2 (2 \sin\theta \cos\theta)}{g}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $2 \sin\theta \cos\theta = \sin(2\theta)$: $$R = \frac{u^2 \sin(2\theta)}{g}$$

Condition for Maximum Horizontal Range ($R_{max}$): For a given launch velocity $u$, the range $R$ depends solely on the launch angle $\theta$. To find the angle that maximizes $R$: $$\frac{dR}{d\theta} = \frac{u^2}{g} \frac{d}{d\theta}[\sin(2\theta)] = \frac{u^2}{g} [2 \cos(2\theta)] = 0$$ $$\cos(2\theta) = 0 \implies 2\theta = 90^\circ \implies \theta = 45^\circ$$ The maximum horizontal range is achieved at a projection angle of $45^\circ$: $$R_{max} = \frac{u^2 \sin(90^\circ)}{g} = \frac{u^2}{g}$$ For any range $R < R_{max}$, two complementary launch angles ($\theta$ and $90^\circ - \theta$) produce the exact same horizontal range, since $\sin[2(90^\circ - \theta)] = \sin(180^\circ - 2\theta) = \sin(2\theta)$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Vertical Motion: Downward gravitational acceleration ($a_y = -g$): The vertical velocity at any time $t$ is: $$v_y(t) = u \sin\theta - g t$$ The vertical displacement at time $t$ is: $$y(t) = (u \sin\theta) t - \frac{1}{2} g t^2 \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

1. Derivation of the Equation of the Path (Trajectory): To determine the geometric path, we eliminate time parameter $t$ by substituting Equation 1 into Equation 2: $$y = (u \sin\theta) \left( \frac{x}{u \cos\theta} \right) - \frac{1}{2} g \left( \frac{x}{u \cos\theta} \right)^2$$ $$y = x \left(\frac{\sin\theta}{\cos\theta}\right) - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta} \implies y = x \tan\theta - \frac{g x^2}{2 u^2 \cos^2\theta}$$ This equation is of the standard quadratic form $y = A x - B x^2$ (where $A = \tan\theta$ and $B = \frac{g}{2u^2 \cos^2\theta}$ are constants). This proves that the trajectory of an unresisted projectile is a parabola with its vertex pointing upward.

2. Derivation of Time of Flight ($T$): The total time of flight $T$ is the total elapsed time from projection until the projectile strikes the horizontal ground plane again ($y = 0$). Setting $y = 0$ in the vertical displacement equation: $$0 = (u \sin\theta) T - \frac{1}{2} g T^2 \implies T \left( u \sin\theta - \frac{1}{2} g T \right) = 0$$ Since $T \neq 0$ (which corresponds to launch): $$u \sin\theta - \frac{1}{2} g T = 0 \implies \frac{1}{2} g T = u \sin\theta$$ $$T = \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g}$$ The time to reach the maximum height $t_h$ (where vertical velocity vanishes, $v_y = 0 \implies u \sin\theta - g t_h = 0$) is exactly half the total time of flight: $$t_h = \frac{u \sin\theta}{g} = \frac{T}{2}$$

3. Derivation of Maximum Vertical Height ($H$): At the peak apex of the trajectory ($t = t_h = \frac{u \sin\theta}{g}$), the vertical displacement reaches its maximum value $H$. Using the kinematic relation $v_y^2 = u_y^2 - 2g H$ with $v_y = 0$: $$0 = (u \sin\theta)^2 - 2g H \implies 2g H = u^2 \sin^2\theta$$ $$H = \frac{u^2 \sin^2\theta}{2g}$$

4. Derivation of Horizontal Range ($R$) and Condition for Maximum Range: The horizontal range $R$ is the horizontal distance covered during the entire time of flight $T$: $$R = x(T) = (u \cos\theta) \cdot T = (u \cos\theta) \left( \frac{2 u \sin\theta}{g} \right) = \frac{u^2 (2 \sin\theta \cos\theta)}{g}$$ Using the trigonometric identity $2 \sin\theta \cos\theta = \sin(2\theta)$: $$R = \frac{u^2 \sin(2\theta)}{g}$$

Condition for Maximum Horizontal Range ($R_{max}$): For a given launch velocity $u$, the range $R$ depends solely on the launch angle $\theta$. To find the angle that maximizes $R$: $$\frac{dR}{d\theta} = \frac{u^2}{g} \frac{d}{d\theta}[\sin(2\theta)] = \frac{u^2}{g} [2 \cos(2\theta)] = 0$$ $$\cos(2\theta) = 0 \implies 2\theta = 90^\circ \implies \theta = 45^\circ$$ The maximum horizontal range is achieved at a projection angle of $45^\circ$: $$R_{max} = \frac{u^2 \sin(90^\circ)}{g} = \frac{u^2}{g}$$ For any range $R < R_{max}$, two complementary launch angles ($\theta$ and $90^\circ - \theta$) produce the exact same horizontal range, since $\sin[2(90^\circ - \theta)] = \sin(180^\circ - 2\theta) = \sin(2\theta)$.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 4.2: Parabolic Trajectory of a Projectile Showing Velocity Vectors and Range — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Derivation ID: MECH-Q18

Derive the expressions for the normal and tangential components of acceleration for a particle executing curvilinear planar motion. Explain the physical significance of each component. Derive the expressions for the normal and tangential components of acceleration for a particle executing curvilinear planar motion. Explain the physical significance of each component. Derive the expressions for the normal and tangential components of acceleration for a particle executing curvilinear planar motion. Explain the physical significance of each component.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\mathbf{a} = a_t \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + a_n \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = \frac{dv}{dt} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + \frac{v^2}{\rho} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n, \quad a = \sqrt{a_t^2 + a_n^2}$$

Tangential Acceleration ($a_t$): $a_t = \frac{dv}{dt} = v\frac{dv}{ds}$, measures rate of change of speed along path tangent.
Normal Acceleration ($a_n$): $a_n = \frac{v^2}{\rho}$, measures rate of change of direction, always directed inward toward center of curvature.
Curvature: $\rho$ is radius of curvature; $a_n = 0$ only for straight path ($\rho \to \infty$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

When a particle moves along a general curved path in a two-dimensional plane, its velocity vector changes continuously in both magnitude (speed) and spatial direction. While Cartesian coordinates $(x,y)$ are often convenient, describing motion using the intrinsic coordinate system defined by Tangential ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$) and Normal ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$) unit vectors provides direct physical insight into the dynamics of curved motion.

Intrinsic Coordinate System Formulation: Let a particle move along a planar curve described by path arc length $s(t)$. At any point $P$ along the trajectory: - Tangential Unit Vector $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$: Directed tangent to the path curve pointing in the direction of increasing arc length $s$ (direction of motion). - Principal Normal Unit Vector $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$: Directed perpendicular to the tangent, pointing inwards toward the instantaneous center of curvature $C$ of the path. By fundamental definition, the velocity vector $\mathbf{v}$ is always directed along the instantaneous tangent: $$\mathbf{v} = v \, \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t = \left(\frac{ds}{dt}\right) \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$$ where $v = \frac{ds}{dt}$ is the scalar speed of the particle.

Derivation of the Acceleration Vector: The acceleration vector $\mathbf{a}$ is the total time derivative of the velocity vector: $$\mathbf{a} = \frac{d\mathbf{v}}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}\left( v \, \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t \right)$$ Applying the product rule of differentiation: $$\mathbf{a} = \frac{dv}{dt} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + v \frac{d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t}{dt} \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

To evaluate the time derivative of the unit tangent vector $\frac{d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t}{dt}$: Let the particle move a differential distance $ds$ from point $P$ to a neighboring point $P'$ along the curve in time $dt$. At point $P$, the tangent is inclined at angle $\theta$ to a fixed reference direction. At point $P'$, the tangent is inclined at angle $\theta + d\theta$. The change in the unit tangent vector is $d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t = \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t(P') - \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t(P)$. Because $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$ has constant unit magnitude ($|\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t| = 1$), the vector triangle formed by $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$, $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$, and $d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$ is an isosceles triangle with vertex angle $d\theta$. For an infinitesimal angle $d\theta$: $$|d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t| \approx 1 \cdot d\theta = d\theta$$ As $dt \to 0$, the vector $d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$ becomes perpendicular to $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$ and points toward the inside of the curve (in the direction of the normal unit vector $\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$). Therefore, in vector form: $$d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t = d\theta \, \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$$ Dividing by differential time $dt$: $$\frac{d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t}{dt} = \frac{d\theta}{dt} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = \left(\frac{d\theta}{ds} \cdot \frac{ds}{dt}\right) \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$$ From differential geometry, the curvature of a plane curve is $\kappa = \frac{d\theta}{ds} = \frac{1}{\rho}$, where $\rho$ is the instantaneous Radius of Curvature. Since $\frac{ds}{dt} = v$: $$\frac{d\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t}{dt} = \left(\frac{1}{\rho} \cdot v\right) \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = \frac{v}{\rho} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

Substituting Equation 2 back into Equation 1 yields the complete acceleration vector: $$\mathbf{a} = \frac{dv}{dt} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + \frac{v^2}{\rho} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = a_t \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + a_n \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$$

Physical Interpretation of Acceleration Components: 1. Tangential Acceleration Component ($a_t$): $$a_t = \frac{dv}{dt} = \dot{v} = v \frac{dv}{ds} = \frac{d^2 s}{dt^2}$$ $a_t$ measures the time rate of change of the magnitude of velocity (speed). - If the particle speeds up, $a_t > 0$ (points in the direction of motion, $+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$). - If the particle slows down (brakes), $a_t < 0$ (opposes motion, $-\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$). - In uniform circular motion (constant speed), $a_t = 0$. 2. Normal (Centripetal) Acceleration Component ($a_n$): $$a_n = \frac{v^2}{\rho}$$ $a_n$ measures the time rate of change of the direction of the velocity vector. - It is always directed perpendicular to the path inward toward the center of curvature ($+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$). - $a_n$ can never be negative, and is zero only if the path is a straight line ($\rho \to \infty$).

Resultant Acceleration Magnitude and Direction: Because the tangential and normal unit vectors are mutually orthogonal ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t \cdot \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = 0$), the total acceleration magnitude is: $$a = \sqrt{a_t^2 + a_n^2} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{v^2}{\rho}\right)^2}$$ The angle $\phi$ between total acceleration $\mathbf{a}$ and the tangential direction of motion is: $$\tan\phi = \frac{a_n}{a_t} = \frac{v^2 / \rho}{dv / dt} \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{a_n}{a_t}\right)$$

When a particle moves along a general curved path in a two-dimensional plane, its velocity vector changes continuously in both magnitude (speed) and spatial direction. While Cartesian coordinates $(x,y)$ are often convenient, describing motion using the intrinsic coordinate system defined by Tangential ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$) and Normal ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$) unit vectors provides direct physical insight into the dynamics of curved motion.

By fundamental definition, the velocity vector $\mathbf{v}$ is always directed along the instantaneous tangent: $$\mathbf{v} = v \, \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t = \left(\frac{ds}{dt}\right) \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$$ where $v = \frac{ds}{dt}$ is the scalar speed of the particle.

At point $P$, the tangent is inclined at angle $\theta$ to a fixed reference direction. At point $P'$, the tangent is inclined at angle $\theta + d\theta$. Substituting Equation 2 back into Equation 1 yields the complete acceleration vector: $$\mathbf{a} = \frac{dv}{dt} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + \frac{v^2}{\rho} \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = a_t \hat{\mathbf{e}}_t + a_n \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$$

Physical Interpretation of Acceleration Components: 1. Tangential Acceleration Component ($a_t$): $$a_t = \frac{dv}{dt} = \dot{v} = v \frac{dv}{ds} = \frac{d^2 s}{dt^2}$$ $a_t$ measures the time rate of change of the magnitude of velocity (speed). - If the particle speeds up, $a_t > 0$ (points in the direction of motion, $+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$). - If the particle slows down (brakes), $a_t < 0$ (opposes motion, $-\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t$). - In uniform circular motion (constant speed), $a_t = 0$. 2. Normal (Centripetal) Acceleration Component ($a_n$): $$a_n = \frac{v^2}{\rho}$$ $a_n$ measures the time rate of change of the direction of the velocity vector. - It is always directed perpendicular to the path inward toward the center of curvature ($+\hat{\mathbf{e}}_n$). - $a_n$ can never be negative, and is zero only if the path is a straight line ($\rho \to \infty$).

Resultant Acceleration Magnitude and Direction: Because the tangential and normal unit vectors are mutually orthogonal ($\hat{\mathbf{e}}_t \cdot \hat{\mathbf{e}}_n = 0$), the total acceleration magnitude is: $$a = \sqrt{a_t^2 + a_n^2} = \sqrt{\left(\frac{dv}{dt}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{v^2}{\rho}\right)^2}$$ The angle $\phi$ between total acceleration $\mathbf{a}$ and the tangential direction of motion is: $$\tan\phi = \frac{a_n}{a_t} = \frac{v^2 / \rho}{dv / dt} \implies \phi = \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{a_n}{a_t}\right)$$

Theory & Application ID: MECH-Q19

State Newton's Second Law of Motion and D'Alembert's Principle of dynamic equilibrium. Apply D'Alembert's principle to analyze the apparent weight in an accelerating elevator and determine the acceleration and cord tension in an Atwood machine. State Newton's Second Law of Motion and D'Alembert's Principle of dynamic equilibrium. Apply D'Alembert's principle to analyze the apparent weight in an accelerating elevator and determine the acceleration and cord tension in an Atwood machine. State Newton's Second Law of Motion and D'Alembert's Principle of dynamic equilibrium. Apply D'Alembert's principle to analyze the apparent weight in an accelerating elevator and determine the acceleration and cord tension in an Atwood machine.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma \mathbf{F} + (-m \mathbf{a}) = 0 \implies \Sigma \mathbf{F} + \mathbf{F}_i = 0, \quad a = \left(\frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g, \quad T = \left(\frac{2 m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g$$

D'Alembert's Principle: Dynamic systems brought to static equilibrium by adding reverse inertia force $\mathbf{F}_i = -m\mathbf{a}$.
Accelerating Elevator: Upward $\implies N = m(g+a)$; Downward $\implies N = m(g-a)$; Free fall $\implies N = 0$.
Atwood Machine: Common acceleration $a = \frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}g$, string tension $T = \frac{2m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}g$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Sir Isaac Newton's Second Law of Motion forms the foundation of classical particle kinetics: the net external force $\Sigma \mathbf{F}$ acting on a particle of constant mass $m$ produces an acceleration $\mathbf{a}$ directly proportional to the force and in the same direction: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F} = m \mathbf{a}$$ While Newton's formulation describes a dynamic non-equilibrium state, in 1743 French mathematician Jean le Rond d'Alembert proposed a principle that transforms dynamic problems into equivalent static equilibrium problems.

D'Alembert's Principle of Dynamic Equilibrium: D'Alembert's principle states that a system of particles or a rigid body undergoing accelerated motion can be brought into a state of dynamic equilibrium by adding an imaginary force called the Inertia Force (or reverse-effective force) to the system of actual external forces. The Inertia Force $\mathbf{F}_i$ is defined mathematically as the negative product of mass and acceleration: $$\mathbf{F}_i = -m \mathbf{a}$$ Adding the inertia force to Newton's second law: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F} + (-m \mathbf{a}) = 0 \implies \Sigma \mathbf{F} + \mathbf{F}_i = 0$$ Statement of the Principle: The vector sum of all external physical forces acting on a body and the fictitious inertia force is identically equal to zero. This formulation allows engineers to analyze complex dynamic mechanisms using the familiar methods of statics: $$\Sigma F_x = 0, \quad \Sigma F_y = 0, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0$$

Application 1: Apparent Weight in an Accelerating Elevator: Consider a passenger of mass $m$ standing on a weighing scale inside an elevator cab undergoing vertical acceleration $a$. Forces acting on the passenger: - Real external forces: Downward weight $W = mg$ ($\downarrow$), Upward normal reaction $N$ from scale ($\uparrow$). - Fictitious inertia force: $\mathbf{F}_i = -m\mathbf{a}$. 1. Elevator Accelerating Upward ($+a \uparrow$): The inertia force acts downward: $F_i = ma$ ($\downarrow$). Applying dynamic equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$N - mg - ma = 0 \implies N = m(g + a)$$ The weighing scale reads an increased apparent weight ($N > mg$). 2. Elevator Accelerating Downward ($-a \downarrow$): The inertia force acts upward: $F_i = ma$ ($\uparrow$). Applying dynamic equilibrium: $$N + ma - mg = 0 \implies N = m(g - a)$$ The apparent weight decreases ($N < mg$). 3. Free Fall ($a = g \downarrow$): $$N = m(g - g) = 0$$ The passenger experiences apparent weightlessness.

Application 2: Atwood's Machine (Connected Bodies Over a Smooth Pulley): Problem Statement: Two bodies of unequal masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ ($m_1 > m_2$) are suspended from the ends of a light, flexible, inextensible cord passing over a frictionless, massless pulley. Determine the common acceleration $a$ of the system and the tension $T$ in the cord using D'Alembert's principle.

Analytical Solution Using Dynamic Equilibrium: Because the cord is inextensible, mass $m_1$ accelerates downward with acceleration $a$ ($\downarrow$), while mass $m_2$ accelerates upward with the identical acceleration $a$ ($\uparrow$). 1. Dynamic Equilibrium of Mass $m_1$: Real forces: Downward weight $m_1 g$ ($\downarrow$), Upward cable tension $T$ ($\uparrow$). Since acceleration is downward, the inertia force acts upward: $F_{i1} = m_1 a$ ($\uparrow$). Applying static equilibrium condition ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$T + m_1 a - m_1 g = 0 \implies T = m_1(g - a) \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

2. Dynamic Equilibrium of Mass $m_2$: Real forces: Downward weight $m_2 g$ ($\downarrow$), Upward cable tension $T$ ($\uparrow$). Since acceleration is upward, the inertia force acts downward: $F_{i2} = m_2 a$ ($\downarrow$). Applying static equilibrium condition ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$T - m_2 g - m_2 a = 0 \implies T = m_2(g + a) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

3. Solving for Common Acceleration $a$: Equating Equations 1 and 2: $$m_1(g - a) = m_2(g + a)$$ $$m_1 g - m_1 a = m_2 g + m_2 a$$ Rearranging terms: $$(m_1 + m_2) a = (m_1 - m_2) g$$ $$a = \left(\frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g$$

4. Solving for Cord Tension $T$: Substituting $a$ into Equation 2: $$T = m_2 \left[ g + \left(\frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g \right] = m_2 g \left[ 1 + \frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2} \right] = m_2 g \left[ \frac{m_1 + m_2 + m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2} \right]$$ $$T = \left(\frac{2 m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g$$ This demonstrates how D'Alembert's principle transforms dynamic systems into straightforward static force balance equations.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Sir Isaac Newton's Second Law of Motion forms the foundation of classical particle kinetics: the net external force $\Sigma \mathbf{F}$ acting on a particle of constant mass $m$ produces an acceleration $\mathbf{a}$ directly proportional to the force and in the same direction: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F} = m \mathbf{a}$$ While Newton's formulation describes a dynamic non-equilibrium state, in 1743 French mathematician Jean le Rond d'Alembert proposed a principle that transforms dynamic problems into equivalent static equilibrium problems.

D'Alembert's Principle of Dynamic Equilibrium: D'Alembert's principle states that a system of particles or a rigid body undergoing accelerated motion can be brought into a state of dynamic equilibrium by adding an imaginary force called the Inertia Force (or reverse-effective force) to the system of actual external forces. The Inertia Force $\mathbf{F}_i$ is defined mathematically as the negative product of mass and acceleration: $$\mathbf{F}_i = -m \mathbf{a}$$ Adding the inertia force to Newton's second law: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F} + (-m \mathbf{a}) = 0 \implies \Sigma \mathbf{F} + \mathbf{F}_i = 0$$ Statement of the Principle: The vector sum of all external physical forces acting on a body and the fictitious inertia force is identically equal to zero. This formulation allows engineers to analyze complex dynamic mechanisms using the familiar methods of statics: $$\Sigma F_x = 0, \quad \Sigma F_y = 0, \quad \Sigma M_O = 0$$

Application 1: Apparent Weight in an Accelerating Elevator: Consider a passenger of mass $m$ standing on a weighing scale inside an elevator cab undergoing vertical acceleration $a$. Forces acting on the passenger: - Real external forces: Downward weight $W = mg$ ($\downarrow$), Upward normal reaction $N$ from scale ($\uparrow$). - Fictitious inertia force: $\mathbf{F}_i = -m\mathbf{a}$. 1. Elevator Accelerating Upward ($+a \uparrow$): The inertia force acts downward: $F_i = ma$ ($\downarrow$). Applying dynamic equilibrium ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$N - mg - ma = 0 \implies N = m(g + a)$$ The weighing scale reads an increased apparent weight ($N > mg$). 2. Elevator Accelerating Downward ($-a \downarrow$): The inertia force acts upward: $F_i = ma$ ($\uparrow$). Applying dynamic equilibrium: $$N + ma - mg = 0 \implies N = m(g - a)$$ The apparent weight decreases ($N < mg$). 3. Free Fall ($a = g \downarrow$): $$N = m(g - g) = 0$$ The passenger experiences apparent weightlessness.

Application 2: Atwood's Machine (Connected Bodies Over a Smooth Pulley): Problem Statement: Two bodies of unequal masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ ($m_1 > m_2$) are suspended from the ends of a light, flexible, inextensible cord passing over a frictionless, massless pulley. Determine the common acceleration $a$ of the system and the tension $T$ in the cord using D'Alembert's principle.

Analytical Solution Using Dynamic Equilibrium: Because the cord is inextensible, mass $m_1$ accelerates downward with acceleration $a$ ($\downarrow$), while mass $m_2$ accelerates upward with the identical acceleration $a$ ($\uparrow$). 1. Dynamic Equilibrium of Mass $m_1$: Real forces: Downward weight $m_1 g$ ($\downarrow$), Upward cable tension $T$ ($\uparrow$). Since acceleration is downward, the inertia force acts upward: $F_{i1} = m_1 a$ ($\uparrow$). Applying static equilibrium condition ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$T + m_1 a - m_1 g = 0 \implies T = m_1(g - a) \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

2. Dynamic Equilibrium of Mass $m_2$: Real forces: Downward weight $m_2 g$ ($\downarrow$), Upward cable tension $T$ ($\uparrow$). Since acceleration is upward, the inertia force acts downward: $F_{i2} = m_2 a$ ($\downarrow$). Applying static equilibrium condition ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$T - m_2 g - m_2 a = 0 \implies T = m_2(g + a) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

3. Solving for Common Acceleration $a$: Equating Equations 1 and 2: $$m_1(g - a) = m_2(g + a)$$ $$m_1 g - m_1 a = m_2 g + m_2 a$$ Rearranging terms: $$(m_1 + m_2) a = (m_1 - m_2) g$$ $$a = \left(\frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g$$

4. Solving for Cord Tension $T$: Substituting $a$ into Equation 2: $$T = m_2 \left[ g + \left(\frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g \right] = m_2 g \left[ 1 + \frac{m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2} \right] = m_2 g \left[ \frac{m_1 + m_2 + m_1 - m_2}{m_1 + m_2} \right]$$ $$T = \left(\frac{2 m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}\right) g$$ This demonstrates how D'Alembert's principle transforms dynamic systems into straightforward static force balance equations.

Formulate the equations of dynamic motion for two connected bodies where one body moves along a rough inclined plane and is connected by a cord over a pulley to a hanging body. ⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$a = \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g, \quad T = \frac{m_1 m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2}(1 + \sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$

Connected Incline Dynamics: Normal reaction $N = m_1 g \cos\alpha$; friction $F_k = \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha$.
Force Balance: $T - m_1 g\sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g\cos\alpha = m_1 a$, and $m_2 g - T = m_2 a$.
Motion Threshold: Mass accelerates up incline only if $m_2 > m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu\cos\alpha)$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A fundamental problem in engineering mechanics involves the dynamic motion of interconnected bodies where one mass slides along a rough inclined plane while connected by a flexible cord over a pulley to a second hanging mass. This configuration models hoist haulage systems, inclined conveyor lines, and mine shaft cars.

System Description and Free Body Formulation: Consider a body $A$ of mass $m_1$ resting on a rough plane inclined at angle $\alpha$ to the horizontal. The coefficient of kinetic friction between body $A$ and the plane is $\mu$. A light, flexible, inextensible cord attached to body $A$ passes over a smooth, frictionless, massless pulley fixed at the apex of the incline, supporting a hanging body $B$ of mass $m_2$ at its opposite end. Assume mass $m_2$ is sufficiently heavy to pull mass $m_1$ up the inclined plane with an acceleration $a$. - Mass $m_1$ moves up the incline with acceleration $a$ directed parallel to the slope ($\nearrow$). - Mass $m_2$ moves vertically downward with acceleration $a$ ($\downarrow$). Both bodies share the same acceleration magnitude $a$ due to cord inextensibility, and the tension $T$ is uniform throughout the cord.

1. Analysis of Body $A$ on the Rough Incline (Moving Upward): Set up a tilted Cartesian coordinate system with the $x$-axis parallel to the incline pointing upward ($\nearrow$) and the $y$-axis perpendicular to the incline pointing outward ($\nwarrow$). Forces acting on mass $m_1$: - Weight $W_1 = m_1 g$ acting vertically downward ($\downarrow$). Resolving into components: - Perpendicular to incline: $m_1 g \cos\alpha$ ($-\hat{\mathbf{j}}$) - Parallel to incline acting down the slope: $m_1 g \sin\alpha$ ($-\hat{\mathbf{i}}$) - Normal reaction $N$ exerted by the incline on $m_1$ ($+\hat{\mathbf{j}}$). - Cable tension $T$ pulling up the incline ($+\hat{\mathbf{i}}$). - Kinetic friction force $F_k = \mu N$. Because the block moves up the slope, friction acts down the slope ($-\hat{\mathbf{i}}$) opposing motion.

Applying equilibrium perpendicular to the incline ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$N - m_1 g \cos\alpha = 0 \implies N = m_1 g \cos\alpha$$ The kinetic friction force is therefore: $$F_k = \mu N = \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha$$ Applying Newton's second law / D'Alembert's dynamic equilibrium along the incline ($\Sigma F_x = m_1 a$): $$T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - F_k = m_1 a$$ Substituting $F_k$: $$T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha = m_1 a \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ Solving for tension $T$: $$T = m_1 a + m_1 g (\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$

2. Analysis of Hanging Body $B$ (Moving Downward): Forces acting on hanging mass $m_2$: - Downward gravitational weight: $m_2 g$ ($\downarrow$). - Upward cable tension: $T$ ($\uparrow$). Applying Newton's second law in the downward direction ($\Sigma F_y = m_2 a$): $$m_2 g - T = m_2 a \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Solving for tension $T$: $$T = m_2 (g - a)$$

3. Derivation of Common System Acceleration ($a$): Adding Equation 1 and Equation 2 eliminates the internal cord tension $T$: $$(T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha) + (m_2 g - T) = m_1 a + m_2 a$$ $$m_2 g - m_1 g (\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Factoring out $g$: $$g \left[ m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right] = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Dividing by total system mass $(m_1 + m_2)$ gives the acceleration: $$a = \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g$$

Condition for Motion to Occur: For mass $m_1$ to accelerate up the incline ($a > 0$), the numerator must be strictly positive: $$m_2 > m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$ If $m_2 < m_1(\sin\alpha - \mu \cos\alpha)$, the block will accelerate down the incline (reversing the sign of friction). If $m_2$ falls between these two limits, the system remains in static equilibrium at rest ($a = 0$).

4. Derivation of Cable Tension ($T$): Substituting the acceleration expression $a$ back into Equation 2: $$T = m_2 (g - a) = m_2 \left[ g - \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ (m_1 + m_2) - m_2 + m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_1 m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ 1 + \sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha \right]$$ This closed-form solution yields both the motion acceleration and internal cable tension for the connected system.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A fundamental problem in engineering mechanics involves the dynamic motion of interconnected bodies where one mass slides along a rough inclined plane while connected by a flexible cord over a pulley to a second hanging mass. This configuration models hoist haulage systems, inclined conveyor lines, and mine shaft cars.

System Description and Free Body Formulation: Consider a body $A$ of mass $m_1$ resting on a rough plane inclined at angle $\alpha$ to the horizontal. The coefficient of kinetic friction between body $A$ and the plane is $\mu$. A light, flexible, inextensible cord attached to body $A$ passes over a smooth, frictionless, massless pulley fixed at the apex of the incline, supporting a hanging body $B$ of mass $m_2$ at its opposite end. Assume mass $m_2$ is sufficiently heavy to pull mass $m_1$ up the inclined plane with an acceleration $a$. - Mass $m_1$ moves up the incline with acceleration $a$ directed parallel to the slope ($\nearrow$). - Mass $m_2$ moves vertically downward with acceleration $a$ ($\downarrow$). Both bodies share the same acceleration magnitude $a$ due to cord inextensibility, and the tension $T$ is uniform throughout the cord.

Resolving into components: - Perpendicular to incline: $m_1 g \cos\alpha$ ($-\hat{\mathbf{j}}$) - Parallel to incline acting down the slope: $m_1 g \sin\alpha$ ($-\hat{\mathbf{i}}$) - Normal reaction $N$ exerted by the incline on $m_1$ ($+\hat{\mathbf{j}}$). - Cable tension $T$ pulling up the incline ($+\hat{\mathbf{i}}$). - Kinetic friction force $F_k = \mu N$. Because the block moves up the slope, friction acts down the slope ($-\hat{\mathbf{i}}$) opposing motion.

Applying equilibrium perpendicular to the incline ($\Sigma F_y = 0$): $$N - m_1 g \cos\alpha = 0 \implies N = m_1 g \cos\alpha$$ The kinetic friction force is therefore: $$F_k = \mu N = \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha$$ Applying Newton's second law / D'Alembert's dynamic equilibrium along the incline ($\Sigma F_x = m_1 a$): $$T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - F_k = m_1 a$$ Substituting $F_k$: $$T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha = m_1 a \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ Solving for tension $T$: $$T = m_1 a + m_1 g (\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$

2. Analysis of Hanging Body $B$ (Moving Downward): Forces acting on hanging mass $m_2$: - Downward gravitational weight: $m_2 g$ ($\downarrow$). - Upward cable tension: $T$ ($\uparrow$). Applying Newton's second law in the downward direction ($\Sigma F_y = m_2 a$): $$m_2 g - T = m_2 a \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Solving for tension $T$: $$T = m_2 (g - a)$$

3. Derivation of Common System Acceleration ($a$): Adding Equation 1 and Equation 2 eliminates the internal cord tension $T$: $$(T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha) + (m_2 g - T) = m_1 a + m_2 a$$ $$m_2 g - m_1 g (\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Factoring out $g$: $$g \left[ m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right] = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Dividing by total system mass $(m_1 + m_2)$ gives the acceleration: $$a = \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g$$

Condition for Motion to Occur: For mass $m_1$ to accelerate up the incline ($a > 0$), the numerator must be strictly positive: $$m_2 > m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$ If $m_2 < m_1(\sin\alpha - \mu \cos\alpha)$, the block will accelerate down the incline (reversing the sign of friction). If $m_2$ falls between these two limits, the system remains in static equilibrium at rest ($a = 0$).

4. Derivation of Cable Tension ($T$): Substituting the acceleration expression $a$ back into Equation 2: $$T = m_2 (g - a) = m_2 \left[ g - \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ (m_1 + m_2) - m_2 + m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_1 m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ 1 + \sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha \right]$$ This closed-form solution yields both the motion acceleration and internal cable tension for the connected system.

Applying Newton's second law in the downward direction ($\Sigma F_y = m_2 a$): $$m_2 g - T = m_2 a \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Solving for tension $T$: $$T = m_2 (g - a)$$

3. Derivation of Common System Acceleration ($a$): Adding Equation 1 and Equation 2 eliminates the internal cord tension $T$: $$(T - m_1 g \sin\alpha - \mu m_1 g \cos\alpha) + (m_2 g - T) = m_1 a + m_2 a$$ $$m_2 g - m_1 g (\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Factoring out $g$: $$g \left[ m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right] = (m_1 + m_2) a$$ Dividing by total system mass $(m_1 + m_2)$ gives the acceleration: $$a = \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g$$

Condition for Motion to Occur: For mass $m_1$ to accelerate up the incline ($a > 0$), the numerator must be strictly positive: $$m_2 > m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)$$ If $m_2 < m_1(\sin\alpha - \mu \cos\alpha)$, the block will accelerate down the incline (reversing the sign of friction). If $m_2$ falls between these two limits, the system remains in static equilibrium at rest ($a = 0$).

4. Derivation of Cable Tension ($T$): Substituting the acceleration expression $a$ back into Equation 2: $$T = m_2 (g - a) = m_2 \left[ g - \frac{m_2 - m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha)}{m_1 + m_2} g \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ (m_1 + m_2) - m_2 + m_1(\sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha) \right]$$ $$T = \frac{m_1 m_2 g}{m_1 + m_2} \left[ 1 + \sin\alpha + \mu \cos\alpha \right]$$ This closed-form solution yields both the motion acceleration and internal cable tension for the connected system.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 4.5: Free Body Diagrams for Block on Rough Incline Connected to Hanging Weight via Pulley — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
Module 5

Work-Energy Theorem, Impulse-Momentum and Direct Central Impact

6 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Derivation ID: MECH-Q21

Define work done by a force and derive expressions for work done by gravity and spring forces. Derive the Work-Energy Theorem for a particle moving under a variable force. Define work done by a force and derive expressions for work done by gravity and spring forces. Derive the Work-Energy Theorem for a particle moving under a variable force. Define work done by a force and derive expressions for work done by gravity and spring forces. Derive the Work-Energy Theorem for a particle moving under a variable force.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$U_{1-2} = \int_1^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = T_2 - T_1 = \frac{1}{2} m v_2^2 - \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2 = \Delta KE$$

Work Done: $U = \int \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}$; Gravity $U_g = -mg(y_2 - y_1)$; Spring $U_s = -\frac{1}{2}k(x_2^2 - x_1^2)$.
Work-Energy Theorem: $F_t = m v \frac{dv}{ds} \implies \int F_t ds = \int mv dv \implies U_{1-2} = T_2 - T_1$.
Key Advantage: Directly links force and displacement without calculating elapsed time.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$U_{1-2} = \int_1^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = T_2 - T_1 = \frac{1}{2} m v_2^2 - \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2 = \Delta KE$$

Work Done: $U = \int \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r}$; Gravity $U_g = -mg(y_2 - y_1)$; Spring $U_s = -\frac{1}{2}k(x_2^2 - x_1^2)$.
Work-Energy Theorem: $F_t = m v \frac{dv}{ds} \implies \int F_t ds = \int mv dv \implies U_{1-2} = T_2 - T_1$.
Key Advantage: Directly links force and displacement without calculating elapsed time.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Work-Energy Theorem provides an integral formulation of Newton's second law that relates the forces acting on a body directly to changes in its velocity over a specified displacement, completely eliminating time as an intermediate variable.

Definition of Work Done by a Force: In classical mechanics, the work done $U$ by a force vector $\mathbf{F}$ acting on a particle as it undergoes an infinitesimal vector displacement $d\mathbf{r}$ is defined as the dot product: $$dU = \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = F \cos\theta \, ds$$ where $\theta$ is the angle between the force vector and the displacement vector, and $ds = |d\mathbf{r}|$ is the scalar path displacement. The total work done by force $\mathbf{F}$ as the particle traverses a continuous path from position 1 to position 2 is the line integral: $$U_{1-2} = \int_1^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = \int_{s_1}^{s_2} F_t \, ds$$ where $F_t = F \cos\theta$ is the tangential component of force along the path of motion.

Work Done by Common Engineering Forces: 1. Work Done by a Constant Force in Rectilinear Motion: If a constant force $F$ acts at a constant angle $\theta$ over linear displacement $s$: $$U = (F \cos\theta) s$$ 2. Work Done by Gravity (Weight of Particle): Let a body of mass $m$ move from vertical elevation $y_1$ to elevation $y_2$. The gravitational force is $\mathbf{F}_g = -mg \hat{\mathbf{j}}$ (acting downward). The differential displacement is $d\mathbf{r} = dx \hat{\mathbf{i}} + dy \hat{\mathbf{j}} + dz \hat{\mathbf{k}}$. $$U_g = \int_{y_1}^{y_2} (-mg \hat{\mathbf{j}}) \cdot (dy \hat{\mathbf{j}}) = -mg \int_{y_1}^{y_2} dy = -mg (y_2 - y_1)$$ - If the body moves downward ($y_2 < y_1$), work done by gravity is positive ($+mg h$). - If the body moves upward ($y_2 > y_1$), work done by gravity is negative ($-mg h$). 3. Work Done by a Linear Elastic Spring: According to Hooke's law, the restoring force exerted by a spring deformed by displacement $x$ from its unstretched equilibrium position is $F_s = -k x$, where $k$ is the spring stiffness ($\text{N/m}$). The work done by the spring as its deformation changes from initial stretch $x_1$ to final stretch $x_2$ is: $$U_s = \int_{x_1}^{x_2} (-k x) dx = -k \left[ \frac{x^2}{2} \right]_{x_1}^{x_2} = -\frac{1}{2} k (x_2^2 - x_1^2) = \frac{1}{2} k x_1^2 - \frac{1}{2} k x_2^2$$

Mathematical Derivation of the Work-Energy Theorem: Consider a particle of mass $m$ moving along a curved path under the action of a resultant force $\mathbf{F}$. Resolving Newton's second law along the tangential direction of motion: $$F_t = m a_t$$ Substituting the kinematic relation for tangential acceleration $a_t = v \frac{dv}{ds}$: $$F_t = m \left( v \frac{dv}{ds} \right)$$ Multiplying both sides by the differential path displacement $ds$: $$F_t \, ds = m v \, dv$$ Integrating both sides between state 1 (position $s_1$, velocity $v_1$) and state 2 (position $s_2$, velocity $v_2$): $$\int_{s_1}^{s_2} F_t \, ds = \int_{v_1}^{v_2} m v \, dv$$ The left-hand side is the total work done by all forces acting on the particle, denoted as $U_{1-2}$ (or $\Sigma W_{1-2}$). Evaluating the integral on the right-hand side: $$\int_{v_1}^{v_2} m v \, dv = m \left[ \frac{v^2}{2} \right]_{v_1}^{v_2} = \frac{1}{2} m v_2^2 - \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2$$ Defining the Kinetic Energy of the particle as $T = \frac{1}{2} m v^2$: $$U_{1-2} = T_2 - T_1 = \Delta T = \Delta KE$$ Statement of the Work-Energy Theorem: The total work done by all external forces (conservative and non-conservative) acting on a particle during a displacement is equal to the change in the kinetic energy of the particle over that displacement.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Theory & Derivation ID: MECH-Q22

Define a conservative force and state its criteria. Define potential energy and prove the Law of Conservation of Mechanical Energy. Apply it to find the velocity of an oscillating simple pendulum. Define a conservative force and state its criteria. Define potential energy and prove the Law of Conservation of Mechanical Energy. Apply it to find the velocity of an oscillating simple pendulum. Define a conservative force and state its criteria. Define potential energy and prove the Law of Conservation of Mechanical Energy. Apply it to find the velocity of an oscillating simple pendulum.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0, \quad \mathbf{F} = -\nabla V, \quad T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2 = E_{\text{total}} = \text{constant}$$

Conservative Force: Work done is path-independent ($\nabla \times \mathbf{F} = 0$); derived from potential $F = -\nabla V$.
Conservation of Mechanical Energy: When non-conservative forces do no work, $T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2$.
Simple Pendulum: Velocity at angle $\theta$ is $v = \sqrt{2gl(\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)}$; cord tension does zero work.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0, \quad \mathbf{F} = -\nabla V, \quad T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2 = E_{\text{total}} = \text{constant}$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\oint \mathbf{F}\cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0, \quad \mathbf{F} = -\nabla V, \quad T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2 = E_{\text{total}} = \text{constant}$$

Conservative Force: Work done is path-independent ($\nabla \times \mathbf{F} = 0$); derived from potential $F = -\nabla V$.
Conservation of Mechanical Energy: When non-conservative forces do no work, $T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2$.
Simple Pendulum: Velocity at angle $\theta$ is $v = \sqrt{2gl(\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)}$; cord tension does zero work.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In engineering mechanics, forces acting on dynamic systems are broadly classified as either conservative or non-conservative (dissipative) forces.

Conservative Forces and Criteria: A force $\mathbf{F}$ is defined as Conservative if the work done by the force in moving a particle between two points depends solely on the initial and final positions of the particle and is completely independent of the physical path taken between them. Mathematical Criteria for a Conservative Force: 1. Path Independence: For any two distinct paths $A$ and $B$ joining positions 1 and 2: $$\int_{1, \text{Path } A}^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = \int_{1, \text{Path } B}^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r}$$ 2. Zero Closed Loop Work: The net work done by a conservative force around any closed loop is identically zero: $$\oint \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0$$ 3. Curl-Free Force Field: The curl of the force vector is identically zero: $$\nabla \times \mathbf{F} = \text{curl}(\mathbf{F}) = 0$$ Examples of conservative forces include gravitational forces, electrostatic forces, and ideal linear elastic spring forces. In contrast, frictional forces and fluid drag are non-conservative because their work depends on path length and is dissipated irreversibly as heat.

Potential Energy Function ($V$): Because the work done by a conservative force is path-independent, it can be expressed as the negative change of a scalar potential function $V(\mathbf{r})$, called the Potential Energy: $$dU = \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = -dV \implies \mathbf{F} = -\nabla V = -\left( \frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{\mathbf{i}} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{\mathbf{j}} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{\mathbf{k}} \right)$$ Integrating from position 1 to position 2: $$U_{1-2} = \int_1^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = -\int_1^2 dV = -(V_2 - V_1) = V_1 - V_2$$ Standard Potential Energy Expressions: - Gravitational Potential Energy: Taking datum at $y = 0$: $$V_g = m g y$$ - Elastic Spring Potential Energy: Taking datum at unstretched length $x = 0$: $$V_e = \frac{1}{2} k x^2$$

Mathematical Proof of the Law of Conservation of Mechanical Energy: Consider a mechanical system acted upon solely by conservative forces. According to the Work-Energy Theorem: $$U_{1-2} = T_2 - T_1$$ Since all operating forces are conservative: $$U_{1-2} = V_1 - V_2$$ Equating these two expressions: $$V_1 - V_2 = T_2 - T_1$$ Rearranging terms so that state 1 terms appear on the left and state 2 terms on the right: $$T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2$$ Defining the Total Mechanical Energy $E$ as the sum of kinetic and potential energy ($E = T + V$): $$E_1 = E_2 = \text{constant}$$ Statement of the Law: In an isolated mechanical system where all active forces are conservative, the total mechanical energy (kinetic plus potential) remains strictly constant throughout the motion.

Physical Application: Velocity of an Oscillating Simple Pendulum: Consider a simple pendulum consisting of a bob of mass $m$ suspended by a light inextensible cord of length $l$. The bob is pulled aside to an initial angle $\theta_0$ with the vertical and released from rest ($v_1 = 0$). Determine the velocity $v$ of the bob when it swings through an intermediate angle $\theta$. 1. Select Datum: Set the gravitational potential energy datum ($y = 0$) at the lowest equilibrium position of the bob ($\theta = 0$). 2. Initial State 1 (Release at angle $\theta_0$): - Height above datum: $h_1 = l - l \cos\theta_0 = l(1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_1 = \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2 = 0$ (released from rest) - Potential energy: $V_1 = m g h_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Total initial energy: $E_1 = T_1 + V_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ 3. Intermediate State 2 (At angle $\theta$): - Height above datum: $h_2 = l(1 - \cos\theta)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2$ - Potential energy: $V_2 = m g h_2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ - Total energy: $E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ 4. Applying Conservation of Mechanical Energy ($E_1 = E_2$): $$m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$$ $$\frac{1}{2} m v^2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) - m g l (1 - \cos\theta) = m g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)$$ Dividing by $m$ and multiplying by 2: $$v^2 = 2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0) \implies v = \sqrt{2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)}$$ At the lowest point ($\theta = 0$, $\cos 0 = 1$), the velocity reaches its maximum: $$v_{max} = \sqrt{2 g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)}$$ Notice that cord tension does zero work because it is always perpendicular to velocity ($T \perp d\mathbf{r}$), making energy conservation exact.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

Determine the velocity $v$ of the bob when it swings through an intermediate angle $\theta$. 1. Select Datum: Set the gravitational potential energy datum ($y = 0$) at the lowest equilibrium position of the bob ($\theta = 0$). 2. Initial State 1 (Release at angle $\theta_0$): - Height above datum: $h_1 = l - l \cos\theta_0 = l(1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_1 = \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2 = 0$ (released from rest) - Potential energy: $V_1 = m g h_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Total initial energy: $E_1 = T_1 + V_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ 3. Intermediate State 2 (At angle $\theta$): - Height above datum: $h_2 = l(1 - \cos\theta)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2$ - Potential energy: $V_2 = m g h_2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ - Total energy: $E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ 4. Applying Conservation of Mechanical Energy ($E_1 = E_2$): $$m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$$ $$\frac{1}{2} m v^2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) - m g l (1 - \cos\theta) = m g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)$$ Dividing by $m$ and multiplying by 2: $$v^2 = 2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0) \implies v = \sqrt{2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)}$$ At the lowest point ($\theta = 0$, $\cos 0 = 1$), the velocity reaches its maximum: $$v_{max} = \sqrt{2 g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)}$$ Notice that cord tension does zero work because it is always perpendicular to velocity ($T \perp d\mathbf{r}$), making energy conservation exact.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In engineering mechanics, forces acting on dynamic systems are broadly classified as either conservative or non-conservative (dissipative) forces.

Conservative Forces and Criteria: A force $\mathbf{F}$ is defined as Conservative if the work done by the force in moving a particle between two points depends solely on the initial and final positions of the particle and is completely independent of the physical path taken between them. Mathematical Criteria for a Conservative Force: 1. Path Independence: For any two distinct paths $A$ and $B$ joining positions 1 and 2: $$\int_{1, \text{Path } A}^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = \int_{1, \text{Path } B}^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r}$$ 2. Zero Closed Loop Work: The net work done by a conservative force around any closed loop is identically zero: $$\oint \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = 0$$ 3. Curl-Free Force Field: The curl of the force vector is identically zero: $$\nabla \times \mathbf{F} = \text{curl}(\mathbf{F}) = 0$$ Examples of conservative forces include gravitational forces, electrostatic forces, and ideal linear elastic spring forces. In contrast, frictional forces and fluid drag are non-conservative because their work depends on path length and is dissipated irreversibly as heat.

Potential Energy Function ($V$): Because the work done by a conservative force is path-independent, it can be expressed as the negative change of a scalar potential function $V(\mathbf{r})$, called the Potential Energy: $$dU = \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = -dV \implies \mathbf{F} = -\nabla V = -\left( \frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{\mathbf{i}} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{\mathbf{j}} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{\mathbf{k}} \right)$$ Integrating from position 1 to position 2: $$U_{1-2} = \int_1^2 \mathbf{F} \cdot d\mathbf{r} = -\int_1^2 dV = -(V_2 - V_1) = V_1 - V_2$$ Standard Potential Energy Expressions: - Gravitational Potential Energy: Taking datum at $y = 0$: $$V_g = m g y$$ - Elastic Spring Potential Energy: Taking datum at unstretched length $x = 0$: $$V_e = \frac{1}{2} k x^2$$

Mathematical Proof of the Law of Conservation of Mechanical Energy: Consider a mechanical system acted upon solely by conservative forces. According to the Work-Energy Theorem: $$U_{1-2} = T_2 - T_1$$ Since all operating forces are conservative: $$U_{1-2} = V_1 - V_2$$ Equating these two expressions: $$V_1 - V_2 = T_2 - T_1$$ Rearranging terms so that state 1 terms appear on the left and state 2 terms on the right: $$T_1 + V_1 = T_2 + V_2$$ Defining the Total Mechanical Energy $E$ as the sum of kinetic and potential energy ($E = T + V$): $$E_1 = E_2 = \text{constant}$$ Statement of the Law: In an isolated mechanical system where all active forces are conservative, the total mechanical energy (kinetic plus potential) remains strictly constant throughout the motion.

Physical Application: Velocity of an Oscillating Simple Pendulum: Consider a simple pendulum consisting of a bob of mass $m$ suspended by a light inextensible cord of length $l$. The bob is pulled aside to an initial angle $\theta_0$ with the vertical and released from rest ($v_1 = 0$). Determine the velocity $v$ of the bob when it swings through an intermediate angle $\theta$. 1. Select Datum: Set the gravitational potential energy datum ($y = 0$) at the lowest equilibrium position of the bob ($\theta = 0$). 2. Initial State 1 (Release at angle $\theta_0$): - Height above datum: $h_1 = l - l \cos\theta_0 = l(1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_1 = \frac{1}{2} m v_1^2 = 0$ (released from rest) - Potential energy: $V_1 = m g h_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ - Total initial energy: $E_1 = T_1 + V_1 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)$ 3. Intermediate State 2 (At angle $\theta$): - Height above datum: $h_2 = l(1 - \cos\theta)$ - Kinetic energy: $T_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2$ - Potential energy: $V_2 = m g h_2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ - Total energy: $E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$ 4. Applying Conservation of Mechanical Energy ($E_1 = E_2$): $$m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) = \frac{1}{2} m v^2 + m g l (1 - \cos\theta)$$ $$\frac{1}{2} m v^2 = m g l (1 - \cos\theta_0) - m g l (1 - \cos\theta) = m g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)$$ Dividing by $m$ and multiplying by 2: $$v^2 = 2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0) \implies v = \sqrt{2 g l (\cos\theta - \cos\theta_0)}$$ At the lowest point ($\theta = 0$, $\cos 0 = 1$), the velocity reaches its maximum: $$v_{max} = \sqrt{2 g l (1 - \cos\theta_0)}$$ Notice that cord tension does zero work because it is always perpendicular to velocity ($T \perp d\mathbf{r}$), making energy conservation exact.

Derivation & Application ID: MECH-Q23

State and derive the Linear Impulse-Momentum Principle from Newton's second law. Explain impulsive forces and derive the dynamic force exerted by a high-velocity fluid jet striking a curved vane. State and derive the Linear Impulse-Momentum Principle from Newton's second law. Explain impulsive forces and derive the dynamic force exerted by a high-velocity fluid jet striking a curved vane. State and derive the Linear Impulse-Momentum Principle from Newton's second law. Explain impulsive forces and derive the dynamic force exerted by a high-velocity fluid jet striking a curved vane.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Impulse-Momentum Principle provides a direct formulation of Newton's second law integrated with respect to time rather than displacement. It relates applied forces and time intervals directly to changes in linear momentum.

Linear Momentum and Impulse Definitions: 1. Linear Momentum ($\mathbf{p}$): The linear momentum of a particle of mass $m$ moving with velocity vector $\mathbf{v}$ is defined as: $$\mathbf{p} = m \mathbf{v}$$ Linear momentum is a vector quantity having the same direction as the instantaneous velocity, with SI units of $\text{kg}\cdot\text{m/s}$ (or $\text{N}\cdot\text{s}$). 2. Linear Impulse ($\mathbf{J}$): The linear impulse of a force $\mathbf{F}$ acting over a time interval from $t_1$ to $t_2$ is defined as the time integral of the force vector: $$\mathbf{J} = \int_{t_1}^{t_2} \mathbf{F} \, dt$$ Impulse is a vector quantity having the direction of the force. Graphically, the magnitude of impulse equals the area under the force-time curve ($F-t$ curve) between $t_1$ and $t_2$. If the force $\mathbf{F}$ is constant in magnitude and direction: $$\mathbf{J} = \mathbf{F} (t_2 - t_1) = \mathbf{F} \Delta t$$ 3. Impulsive Forces: An impulsive force is a very large force that acts over an extremely short duration $\Delta t$ (such as an impact blow, hammer strike, explosion, or projectile collision). Because the time interval is vanishingly small, non-impulsive forces (such as gravity or light spring forces) produce negligible impulse during the impact interval and are neglected in momentum analysis.

Mathematical Derivation of the Impulse-Momentum Principle: Consider a particle of constant mass $m$ acted upon by a resultant force $\mathbf{F}$. Newton's second law states: $$\mathbf{F} = m \mathbf{a} = m \frac{d\mathbf{v}}{dt} = \frac{d(m\mathbf{v})}{dt} = \frac{d\mathbf{p}}{dt}$$ Multiplying both sides by differential time $dt$: $$\mathbf{F} \, dt = d(m\mathbf{v})$$ Integrating both sides over the time interval from $t_1$ (when velocity is $\mathbf{v}_1$) to $t_2$ (when velocity is $\mathbf{v}_2$): $$\int_{t_1}^{t_2} \mathbf{F} \, dt = \int_{\mathbf{v}_1}^{\mathbf{v}_2} d(m\mathbf{v}) = m \mathbf{v}_2 - m \mathbf{v}_1$$ Substituting the definition of impulse $\mathbf{J}$: $$\mathbf{J}_{1-2} = m \mathbf{v}_2 - m \mathbf{v}_1 = \mathbf{p}_2 - \mathbf{p}_1 = \Delta \mathbf{p}$$ Statement of the Impulse-Momentum Principle: The total linear impulse exerted on a particle by the resultant force over a given time interval is equal to the change in the linear momentum of the particle over that same time interval. In scalar Cartesian components: $$\int_{t_1}^{t_2} F_x \, dt = m v_{2x} - m v_{1x}$$ $$\int_{t_1}^{t_2} F_y \, dt = m v_{2y} - m v_{1y}$$

Engineering Application: Hydrodynamic Force Exerted by a Fluid Jet on a Fixed Vane: Consider a continuous high-speed liquid jet (e.g., water in a Pelton turbine) of fluid density $\rho$, cross-sectional area $A$, and velocity $v$ discharging horizontally. The jet strikes a stationary solid curved vane that deflects the jet through an angle $\beta$ without altering its speed ($v_{exit} = v$, assuming smooth frictionless flow). 1. Mass Flow Rate: The mass of water striking the vane per second is: $$\dot{m} = \rho A v = \rho Q$$ where $Q = A v$ is the volumetric discharge rate ($\text{m}^3\text{/s}$). 2. Applying Impulse-Momentum Equation to Steady Fluid Flow: For a continuous flow stream, the impulse-momentum principle translates into the momentum rate equation: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F} = \dot{m} (\mathbf{v}_{out} - \mathbf{v}_{in})$$ where $\Sigma \mathbf{F}$ is the net force exerted by the vane on the fluid stream. 3. Resolving Velocity Components: - Initial velocity vector entering horizontally along $x$: $$\mathbf{v}_{in} = v \hat{\mathbf{i}} + 0 \hat{\mathbf{j}}$$ - Final velocity vector exiting the curved vane at angle $\beta$ to the horizontal: $$\mathbf{v}_{out} = (v \cos\beta)\hat{\mathbf{i}} + (v \sin\beta)\hat{\mathbf{j}}$$ 4. Force Exerted on the Fluid Stream: - Horizontal force on fluid: $$F_x = \dot{m}(v_{out, x} - v_{in, x}) = \dot{m}(v \cos\beta - v) = -\dot{m} v (1 - \cos\beta)$$ - Vertical force on fluid: $$F_y = \dot{m}(v_{out, y} - v_{in, y}) = \dot{m}(v \sin\beta - 0) = \dot{m} v \sin\beta$$ 5. Dynamic Force Exerted by the Fluid Jet on the Vane: By Newton's third law of action and reaction, the force $\mathbf{R}_{jet}$ exerted by the jet on the vane is equal and opposite to the force exerted by the vane on the fluid: $$R_{jet, x} = -F_x = \dot{m} v (1 - \cos\beta) = \rho A v^2 (1 - \cos\beta)$$ $$R_{jet, y} = -F_y = -\dot{m} v \sin\beta = -\rho A v^2 \sin\beta$$ Special Case 1: Flat Plate Perpendicular to Jet ($\beta = 90^\circ, \cos 90^\circ = 0$): $$R_x = \rho A v^2$$ Special Case 2: Semicircular Curved Vane Deflecting Jet by $180^\circ$ ($\beta = 180^\circ, \cos 180^\circ = -1$): $$R_x = \rho A v^2 [1 - (-1)] = 2 \rho A v^2$$ The horizontal force on a $180^\circ$ reversing cup vane is twice that on a flat plate, which is why Pelton turbine buckets are split into double-hemispherical cups.

Graphically, the magnitude of impulse equals the area under the force-time curve ($F-t$ curve) between $t_1$ and $t_2$. Derivation & Application ID: MECH-Q24

State and prove the Principle of Conservation of Linear Momentum for a system of interacting particles. Apply the principle to determine the recoil velocity of an artillery gun firing a shell. State and prove the Principle of Conservation of Linear Momentum for a system of interacting particles. Apply the principle to determine the recoil velocity of an artillery gun firing a shell. State and prove the Principle of Conservation of Linear Momentum for a system of interacting particles. Apply the principle to determine the recoil velocity of an artillery gun firing a shell.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = \frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt} = 0 \implies \mathbf{P} = \sum m_i \mathbf{v}_i = \text{constant}, \quad V_R = -\left(\frac{m}{M}\right) v_b$$

Linear Momentum Conservation: Internal forces cancel in pairs ($\Sigma \mathbf{f}_{ij} = 0$). If $\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = 0$, total momentum is conserved.
Recoil Velocity: Firing projectile with muzzle speed $v_b$ imparts cannon recoil $V_R = -(m/M)v_b$.
Generality: Strictly conserved regardless of whether mechanical energy is lost.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = \frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt} = 0 \implies \mathbf{P} = \sum m_i \mathbf{v}_i = \text{constant}, \quad V_R = -\left(\frac{m}{M}\right) v_b$$

Linear Momentum Conservation: Internal forces cancel in pairs ($\Sigma \mathbf{f}_{ij} = 0$). If $\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = 0$, total momentum is conserved.
Recoil Velocity: Firing projectile with muzzle speed $v_b$ imparts cannon recoil $V_R = -(m/M)v_b$.
Generality: Strictly conserved regardless of whether mechanical energy is lost.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = \frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt} = 0 \implies \mathbf{P} = \sum m_i \mathbf{v}_i = \text{constant}, \quad V_R = -\left(\frac{m}{M}\right) v_b$$

Linear Momentum Conservation: Internal forces cancel in pairs ($\Sigma \mathbf{f}_{ij} = 0$). If $\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = 0$, total momentum is conserved.
Recoil Velocity: Firing projectile with muzzle speed $v_b$ imparts cannon recoil $V_R = -(m/M)v_b$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Principle of Conservation of Linear Momentum is one of the most fundamental conservation laws in physics and engineering. It applies universally to all mechanical interactions, collisions, and propulsion systems, regardless of whether mechanical kinetic energy is conserved or whether the internal forces are linear or non-linear.

Derivation for a System of Interacting Particles: Consider an arbitrary system composed of $n$ interacting particles of masses $m_1, m_2, \dots, m_n$ moving with velocities $\mathbf{v}_1, \mathbf{v}_2, \dots, \mathbf{v}_n$. The total linear momentum $\mathbf{P}$ of the system is the vector sum of the linear momentums of all constituent particles: $$\mathbf{P} = \sum_{i=1}^n m_i \mathbf{v}_i = m_1 \mathbf{v}_1 + m_2 \mathbf{v}_2 + \dots + m_n \mathbf{v}_n$$ The forces acting on any $i$-th particle are divided into two categories: 1. Internal Forces ($\mathbf{f}_{ij}$): Forces exerted on particle $i$ by particle $j$ inside the system (e.g., intermolecular forces, structural tension, mutual contact forces). 2. External Forces ($\mathbf{F}_i^{ext}$): Forces exerted on particle $i$ by bodies outside the system boundary (e.g., gravity, external applied loads).

Applying Newton's second law to the $i$-th particle: $$\mathbf{F}_i^{ext} + \sum_{j \neq i} \mathbf{f}_{ij} = \frac{d}{dt}(m_i \mathbf{v}_i)$$ Summing this equation over all $n$ particles in the system: $$\sum_{i=1}^n \mathbf{F}_i^{ext} + \sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j \neq i} \mathbf{f}_{ij} = \frac{d}{dt}\left( \sum_{i=1}^n m_i \mathbf{v}_i \right) = \frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt}$$ According to Newton's third law of motion, for every action force $\mathbf{f}_{ij}$ exerted by particle $j$ on particle $i$, particle $i$ exerts an equal and opposite reaction force $\mathbf{f}_{ji}$ on particle $j$: $$\mathbf{f}_{ij} = -\mathbf{f}_{ji} \implies \mathbf{f}_{ij} + \mathbf{f}_{ji} = 0$$ Consequently, when summed over the entire closed system, all internal action-reaction force pairs cancel identically: $$\sum_{i=1}^n \sum_{j \neq i} \mathbf{f}_{ij} = 0$$ The equation simplifies to: $$\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = \frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt}$$ This proves that the time rate of change of the total linear momentum of a system of particles depends solely on the resultant external force acting on the system.

Statement of the Principle of Conservation of Linear Momentum: If the resultant external force acting on a system of particles is identically zero ($\Sigma \mathbf{F}^{ext} = 0$): $$\frac{d\mathbf{P}}{dt} = 0 \implies \mathbf{P} = \sum_{i=1}^n m_i \mathbf{v}_i = \text{constant}$$ The total linear momentum of an isolated system remains constant in both magnitude and direction over time. For two interacting bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ colliding or separating: $$m_1 \mathbf{u}_1 + m_2 \mathbf{u}_2 = m_1 \mathbf{v}_1 + m_2 \mathbf{v}_2$$ where $\mathbf{u}_1, \mathbf{u}_2$ are velocities before interaction and $\mathbf{v}_1, \mathbf{v}_2$ are velocities after interaction.

Application: Recoil Velocity of an Artillery Gun: Problem Statement: A cannon of mass $M$ mounted on a frictionless horizontal track fires a shell of mass $m$ with a horizontal muzzle velocity $v_b$ relative to the barrel. Determine the recoil velocity $V_R$ of the cannon.

Step-by-Step Analytical Solution: 1. Identify the System: Define the system as the cannon plus the shell: total mass $= M + m$. 2. Analyze External Forces: In the horizontal direction, there are no external applied forces (the horizontal track is frictionless, $\Sigma F_x^{ext} = 0$). The explosion of gunpowder generates explosive expanding gas pressures that are purely internal to the cannon-shell system. Therefore, total linear momentum in the horizontal direction is strictly conserved: $$P_{initial, x} = P_{final, x}$$ 3. Initial State (Before Firing): Both the cannon and the shell are at rest: $$P_{initial, x} = 0$$ 4. Final State (After Firing): Let the shell be fired with velocity $v_b$ in the positive $x$-direction ($+\hat{\mathbf{i}}$). Let $V_R$ be the recoil velocity of the cannon in the negative $x$-direction ($-\hat{\mathbf{i}}$). The total final momentum is: $$P_{final, x} = m v_b + M V_R$$ 5. Applying Conservation of Momentum: $$0 = m v_b + M V_R \implies M V_R = -m v_b$$ Solving for the recoil velocity: $$V_R = -\left(\frac{m}{M}\right) v_b$$ The negative sign confirms that the cannon recoils in the opposite direction to the trajectory of the fired projectile. Because the mass of the cannon is much larger than the mass of the shell ($M \gg m$), the ratio $\frac{m}{M}$ is small, ensuring that the recoil velocity $V_R$ remains manageable and can be absorbed by hydraulic recoil brake buffers.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$e = \frac{v_2 - v_1}{u_1 - u_2}, \quad v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2)u_1 + m_2(1+e)u_2}{m_1 + m_2}, \quad v_2 = \frac{m_1(1+e)u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1)u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

Direct Central Impact: Centers of mass and velocities lie along the normal line of impact.
Restitution Coefficient ($e$): $e = \frac{\text{Separation Velocity}}{\text{Approach Velocity}} = \frac{v_2 - v_1}{u_1 - u_2}$.
Special Cases: Elastic ($e = 1$) $\implies$ equal masses exchange velocities; Plastic ($e = 0$) $\implies$ bodies coalesce with common velocity.
