Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Impact is defined as a collision between two bodies occurring over a very short duration of time, during which comparatively large impulsive contact forces are generated. In engineering dynamics, an impact is classified as: 1. Direct Impact: The velocities of both colliding bodies are directed along the common normal to the contacting surfaces (the line of impact). 2. Central Impact: The centers of mass ($CG$) of both colliding bodies lie on the line of impact. A Direct Central Impact occurs when both bodies move collinear along the line connecting their centers of mass before and after impact.

Physical Phases of Collision and Coefficient of Restitution ($e$): When two elastic bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ collide with initial approach velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ (with $u_1 > u_2$ along the common line of impact): 1. Period of Deformation: Contact begins; the bodies deform elastically and plastically. The impulsive deformation force compresses both bodies until they reach a common intermediate velocity $v_c$ at the instant of maximum deformation. Let $\int P \, dt$ be the deformation impulse. 2. Period of Restitution: The internal elastic strain energy stored during deformation attempts to restore the original shapes, pushing the bodies apart until they separate with final velocities $v_1$ and $v_2$. Let $\int R \, dt$ be the restitution impulse.

Newton's Experimental Law of Collision and Definition of $e$: Formulated empirically by Sir Isaac Newton, the Coefficient of Restitution $e$ is defined as the ratio of the magnitude of the restitution impulse to the deformation impulse: $$e = \frac{\int R \, dt}{\int P \, dt}$$ Newton demonstrated that this impulse ratio is identically equal to the ratio of the relative velocity of separation to the relative velocity of approach along the line of impact: $$e = \frac{\text{Relative Velocity of Separation}}{\text{Relative Velocity of Approach}} = \frac{v_2 - v_1}{u_1 - u_2}$$ where $0 \le e \le 1$. - Perfectly Elastic Collision ($e = 1$): Complete restitution, kinetic energy is conserved. - Inelastic Collision ($0 < e < 1$): Partial restitution, some kinetic energy is dissipated as heat and sound. - Perfectly Plastic / Inelastic Collision ($e = 0$): Zero restitution; colliding bodies stick together and move with a single common velocity ($v_1 = v_2$).

Mathematical Derivation of Post-Collision Velocities ($v_1$ and $v_2$): Consider two bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a straight horizontal line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ ($u_1 > u_2$). Let their post-collision velocities be $v_1$ and $v_2$. Because the impulsive contact forces during impact are internal to the two-body system, total linear momentum along the line of impact is strictly conserved: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 v_2 \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ From the definition of the coefficient of restitution: $$v_2 - v_1 = e (u_1 - u_2) \implies v_2 = v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

1. Derivation of Velocity of Body 1 ($v_1$): Substituting Equation 2 ($v_2 = v_1 + e(u_1 - u_2)$) directly into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 \left[ v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \right]$$ Expanding terms: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_1 + m_2 e u_1 - m_2 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_1$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = m_1 u_1 - m_2 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 + m_2 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

2. Derivation of Velocity of Body 2 ($v_2$): From Equation 2, $v_1 = v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2)$. Substituting this into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 \left[ v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2) \right] + m_2 v_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_2 - m_1 e u_1 + m_1 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 u_1 + m_1 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 - m_1 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_2 = \frac{m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

Special Physical Cases: 1. Identical Masses in Perfectly Elastic Collision ($m_1 = m_2 = m$, $e = 1$): $$v_1 = \frac{(m - m)u_1 + m(2)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_2}{2m} = u_2$$ $$v_2 = \frac{m(2)u_1 + (m - m)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_1}{2m} = u_1$$ The two bodies exchange their velocities completely upon impact (as observed in Newton's Cradle). 2. Perfectly Plastic Collision ($e = 0$): $$v_1 = v_2 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Both bodies coalesce and travel together at the common system center-of-mass velocity.

Mathematical Derivation of Post-Collision Velocities ($v_1$ and $v_2$): Consider two bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a straight horizontal line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ ($u_1 > u_2$). Let their post-collision velocities be $v_1$ and $v_2$. Because the impulsive contact forces during impact are internal to the two-body system, total linear momentum along the line of impact is strictly conserved: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 v_2 \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ From the definition of the coefficient of restitution: $$v_2 - v_1 = e (u_1 - u_2) \implies v_2 = v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

1. Derivation of Velocity of Body 1 ($v_1$): Substituting Equation 2 ($v_2 = v_1 + e(u_1 - u_2)$) directly into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 \left[ v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \right]$$ Expanding terms: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_1 + m_2 e u_1 - m_2 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_1$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = m_1 u_1 - m_2 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 + m_2 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

2. Derivation of Velocity of Body 2 ($v_2$): From Equation 2, $v_1 = v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2)$. Substituting this into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 \left[ v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2) \right] + m_2 v_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_2 - m_1 e u_1 + m_1 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 u_1 + m_1 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 - m_1 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_2 = \frac{m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

Special Physical Cases: 1. Identical Masses in Perfectly Elastic Collision ($m_1 = m_2 = m$, $e = 1$): $$v_1 = \frac{(m - m)u_1 + m(2)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_2}{2m} = u_2$$ $$v_2 = \frac{m(2)u_1 + (m - m)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_1}{2m} = u_1$$ The two bodies exchange their velocities completely upon impact (as observed in Newton's Cradle). 2. Perfectly Plastic Collision ($e = 0$): $$v_1 = v_2 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Both bodies coalesce and travel together at the common system center-of-mass velocity.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Impact is defined as a collision between two bodies occurring over a very short duration of time, during which comparatively large impulsive contact forces are generated. In engineering dynamics, an impact is classified as: 1. Direct Impact: The velocities of both colliding bodies are directed along the common normal to the contacting surfaces (the line of impact). 2. Central Impact: The centers of mass ($CG$) of both colliding bodies lie on the line of impact. A Direct Central Impact occurs when both bodies move collinear along the line connecting their centers of mass before and after impact.

Physical Phases of Collision and Coefficient of Restitution ($e$): When two elastic bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ collide with initial approach velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ (with $u_1 > u_2$ along the common line of impact): 1. Period of Deformation: Contact begins; the bodies deform elastically and plastically. The impulsive deformation force compresses both bodies until they reach a common intermediate velocity $v_c$ at the instant of maximum deformation. Let $\int P \, dt$ be the deformation impulse. 2. Period of Restitution: The internal elastic strain energy stored during deformation attempts to restore the original shapes, pushing the bodies apart until they separate with final velocities $v_1$ and $v_2$. Let $\int R \, dt$ be the restitution impulse.

Newton's Experimental Law of Collision and Definition of $e$: Formulated empirically by Sir Isaac Newton, the Coefficient of Restitution $e$ is defined as the ratio of the magnitude of the restitution impulse to the deformation impulse: $$e = \frac{\int R \, dt}{\int P \, dt}$$ Newton demonstrated that this impulse ratio is identically equal to the ratio of the relative velocity of separation to the relative velocity of approach along the line of impact: $$e = \frac{\text{Relative Velocity of Separation}}{\text{Relative Velocity of Approach}} = \frac{v_2 - v_1}{u_1 - u_2}$$ where $0 \le e \le 1$. - Perfectly Elastic Collision ($e = 1$): Complete restitution, kinetic energy is conserved. - Inelastic Collision ($0 < e < 1$): Partial restitution, some kinetic energy is dissipated as heat and sound. - Perfectly Plastic / Inelastic Collision ($e = 0$): Zero restitution; colliding bodies stick together and move with a single common velocity ($v_1 = v_2$).

Mathematical Derivation of Post-Collision Velocities ($v_1$ and $v_2$): Consider two bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a straight horizontal line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ ($u_1 > u_2$). Let their post-collision velocities be $v_1$ and $v_2$. Because the impulsive contact forces during impact are internal to the two-body system, total linear momentum along the line of impact is strictly conserved: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 v_2 \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$ From the definition of the coefficient of restitution: $$v_2 - v_1 = e (u_1 - u_2) \implies v_2 = v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$

1. Derivation of Velocity of Body 1 ($v_1$): Substituting Equation 2 ($v_2 = v_1 + e(u_1 - u_2)$) directly into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 \left[ v_1 + e (u_1 - u_2) \right]$$ Expanding terms: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_1 + m_2 e u_1 - m_2 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_1$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = m_1 u_1 - m_2 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 + m_2 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_1 = (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

2. Derivation of Velocity of Body 2 ($v_2$): From Equation 2, $v_1 = v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2)$. Substituting this into Equation 1: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 \left[ v_2 - e (u_1 - u_2) \right] + m_2 v_2 = (m_1 + m_2) v_2 - m_1 e u_1 + m_1 e u_2$$ Rearranging to isolate $(m_1 + m_2) v_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 u_1 + m_1 e u_1 + m_2 u_2 - m_1 e u_2$$ Factoring $u_1$ and $u_2$: $$(m_1 + m_2) v_2 = m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2$$ Dividing by $(m_1 + m_2)$ yields: $$v_2 = \frac{m_1 (1 + e) u_1 + (m_2 - e m_1) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$

Special Physical Cases: 1. Identical Masses in Perfectly Elastic Collision ($m_1 = m_2 = m$, $e = 1$): $$v_1 = \frac{(m - m)u_1 + m(2)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_2}{2m} = u_2$$ $$v_2 = \frac{m(2)u_1 + (m - m)u_2}{2m} = \frac{2m u_1}{2m} = u_1$$ The two bodies exchange their velocities completely upon impact (as observed in Newton's Cradle). 2. Perfectly Plastic Collision ($e = 0$): $$v_1 = v_2 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Both bodies coalesce and travel together at the common system center-of-mass velocity.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.5: Contact Phases During Direct Central Collision: Approach, Maximum Deformation, and Restitution — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)

Derivation ID: MECH-Q26

Derive the complete mathematical expression for the loss of kinetic energy during direct central inelastic impact of two bodies: Delta E = (m1*m2)/(2*(m1+m2)) * (1 - e^2) * (u1 - u2)^2. Discuss perfectly elastic, inelastic, and plastic collisions. Discuss perfectly elastic, inelastic, and plastic collisions. Derive the complete mathematical expression for the loss of kinetic energy during direct central inelastic impact of two bodies: Delta E = (m1*m2)/(2*(m1+m2)) * (1 - e^2) * (u1 - u2)^2. Discuss perfectly elastic, inelastic, and plastic collisions.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2 = \frac{1}{2} \mu (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$

Loss of Kinetic Energy: $\Delta E = E_{initial} - E_{final} = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)}(1 - e^2)(u_1 - u_2)^2$.
Reduced Mass: $\mu = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}$.
Regimes:
- Perfectly Elastic ($e = 1$): $\Delta E = 0$ (energy conserved).
- Inelastic ($0 < e < 1$): $\Delta E > 0$ (dissipated as heat/sound).
- Perfectly Plastic ($e = 0$): Maximum energy loss.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

During a real, inelastic direct central collision between two bodies, while the total linear momentum is strictly conserved ($\Sigma \mathbf{P} = \text{constant}$), the total mechanical kinetic energy is not conserved. A portion of the initial kinetic energy is converted irreversibly into acoustic wave energy (sound), thermal energy (heat), and internal plastic strain energy during permanent deformation.

System Setup and Initial Energy Formulation: Consider two smooth bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a common straight line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ before impact. Let $e$ be the coefficient of restitution, and let $v_1$ and $v_2$ be their post-collision velocities. The total initial kinetic energy of the system before impact is: $$E_1 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2$$ The total final kinetic energy of the system after impact is: $$E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2$$ The net loss of kinetic energy during the collision is defined as: $$\Delta E = E_1 - E_2 = \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2\right) - \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2\right) \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

Step-by-Step Algebraic Derivation: From the fundamental governing equations of direct central impact: Conservation of linear momentum: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 v_2 \implies m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = m_2 (v_2 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Newton's law of restitution: $$v_2 - v_1 = e (u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 3)}$$

Multiplying Equation 1 by 2: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) + m_2 (u_2^2 - v_2^2) = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) - m_2 (v_2^2 - u_2^2)$$ Factoring the differences of squares: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)(u_1 + v_1) - m_2 (v_2 - u_2)(v_2 + u_2)$$ Using Equation 2, let $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = m_2 (v_2 - u_2)$. Substituting $K$: $$2 \Delta E = K (u_1 + v_1) - K (v_2 + u_2) = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - (v_2 - v_1) \right]$$ Substituting Equation 3 ($v_2 - v_1 = e(u_1 - u_2)$) into the bracket: $$2 \Delta E = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - e(u_1 - u_2) \right] = K (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 4)}$$

Now we must evaluate the factor $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)$ in terms of initial velocities and mass parameters. From the derived formula for post-collision velocity $v_1$: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Subtracting $v_1$ from $u_1$: $$u_1 - v_1 = u_1 - \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{(m_1 + m_2) u_1 - (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_1 - m_1 u_1 + e m_2 u_1 - m_2 u_2 - e m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{m_2 (1 + e) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Multiplying by mass $m_1$ to obtain $K$: $$K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \quad \text{--- (Equation 5)}$$

Substituting Equation 5 into Equation 4: $$2 \Delta E = \left[ \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \right] (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2)$$ $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 + e)(1 - e) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Since $(1 + e)(1 - e) = 1 - e^2$: $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Dividing by 2 gives the celebrated expression for the Loss of Kinetic Energy: $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Defining the Reduced Mass of the two-body system as $\mu = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}$: $$\Delta E = \frac{1}{2} \mu (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$

Analysis of Physical Regimes: 1. Perfectly Elastic Impact ($e = 1$): $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - 1^2)(u_1 - u_2)^2 = 0$$ The loss of kinetic energy is zero ($\Delta E = 0$). Kinetic energy is strictly conserved. 2. Inelastic Impact ($0 < e < 1$): Since $0 < e < 1$, $(1 - e^2) > 0$. Therefore $\Delta E > 0$, confirming that kinetic energy is always lost during real physical collisions. 3. Perfectly Plastic / Inelastic Impact ($e = 0$): $$\Delta E_{max} = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ The loss of kinetic energy is maximized when $e = 0$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

During a real, inelastic direct central collision between two bodies, while the total linear momentum is strictly conserved ($\Sigma \mathbf{P} = \text{constant}$), the total mechanical kinetic energy is not conserved. A portion of the initial kinetic energy is converted irreversibly into acoustic wave energy (sound), thermal energy (heat), and internal plastic strain energy during permanent deformation.

System Setup and Initial Energy Formulation: Consider two smooth bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a common straight line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ before impact. Let $e$ be the coefficient of restitution, and let $v_1$ and $v_2$ be their post-collision velocities. The total initial kinetic energy of the system before impact is: $$E_1 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2$$ The total final kinetic energy of the system after impact is: $$E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2$$ The net loss of kinetic energy during the collision is defined as: $$\Delta E = E_1 - E_2 = \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2\right) - \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2\right) \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

Multiplying Equation 1 by 2: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) + m_2 (u_2^2 - v_2^2) = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) - m_2 (v_2^2 - u_2^2)$$ Factoring the differences of squares: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)(u_1 + v_1) - m_2 (v_2 - u_2)(v_2 + u_2)$$ Using Equation 2, let $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = m_2 (v_2 - u_2)$. Substituting $K$: $$2 \Delta E = K (u_1 + v_1) - K (v_2 + u_2) = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - (v_2 - v_1) \right]$$ Substituting Equation 3 ($v_2 - v_1 = e(u_1 - u_2)$) into the bracket: $$2 \Delta E = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - e(u_1 - u_2) \right] = K (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 4)}$$

Now we must evaluate the factor $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)$ in terms of initial velocities and mass parameters. From the derived formula for post-collision velocity $v_1$: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Subtracting $v_1$ from $u_1$: $$u_1 - v_1 = u_1 - \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{(m_1 + m_2) u_1 - (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_1 - m_1 u_1 + e m_2 u_1 - m_2 u_2 - e m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{m_2 (1 + e) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Multiplying by mass $m_1$ to obtain $K$: $$K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \quad \text{--- (Equation 5)}$$

Substituting Equation 5 into Equation 4: $$2 \Delta E = \left[ \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \right] (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2)$$ $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 + e)(1 - e) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Since $(1 + e)(1 - e) = 1 - e^2$: $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Dividing by 2 gives the celebrated expression for the Loss of Kinetic Energy: $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Defining the Reduced Mass of the two-body system as $\mu = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}$: $$\Delta E = \frac{1}{2} \mu (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$

Analysis of Physical Regimes: 1. Perfectly Elastic Impact ($e = 1$): $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - 1^2)(u_1 - u_2)^2 = 0$$ The loss of kinetic energy is zero ($\Delta E = 0$). Kinetic energy is strictly conserved. 2. Inelastic Impact ($0 < e < 1$): Since $0 < e < 1$, $(1 - e^2) > 0$. Therefore $\Delta E > 0$, confirming that kinetic energy is always lost during real physical collisions. 3. Perfectly Plastic / Inelastic Impact ($e = 0$): $$\Delta E_{max} = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ The loss of kinetic energy is maximized when $e = 0$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

During a real, inelastic direct central collision between two bodies, while the total linear momentum is strictly conserved ($\Sigma \mathbf{P} = \text{constant}$), the total mechanical kinetic energy is not conserved. A portion of the initial kinetic energy is converted irreversibly into acoustic wave energy (sound), thermal energy (heat), and internal plastic strain energy during permanent deformation.

System Setup and Initial Energy Formulation: Consider two smooth bodies of masses $m_1$ and $m_2$ moving along a common straight line with initial velocities $u_1$ and $u_2$ before impact. Let $e$ be the coefficient of restitution, and let $v_1$ and $v_2$ be their post-collision velocities. The total initial kinetic energy of the system before impact is: $$E_1 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2$$ The total final kinetic energy of the system after impact is: $$E_2 = \frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2$$ The net loss of kinetic energy during the collision is defined as: $$\Delta E = E_1 - E_2 = \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 u_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 u_2^2\right) - \left(\frac{1}{2} m_1 v_1^2 + \frac{1}{2} m_2 v_2^2\right) \quad \text{--- (Equation 1)}$$

Step-by-Step Algebraic Derivation: From the fundamental governing equations of direct central impact: Conservation of linear momentum: $$m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_2 = m_1 v_1 + m_2 v_2 \implies m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = m_2 (v_2 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 2)}$$ Newton's law of restitution: $$v_2 - v_1 = e (u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 3)}$$

Multiplying Equation 1 by 2: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) + m_2 (u_2^2 - v_2^2) = m_1 (u_1^2 - v_1^2) - m_2 (v_2^2 - u_2^2)$$ Factoring the differences of squares: $$2 \Delta E = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)(u_1 + v_1) - m_2 (v_2 - u_2)(v_2 + u_2)$$ Using Equation 2, let $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = m_2 (v_2 - u_2)$. Substituting $K$: $$2 \Delta E = K (u_1 + v_1) - K (v_2 + u_2) = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - (v_2 - v_1) \right]$$ Substituting Equation 3 ($v_2 - v_1 = e(u_1 - u_2)$) into the bracket: $$2 \Delta E = K \left[ (u_1 - u_2) - e(u_1 - u_2) \right] = K (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2) \quad \text{--- (Equation 4)}$$

Now we must evaluate the factor $K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1)$ in terms of initial velocities and mass parameters. From the derived formula for post-collision velocity $v_1$: $$v_1 = \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Subtracting $v_1$ from $u_1$: $$u_1 - v_1 = u_1 - \frac{(m_1 - e m_2) u_1 + m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{(m_1 + m_2) u_1 - (m_1 - e m_2) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_1 u_1 + m_2 u_1 - m_1 u_1 + e m_2 u_1 - m_2 u_2 - e m_2 u_2}{m_1 + m_2} = \frac{m_2 (1 + e) u_1 - m_2 (1 + e) u_2}{m_1 + m_2}$$ $$u_1 - v_1 = \frac{m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2}$$ Multiplying by mass $m_1$ to obtain $K$: $$K = m_1 (u_1 - v_1) = \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \quad \text{--- (Equation 5)}$$

Substituting Equation 5 into Equation 4: $$2 \Delta E = \left[ \frac{m_1 m_2 (1 + e)(u_1 - u_2)}{m_1 + m_2} \right] (1 - e)(u_1 - u_2)$$ $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 + e)(1 - e) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Since $(1 + e)(1 - e) = 1 - e^2$: $$2 \Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Dividing by 2 gives the celebrated expression for the Loss of Kinetic Energy: $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ Defining the Reduced Mass of the two-body system as $\mu = \frac{m_1 m_2}{m_1 + m_2}$: $$\Delta E = \frac{1}{2} \mu (1 - e^2) (u_1 - u_2)^2$$

Analysis of Physical Regimes: 1. Perfectly Elastic Impact ($e = 1$): $$\Delta E = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (1 - 1^2)(u_1 - u_2)^2 = 0$$ The loss of kinetic energy is zero ($\Delta E = 0$). Kinetic energy is strictly conserved. 2. Inelastic Impact ($0 < e < 1$): Since $0 < e < 1$, $(1 - e^2) > 0$. Therefore $\Delta E > 0$, confirming that kinetic energy is always lost during real physical collisions. 3. Perfectly Plastic / Inelastic Impact ($e = 0$): $$\Delta E_{max} = \frac{m_1 m_2}{2(m_1 + m_2)} (u_1 - u_2)^2$$ The loss of kinetic energy is maximized when $e = 0$.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.6: Initial vs Final Kinetic Energies and Energy Dissipation Curve as Function of e — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
